Flagged LLT polynomials, nonsymmetric plethysm, and nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials Thanks: Authors were supported by NSF Grants DMS-2154282 and 2452208 (J. B.), DMS-2154281 and 2452209 (J. M.), DMS-2303175 (G. S.) and DMS-1929284 (all authors in residence at MathematicsCollaborate@ICERM)
Jonah Blasiak, Mark Haiman, Jennifer Morse, Anna Pun, George H. Seelinger

Abstract
. The plethystic transformation f ⁡ [ X ] ↦ f ⁡ [ X / ( 1 − t ) ] f[X]\mapsto f[X/(1-t)] and LLT polynomials are central to the theory of symmetric Macdonald polynomials. In this work, we introduce and study nonsymmetric flagged LLT polynomials . We show that these admit both an algebraic and a combinatorial description, that they Weyl symmetrize to the usual symmetric LLT polynomials, and we conjecture that they expand positively in terms of Demazure atoms. Additionally, we construct a nonsymmetric plethysm operator Π t , x \Pi_{t,x} on 𝔎 ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x n ] \mathfrak{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}] , which serves as an analogue of f ⁡ [ X ] ↦ f ⁡ [ X / ( 1 − t ) ] f[X]\mapsto f[X/(1-t)] . We prove that Π t , x \Pi_{t,x} remarkably maps flagged LLT polynomials defined over a signed alphabet to ones over an unsigned alphabet. Our main application of this theory is to formulate a nonsymmetric version of Macdonald positivity, similar in spirit to conjectures of Knop and Lapointe, but with several new features. To do this, we recast the Haglund-Haiman-Loehr formula for nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials ℰ μ ​ ( x , q , t ) \mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x;q,t) as a positive sum of signed flagged LLT polynomials. Then, after taking a suitable stable limit of both ℰ μ ​ ( x , q , t ) \mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x;q,t) and Π t , x \Pi_{t,x} , we obtain modified nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials which are positive sums of flagged LLT polynomials and thus are conjecturally atom positive, strengthening the Macdonald positivity conjecture.



1.
Introduction

In the 1980s Macdonald
[
37
]
introduced a family of
orthogonal polynomials
P_{\mu}(X;q,t)
in the algebra of symmetric
functions
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)}(X)
and conjectured that carefully
chosen scalar multiples
J_{\mu}=c_{\mu}P_{\mu}
satisfy a remarkable
positivity property. In terms of the plethystically modified

H_{\mu}(X;q,t)=J_{\mu}[X/(1-t);q,t]
, Macdonald’s conjecture becomes
Schur positivity of
H_{\mu}
. Subsequent work revealed that this
modified setting has rich combinatorial and representation theoretic
layers.

At the outset, Garsia and Haiman

[
14
]
constructed an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-submodule of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}]
and conjectured that its graded Frobenius
series is
t^{n(\mu)}H_{\mu}(X;q,t^{-1})
. This was proven in

[
24
]
, using the geometry of Hilbert schemes, and settled
the positivity conjecture for
H_{\mu}
. Meanwhile, the agenda
to establish Schur positivity spawned many new directions in the
theory of symmetric functions, including representation theoretic and
combinatorial studies of the
\nabla
operator, the elliptic Hall
algebra, and
k
-Schur functions.

Each of these new directions is shaped by the central role played by
LLT polynomials
[
32
]
,

t
-analogs of products of skew Schur functions closely tied to
Kazhdan-Lusztig theory. They have appeared in numerous results and
conjectures in Macdonald theory including
[
1
,
7
,
10
,
22
,
23
,
25
,
35
,
43
]
,
where they are the
building blocks of combinatorial formulas for
H_{\mu}
,
\nabla e_{n}
,

\nabla s_{\lambda}
, and other symmetric functions in the same vein.
Haglund-Haiman-Loehr
[
20
]

gave a formula expressing
H_{\mu}

as a positive sum of LLT polynomials, which, combined with the Schur
positivity of LLT polynomials
[
19
]
, gives a Hilbert
scheme-free proof of Macdonald positivity.

Concurrent to these symmetric function activities,
a separate theory was being developed by Opdam
[
44
]
,
Macdonald
[
39
]
, and Cherednik
[
11
]

around the nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
E_{\alpha}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t)

(\alpha\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n})
.
The nonsymmetric setting brought in new tools from representation theory and topology and took on a life of its own.
It also offered new insights into the symmetric setting.
For instance,
E_{\alpha}
can be computed via a recurrence relation
[
26
,
47
]
which has no analog in the symmetric world,
and then the
P_{\mu}
can be recovered by Hecke symmetrizing the
E_{\alpha}
.

The web of research inspired by the study of
H_{\mu}
and its Schur
positivity, together with the advantages offered by the nonsymmetric
setting, highlights the question:
is there a
nonsymmetric analog of
H_{\mu}
that supports a framework equally
rich in combinatorics and representation theory?
The question is
guided by the schematic diagram:

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\mu}(X;q,t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\mu}(X;q,t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(x;q,t)}
?
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\mapsto f[X/(1-t)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hecke\ sym.}

in which the missing corner should be a nonsymmetric counterpart to the
modified Macdonald polynomials with positivity and the potential for
developing surrounding theories similar to those around
H_{\mu}
.

A first step towards this question was taken in 1997 by Knop

[
26
]
, who defined integral forms
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}=c_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}
, analogous to the
J_{\mu}
, whose monomial expansion
coefficients lie in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
; this is significant because, as in the
symmetric case, identifying an appropriate integral form is
necessary to even formulate a positivity conjecture.
Building off this a decade later, Knop
[
27
]
formulated a
positivity conjecture for (what we call) the left stable versions of

{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
, involving Kazhdan-Lusztig theory. Progress stalled
for many years until Lapointe
[
29
]
arrived at a different
positivity conjecture inspired by his earlier joint work
[
8
,
9
]
on
Macdonald superpolynomials. His conjecture involves a left stable
version of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
more general than Knop’s and found
independently by Goodberry
[
16
,
17
]
. In other
related work, Goodberry and Orr
[
18
]
and Bechtloff
Weising and Orr
[
3
]

studied these left
stable versions from a
K
-theoretic viewpoint, and Bechtloff Weising

[
4
]
investigated a right stable version of

{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
.

Here, for the first time, we bring the powerful machinery of LLT
polynomials into the nonsymmetric setting. This approach allows us to
complete diagram (
1
) with (a stable version of)
the previously undiscovered corner, introducing a
nonsymmetric
plethysm map
for the bottom arrow and
modified
r
-nonsymmetric
Macdonald polynomials

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
for the ‘
?
’. We
introduce flagged LLT polynomials which Weyl symmetrize to ordinary
LLT polynomials, conjecture that they are positive in terms of
Demazure atoms, and show that
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
is a positive sum
of flagged LLT polynomials. This yields a positivity conjecture for

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
which strengthens Macdonald positivity. These
contributions are highlighted in our summary of the paper below, which
is divided into three parts to match the layout of the paper.

1.1.
Flagged symmetric functions and nonsymmetric plethysm (§§
3
–
4
)

We develop a theory of nonsymmetric plethysm by first formalizing
ideas related to flagged Schur functions and Demazure characters
implicit in works of Lascoux, Macdonald, Reiner-Shimozono and
others
[
31
,
40
,
46
]
. We identify a subspace of

flagged symmetric functions
,
\mathcal{F\!L}
, of the algebra of
multi-symmetric functions
\Lambda(X_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda(X_{l})
. It is defined as the linear span of flagged versions
of homogeneous symmetric functions, and contains the flagged skew
Schur functions first studied in the 80’s and 90’s by Lascoux–Schützenberger
[
34
]
, Gessel–Viennot

[
15
]
, and Wachs
[
49
]
. Our viewpoint begins with
the observation that
\mathcal{F\!L}
is linearly isomorphic to the
polynomial ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{l}]
; this is simple yet powerful
because it allows us to define operations on the latter using
plethystic manipulations on
\mathcal{F\!L}
.

These foundations laid, we define the
nonsymmetric plethysm

operator
\Pi_{t,(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}=\Pi_{t,x}
on the polynomial
ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
to be the linear map determined by

(2)

\Pi_{t,x}\Big(h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]\,h_{a_{2}}[x_{2}+(1-t)\,x_{1}]\cdots h_{a_{n}}[x_{n}+(1-t)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1})]\Big)\\
=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]\,h_{a_{2}}[x_{1}+x_{2}]\cdots h_{a_{n}}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}].

We give several other descriptions of
\Pi_{t,x}
as well as
formulas for its inverse (§§
4.2
–
4.5
). We prove that the
action of
\Pi_{t,x}
on symmetric polynomials converges for large
n

to the plethystic transformation
f[X]\mapsto f[X/(1-t)]
of
symmetric functions. Even better, we find formulas for the limit when
the input is symmetric in a growing block of variables. Of particular
importance for our applications to nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials,
we show that, restricting to polynomials
symmetric in
x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n}
,
\Pi_{t,x}
converges to the
following linear operator
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
on
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials

{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda(Y)
, given by

(3)

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,h[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y]\bigr)=(\Pi_{t,x_{1},\dots,x_{r}}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{r}))\,h\Big[\frac{(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y)}{1-t}\Big].

See Theorem
4.7.3
and (
326
) in
Lemma
7.5.3
.

1.2.
Flagged LLT polynomials (§
6
)

We define
flagged
LLT polynomials

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
,
which are flagged
symmetric functions that generalize
symmetric LLT polynomials, just as
flagged Schur polynomials
generalize usual symmetric Schur polynomials.
While we give a purely algebraic definition of flagged LLT
polynomials, Theorem
6.4.9
shows that they
also have an explicit combinatorial formula as a weight generating function
over flagged tableaux, generalizing the usual attacking pair tableau
formula for symmetric LLT polynomials.
In full generality, these two descriptions work for any totally ordered

signed
alphabet.

Strikingly, flagged LLT polynomials interact well with nonsymmetric
plethysm: for a suitable choice of signed alphabet,

\Pi_{t,x}
sends a signed flagged LLT polynomial, denoted
{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
, to
the unsigned flagged LLT polynomial
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
with the same indexing data, i.e,

(4)

\displaystyle\Pi_{t,x}({\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma})={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}.

See Proposition
6.7.3
. Additionally, we
propose two positivity conjectures
(see §
6.6
) on the expansion of the

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
into Demazure atoms, which generalize the
known Schur positivity of symmetric LLT polynomials in two directions.

1.3.
Nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials (§
7
)

The main
application of our results above is
to the theory of nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials.

We show that the Haglund-Haiman-Loehr formula
[
21
]
for
nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
can be recast as
a positive sum of signed flagged LLT polynomials

{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
for ribbons: specifically, using notation
defined in §§
7.1
–
7.2
, it reads

(5)

{\mathcal{E}}_{(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})}(x;q,t)=t^{n(\alpha_{+})}\sum_{({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)\in R}\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to14.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.14055pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{
}{
}{
}{
}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 9.84 0 M 0 9.84 L 9.84 9.84 M 0 19.68 L 9.84 19.68 M 0 0 L 0 19.69 M 9.84 0 L 9.84 19.69 M 9.84 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.19257pt}{9.16284pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)}\bigr)\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}^{-}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1})\,.

From this, we
show how limiting versions of this identity
recover and unify various stabilizations of nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomials previously studied by Bechtloff Weising

[
4
]
, Goodberry
[
16
,
17
]
,
Knop
[
27
]
, and Lapointe
[
29
]
.

We introduce
modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)
(
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
, partition

\lambda
) as the
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
-images of right stable nonsymmetric
Macdonald polynomials
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)
, a
rescaling of the version in
[
4
]
. The modified

r
-nonsymmetric polynomials satisfy properties bearing a striking
resemblance to those of
H_{\mu}
which made the modified symmetric
theory so broad. We prove that the modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomials are monomial positive and conjecture that they are in fact
Demazure atom positive. By showing that
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
Weyl
symmetrizes to
H_{\mu}
for
\mu
the partition rearrangement of

(\eta;\lambda)
, this conjecture strengthens the Schur positivity of

H_{\mu}
(see (
339
) and Remark

7.7.4
). Further, applying a stable limit
version of (
4
) to formula (
5
)
yields an expression for
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
as a
positive sum of unsigned flagged LLT polynomials

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
(Theorem
7.5.5
(b)). In fact,
this formulation for
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}
shows that the positivity
conjecture for modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials is implied by our atom positivity conjecture for flagged LLT polynomials.

Our results imply the following commutative diagram (see (
352
) and (
358
)).

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t)\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!H_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{(\eta|\lambda)}(x,Y;q,t)\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\mathsf{H}_{(\eta|\lambda)}(x,Y;q,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\mapsto f[X/(1-t)]}

Hecke sym.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}}

Weyl sym.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we fix notation and terminology and summarize various
known results.

2.1.
Partitions and compositions

A
weak composition
(resp.
strict composition
) of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is a finite sequence of
nonnegative (resp. positive) integers
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k})
such that
|\mu|\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{k}=n
.

A
partition

\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{k})
of
n
is a weakly decreasing strict composition of
n
.
Sometimes we also allow partitions to have trailing zeroes. Either way,
the
length of
\lambda
, denoted
\ell(\lambda)
, means the
number of nonzero parts. If
\mu
is a weak composition,
\mu_{+}

denotes the partition obtained by sorting the nonzero parts of
\mu

into weakly decreasing order (or sometimes, all the parts of
\mu
,
if we are allowing partitions with trailing zeroes). Of use later
will be the number
n(\lambda)
defined by

(7)

n(\lambda)=\sum_{i}(i-1)\lambda_{i}=\sum_{i}\binom{\lambda_{i}^{*}}{2}\,.

Associated to any strict composition
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
of

n
is a partition of the set
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
into intervals
of sizes
r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}
. We refer to these intervals as
{\mathbf{r}}
-blocks
—e.g., for
{\mathbf{r}}=(4,1,2)
, the
{\mathbf{r}}
-blocks are

(8)

[7]=\{1,\ldots,4\}\cup\{5\}\cup\{6,7\}.

2.2.
Symmetric groups

We write
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
for the symmetric group of permutations of

\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
The group
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is a Coxeter group with generators
s_{i}=(i\leftrightarrow i+1)
for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
given by adjacent
transpositions. The
length

\ell(w)
of
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
,
defined as the length of a reduced (i.e., shortest) factorization
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
, is also given by the number of inversions
of the permutation
w
:

(9)

\ell(w)=\inv(w)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}|\{(i<j)\mid w(i)>w(j)\}|.

We write
w_{0}
for the longest permutation
w_{0}(i)=n+1-i
of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

Given a strict composition
{\mathbf{r}}
of
n
,
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\cong{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{r_{k}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}

denotes the Young subgroup consisting of permutations that map every

{\mathbf{r}}
-block onto itself;
it is a Coxeter subgroup, generated by the
s_{i}
for which
i
and
i+1
are
in the same
{\mathbf{r}}
-block.

Given Young subgroups
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
,
every coset of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
contains a unique element of minimal (resp. maximal)
length called a minimal (resp. maximal) coset representative.
We denote the set of minimal coset representatives of the left cosets
\{w\,{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}:w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\}

by
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
.

The Bruhat order on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is the transitive closure of the relations

w<w\,r
for
\ell(w)<\ell(w\,r)
and
r
in the set of reflections

\{s_{ij}=(i\leftrightarrow j):1\leq i<j\leq n\}
.

2.3.
Symmetric functions

A
symmetric series

f(X)
with coefficients in a commutative
ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
is a formal
{\mathfrak{K}}
-linear combination of monomials
in infinitely many variables
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, symmetric in
all the variables (the symbols
X
and
x_{i}
need not be related).
A
symmetric function
is a symmetric series of bounded degree. A

multi-symmetric function
(resp.
series
) is a function

f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
of several (disjoint) sets of variables

X_{i}
which is a symmetric function (resp. series) in each set of
variables.

We use Macdonald’s notation
[
38
]
for the algebra of
symmetric functions
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}
, the elementary, complete,
and power-sum symmetric functions
e_{k}
,
h_{k}
,
p_{k}
,

e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda_{1}}e_{\lambda_{2}}\cdots
,
h_{\lambda}=h_{\lambda_{1}}h_{\lambda_{2}}\cdots
,
p_{\lambda}=p_{\lambda_{1}}p_{\lambda_{2}}\cdots
, monomial symmetric
functions
m_{\lambda}
, Schur functions
s_{\lambda}
, and skew
Schur functions
s_{\lambda/\mu}
. The functions
e_{\lambda}
,

h_{\lambda}
,
m_{\lambda}
, and
s_{\lambda}
are graded free

{\mathfrak{K}}
module bases of
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}
, in which the element
indexed by a partition
\lambda
is homogeneous of degree
|\lambda|
. If
{\mathfrak{K}}
contains
{\mathbb{Q}}
, the
p_{\lambda}
are also a
basis.

We write
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)
,
e_{k}(X)
,
h_{k}(X)
, etc. if we
need to specify the set of variables
X
, and write
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
for the algebra of multi-symmetric functions.
We also write
\Lambda(X)
or
\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
when

{\mathfrak{K}}
is understood. Caution: unlike Macdonald

[
38
]
, by omitting
{\mathfrak{K}}
, we do not mean that
{\mathfrak{K}}={\mathbb{Z}}
.

If
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
, the algebra of symmetric functions is a
polynomial ring

(10)

\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)={\mathfrak{K}}[e_{1}(X),e_{2}(X),\ldots]={\mathfrak{K}}[h_{1}(X),h_{2}(X),\ldots]={\mathfrak{K}}[p_{1}(X),p_{2}(X),\ldots]

in the
e_{k}(X)
,
h_{k}(X)
, or
p_{k}(X)
(the first two hold for
any
{\mathfrak{K}}
). Likewise, the algebra of multi-symmetric functions

\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\cong\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{1})\otimes_{{\mathfrak{K}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathfrak{K}}}\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{l})
is a polynomial ring in the
e_{k}(X_{i})
,
h_{k}(X_{i})
,
or
p_{k}(X_{i})
. The algebra of (multi
-
)symmetric series is
a formal power series ring in the same generators.

From now on, unless otherwise specified,
we always assume that
the coefficients of a (multi
-
)symmetric function or series belong to a
ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
containing
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Given this assumption, we often
regard a (multi
-
)symmetric function or series simply as a polynomial or
power series in symbolic power-sums
p_{k}(X)
in
formal
alphabets

X
of infinitely many possibly unspecified variables. We
can then specialize
f(X)
to a symmetric function or series

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
or
f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
in any finite or
infinite set of variables using plethystic evaluation—see §
2.5
, item (i).

2.4.
Coefficients

In this paper we will encounter numerous multivariate functions

f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{l};q,t,\ldots)
of the following form:
f
is a (multi
-
)symmetric function
or series in sets of variables or formal alphabets
X_{i}
, and a
polynomial, power series, or Laurent polynomial or series in variables

x_{i}
, with coefficients in a field
{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t,\ldots)
of
rational functions of parameters
q,t,\ldots
. This includes
nonsymmetric functions
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t,\ldots)
as the
case with no alphabets
X_{i}
.

In keeping with our blanket hypothesis that coefficient rings should contain
{\mathbb{Q}}
,
we always think of, e.g., polynomials with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
as special
cases of polynomials with coefficients in a larger ring containing
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Note that if a function involving parameters
q,t\in{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
happens
to have coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
, then we could instead take the larger ring to be
{\mathbb{Q}}[q,t]

and treat the parameters as ordinary variables.

A multivariate function
f
as above can be freely regarded as a
function of any subset of the variables and formal alphabets, with
coefficients that are functions of the rest. We denote the operation
of taking a coefficient by angle brackets, e.g.,
\langle x^{{\mathbf{a}}}\rangle\,f
for the coefficient of a monomial
x^{{\mathbf{a}}}=x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{k}^{a_{k}}
, or
\langle m_{\lambda}\rangle\,f
for the coefficient of a monomial symmetric function. Note that
when
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, we have
\langle m_{\lambda}(X)\rangle\,f(X)=\langle x^{\lambda}\rangle\,f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
. When taking coefficients with respect to a basis of
\Lambda(X)
, the basis will either be clear from the context, or specified.

2.5.
Plethystic evaluation

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}
be an algebra whose elements are formal series in a
possibly infinite set of indeterminates with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Assume that
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
and that
{\mathfrak{A}}
is closed under
the operations

(11)

Z\mapsto p_{k}[Z]=Z|_{z\mapsto z^{k}}

of substituting
z^{k}
for every indeterminate
z
.
1
1

1

This
makes
{\mathfrak{A}}
a
\Lambda
-ring
with
Adams operations

p_{k}[-]
. If
{\mathfrak{A}}
is any
\Lambda
-ring, then
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(X)
is the free
\Lambda
-algebra over
{\mathfrak{A}}
on one
generator
X
, and plethystic evaluation can be defined by
f[Z]=\epsilon_{Z}\,f(X)
for
f(X)\in\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(X)
and
Z\in{\mathfrak{A}}
, where
\epsilon_{Z}\colon\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(X)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{A}}
is the unique homomorphism of
\Lambda
-algebras over

{\mathfrak{A}}
such that
\epsilon_{Z}(X)=Z
.
Note that formal series
encompass ordinary and Laurent polynomials, power and Laurent series,
and symmetric functions and series
f(X)
when the variables
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
are among the indeterminates. They also
encompass rational functions of indeterminates if we embed
{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t,\ldots)
into a field
K
of iterated formal Laurent series.
Although such an embedding
{\mathbbm{k}}\subseteq K
is not unique, the choice
is unimportant, since the operations
p_{k}[-]
defined intrinsically
on
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t,\ldots)
are compatible with any Laurent series
expansion. In particular, any multivariate function

f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{l};q,t,\ldots)
of the form in §
2.4
can be considered
as an element of a suitable algebra
{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Definition 2.5.1
.

Given
{\mathfrak{A}}
as above,
Z\in{\mathfrak{A}}
, and
f(X)\in\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)
, where
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
, the

plethystic evaluation

f[Z]
is the result of substituting

p_{k}[Z]
for
p_{k}(X)
in the expansion of
f(X)
as a polynomial
in the power-sums
p_{k}(X)
. The plethystic evaluation

f[Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{l}]
of a multi-symmetric function

f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is defined similarly, as the result of
substituting
p_{k}[Z_{i}]
for
p_{k}(X_{i})
in the expansion of
f

as a polynomial in all of the
p_{k}(X_{i})
. Note that this is
equivalent to applying each plethystic evaluation
X_{i}\mapsto Z_{i}

separately, in any order.

Plethystic evaluations of (multi
-
)symmetric series
f
are defined in
the same way, provided that the result makes sense as a formal series,
i.e., that every coefficient is given by a finite sum.

Since the meaning of
p_{k}[Z]
is intrinsic for any suitable algebra

{\mathfrak{A}}
containing
Z
, we generally don’t specify
{\mathfrak{A}}
. In
situations where we think of
Z
as fixed and
f(X)
as varying, we
also refer to plethystic evaluation
f(X)\mapsto f[Z]
as
specializing
X
to
Z
.

We now recall some standard properties of plethystic evaluation.

(i) Since
p_{k}[z_{1}+z_{2}+\cdots]=p_{k}(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots)
,
it follows immediately that for any symmetric function or series
f
,
we have

(12)

f[z_{1}+z_{2}+\cdots]=f(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots)\,;\quad\text{in
particular,}\quad f[z_{1}+\cdots+z_{n}]=f(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\,.

In light of this, we make the convention that in the context of
symmetric functions or series in a set of indeterminates
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
such that
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
,

(13)

when
X
appears in the argument of
a plethystic evaluation, it stands for the
plethystic alphabet

X=x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots
.

In particular, we have the identity
f[X]=f(X)
. We also use this
convention to define plethystic transformations such as
f(X)\mapsto f[(1-t)X]
. We have taken
{\mathfrak{K}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
rather than

{\mathfrak{K}}={\mathfrak{A}}
in Definition
2.5.1
in order to allow
for the possibility that
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
.

If
X
is a formal alphabet of unspecified variables, we apply
(
13
) by introducing new variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
and expressing the result of the plethystic evaluation in
terms of the original formal alphabet. For example,

(14)

e_{2}[X+Y]=e_{2}[x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+y_{1}+y_{2}+\cdots]=e_{2}(X)+e_{1}(X)e_{1}(Y)+e_{2}(Y).

The result of course does not depend on the choice of names for the
new variables.

(ii) We assumed that
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
and defined plethystic
evaluation in terms of power-sums
p_{k}
for simplicity. However, if

Z\in{\mathfrak{A}}
is a formal series with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, we can
write it as a sum
Z=a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots-b_{1}-b_{2}-\cdots
of
Laurent monomials
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
in the indeterminates, in general
with repeated terms. Then

(15)

h_{k}[Z]=h_{k}[a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots-b_{1}-b_{2}-\cdots]=\sum_{i+j=k}(-1)^{j}h_{i}(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)e_{j}(b_{1},b_{2},\ldots).

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{A}}
be the subring consisting of
elements
Z\in{\mathfrak{A}}
with integer coefficients, and let
{\mathfrak{K}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}={\mathfrak{K}}\cap{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Since
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}}={\mathfrak{K}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}[h_{1},h_{2},\ldots]
, it follows from
(
15
) that if
f(X)
has coefficients in
{\mathfrak{K}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
,
then
f[Z]\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
for all
Z\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(iii) It is often useful to work with plethystic evaluations of the
symmetric series

(16)

\Omega(X)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}h_{k}(X)=\exp\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{p_{k}(X)}{k}\bigr)=\prod_{i}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}},

where
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, the three quantities being equal by

[
38
, (2.10)]
. As an example, and as motivation for
analogous formulas such as (
46
) and
(
67
) later on, we can use the fact that
\Omega[X\,Y]=\prod_{i,j}(1-x_{i}y_{j})^{-1}
for
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
,
Y=y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
to write the classical
Cauchy identity
[
38
, (4.3)]
in the form

(17)

\Omega[X\,Y]=\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(X)s_{\lambda}(Y).

The formula
\Omega(X)=\exp\bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p_{k}(X)/k\bigr)
implies the identity

(18)

\Omega[A+B]=\Omega[A]\,\Omega[B]

whenever the plethystic evaluations
\Omega[A]
and
\Omega[B]
are
defined (e.g.,
\Omega[X]=\Omega(X)
is defined if
X
is a formal
alphabet, but
\Omega[1]
is not defined, since
h_{k}[1]=1
for
all
k
).

(iv) A caution to be observed when using plethystic evaluation is that
it does not commute with substituting values for indeterminates. For
example, although it is true that
f[tX]=f(tx_{1},tx_{2},\ldots)
if
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
and
t
is an indeterminate, it is

not
true that
f[-X]=f(-x_{1},-x_{2},\ldots)
. In fact,

f[-X]=(\omega\,f)(-x_{1},-x_{2},\ldots)
, where
\omega
is
the standard involution on
\Lambda(X)
in
[
38
, (2.7)]
.

However, it is permissible to substitute a Laurent monomial
z^{{\mathbf{e}}}

in indeterminates for an indeterminate, because
p_{k}[z^{{\mathbf{e}}}]=(z^{{\mathbf{e}}})^{k}
for all
k
.
2
2

2

In
\Lambda
-ring terminology,
elements satisfying
p_{k}[x]=x^{k}
for all
k
are
line
elements
. For a substitution
t\mapsto x
to be a
\Lambda
-ring
homomorphism, we must substitute a line element
x
for the line
element
t
.
For example, substituting
tx_{i}
for
x_{i}
in the
identity
f[X]=f(X)
gives
f[tX]=f(tx_{1},tx_{2},\ldots)
.

(v) As an application of (iii)–(iv), (
18
) implies
that
\Omega[-X]=\Omega(X)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}e_{k}(X)
, by
[
38
, (2.6)]
, and therefore also that

(19)

\Omega[t(X-Y)]=\Omega[t\,X]\,\Omega[-t\,Y]=\sum\nolimits_{i,j}(-1)^{j}h_{i}(X)\,e_{j}(Y)\,t^{i+j}.

Taking the coefficient of
t^{k}
and substituting Laurent monomials

a_{i}
,
b_{j}
for the variables in
X
and
Y
proves
(
15
).

(vi) Identifying
\Lambda(X)\otimes\Lambda(X)
with
\Lambda(X,Y)
, so that
f(X)g(Y)
stands for
f\otimes g
, the operation

f(X)\mapsto f[X+Y]
(as in (
14
), for
instance) defines a coproduct
\Delta\colon\Lambda(X)\rightarrow\Lambda(X)\otimes\Lambda(X)
on the algebra of symmetric functions.

2.6.
Symmetric limits

There are several possible senses in which a sequence of polynomials
or power series
f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
may converge to a
symmetric function or series

(20)

f(X)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

in a formal alphabet
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, where
f
and all

f_{n}
have coefficients in a ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
.

(i) We say that the
f_{n}

converge strongly to
f
if

(21)

f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]

for all
n
. Strong convergence implies convergence in the other
senses below. If the
f_{n}
converge strongly, they are symmetric
and satisfy
f_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},0,\ldots,0)=f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
for
m\geq n
. Conversely, these
conditions imply that the
f_{n}
converge strongly to a unique
symmetric series
f(X)
. This is essentially the definition of
symmetric series
[
38
, I.2, pp. 18-19]
.

(ii) Let
t
be an indeterminate in
{\mathfrak{K}}
. We say that the

f_{n}

converge
t
-adically to
f
if for all
e>0
, we have

(22)

f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\equiv f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\pmod{(t^{e})}

for
n
sufficiently large. Here,
f\equiv g\pmod{(t^{e})}
means
that the coefficients of
f-g
vanish to order
\geq e
at
t=0
.
Note that this interpretation makes sense even if
{\mathfrak{K}}
contains

{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
or is a ring of Laurent polynomials or Laurent series in

t
. If
{\mathfrak{K}}
is a polynomial or power series ring in
t
, then

f\equiv g\pmod{(t^{e})}
means that the coefficients of
f-g
are in
the ideal
(t^{e})\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
.

Strictly speaking,
t
-adic convergence does not imply that the

f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
are symmetric, but we will only use it in
that case.

(iii) Suppose that the elements of
{\mathfrak{K}}
are formal series in some
designated variables
Y=y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
, and that taking
coefficients with respect to these variables is well-defined (the
coefficients might be functions of other indeterminates). As in §
2.5
, this encompasses ordinary and Laurent polynomials,
power and Laurent series, and symmetric functions and series in
Y
,
but not rational functions.

We say that the
f_{n}

converge formally to
f
in
X
and
Y

if we have
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
as formal series in
X,Y
—i.e., if for
every monomial
x^{{\mathbf{a}}}y^{{\mathbf{b}}}
, we have
\langle x^{{\mathbf{a}}}y^{{\mathbf{b}}}\rangle f_{n}=\langle x^{{\mathbf{a}}}y^{{\mathbf{b}}}\rangle f
for
n

sufficiently large.

A little more generally, elements of
{\mathfrak{K}}
that are symmetric
functions or series in a formal alphabet
A
can be identified with
symmetric functions in new variables
A=a_{1},a_{2},\ldots

adjoined to
Y
. Formal convergence obviously does not depend on the
names chosen for the new variables. In terms of the original
X
,
Y

and formal alphabet
A
, it is equivalent to the property that for all

{\mathbf{a}}
,
{\mathbf{b}}
, and
\lambda
, we have
\langle x^{{\mathbf{a}}}y^{{\mathbf{b}}}m_{\lambda}(A)\rangle f_{n}=\langle x^{{\mathbf{a}}}y^{{\mathbf{b}}}m_{\lambda}(A)\rangle f
for
n
sufficiently large, or the same with any basis
of
\Lambda(A)
in place of
m_{\lambda}
.

It is by no means necessary that the
f_{n}
be symmetric to have a
symmetric formal limit
f
. In particular, it suffices that
f_{n}

be symmetric in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p(n)}
, where
p(n)
grows without
bound as
n\rightarrow\infty
. This specific situation arises, for
instance, in Corollary
4.6.5
.

2.7.
Diagrams and tableaux

Given
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}
with
\alpha_{j}\leq\beta_{j}

for all
j
, the
diagram

\beta/\alpha
is the array of unit
lattice squares (or
boxes
) whose northeast corners have
coordinates
(i,j)
for
j=1,\ldots,l
and
\alpha_{j}<i\leq\beta_{j}
(if
\alpha_{j}=\beta_{j}
, then the
j
-th row of
\beta/\alpha
is empty). When
\beta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\beta_{l}
and

\alpha_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\alpha_{l}
, the diagram
\beta/\alpha
is a

skew diagram
. In particular, the Young or Ferrers diagram of a
partition
\lambda
is the skew diagram
\lambda/(0^{\ell(\lambda)})
. Note that we use the French style in which the diagram of a
partition has its southwest corner at the origin.

A
filling
of a diagram
\nu
with letters from a set
{\mathcal{A}}
is
a map
T\colon\nu\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
, pictured by placing the letter

T(x)
in each box
x
of
\nu
.

A
signed alphabet
is a totally ordered set
{\mathcal{A}}
equipped
with a partition
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}\coprod{\mathcal{A}}^{-}
into
positive
and
negative
letters. A
super filling
of a
general diagram
\nu
is a map to a signed alphabet. A super filling

T
is
row-increasing
if every row of
T
is weakly increasing
with no repeated negative letters. If
\nu
is a skew diagram, a

super tableau
on
\nu
is a row-increasing super filling

T\colon\nu\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
such that, in addition, each column
of
T
is weakly increasing with no repeated positive letters. If

{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}
has only positive letters, then a super tableau is
a semistandard tableau in the usual sense. The set of semistandard
tableaux is denoted by
\SSYT(\nu,{\mathcal{A}})
.

For
a,b
in a signed alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
, we use the notation

(23)

\displaystyle a\succ b\quad\text{(or $b\prec a$)}

\displaystyle\text{if $a>b$ or
$a=b\in{\mathcal{A}}^{-}$},

\displaystyle a\preccurlyeq b\quad\text{(or $b\succcurlyeq a$)}

\displaystyle\text{if $a<b$ or
$a=b\in{\mathcal{A}}^{+}$}.

Note that
\preccurlyeq
is the negation of
\succ
, and that
\prec
,

\preccurlyeq
reduce to
<
,
\leq
if
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}
has only
positive letters. In this notation, a super filling is row-increasing
if and only if the entries in each row satisfy
a_{1}\preccurlyeq a_{2}\preccurlyeq\cdots\preccurlyeq a_{k}
, and the condition on the columns of a super
tableau can be written
a_{1}\prec a_{2}\prec\cdots\prec a_{k}
.

2.8.
Symmetric LLT polynomials

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\ldots,\nu^{(k)})
be a tuple of diagrams.
We identify
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with the disjoint union of its components,
i.e., a box in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
means a box in a specific component
\nu^{(j)}
. A row-increasing super filling on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is a filling

T\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
whose restriction to each
component
\nu^{(j)}
is a row-increasing super filling; likewise for
super tableaux if
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is a tuple of skew diagrams. We write

\SSYT_{\pm}({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\mathcal{A}})
for the set of super tableaux on

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, or
\SSYT({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\mathcal{A}})
if
\mathcal{A}
has only positive
letters.

The
content
of a box
x
in a diagram
\nu
is
c(x)=i-j
, where

(i,j)
is the northeast corner of
x
.

Definition 2.8.1
.

Given a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of diagrams, boxes
x\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{(i)}
and

y\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{(j)}
form an
attacking pair
if either
i<j
and

c(y)=c(x)
, or
i>j
and
c(y)=c(x)+1
. For a row-increasing super
filling
T:{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\to\mathcal{A}
, an
attacking inversion
in
T

is an attacking pair
(x,y)
such that
T(x)\succ T(y)
. The number
of attacking inversions in
T
is denoted
\inv_{{\rm o}}(T)
.

For a positive alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}
, the
LLT polynomial

indexed by a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of skew diagrams is defined by

(24)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(X;t)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\sum_{T\in\SSYT({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\mathcal{A}})}t^{\inv_{{\rm o}}(T)}\,x^{T},

where
x^{T}=\prod_{u\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}x_{T(u)}
. It is known that

{\mathcal{G}}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(X;t)
is symmetric in the variables
x_{a}
(
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
). Up to factors of the form
t^{e}
, these are the same
polynomials as defined by Lascoux, Leclerc, and Thibon (LLT) in

[
32
]
using ribbon tableaux and spin—see also
[
22
, §5]
and
[
20
, §10]
.

We can regard formula (
24
) for an infinite positive
alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
as defining a symmetric function
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(X;t)\in\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}[t]}(X)
in a formal alphabet
X
. Then we
have the following generalization of formula (
24
)
to signed alphabets.

Lemma 2.8.2

(
[
20
, (81)–(82) and Proposition 4.2]
)
.

Given a signed alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
and a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of skew
diagrams, specializing
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(X;t)
with
X=X^{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{+}}x_{a}-\sum_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}^{-}}x_{b}

yields

(25)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[X^{{\mathcal{A}}};t]=\sum_{T\in\SSYT_{\pm}({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\mathcal{A}})}(-1)^{m(T)}\,t^{\inv_{{\rm o}}(T)}\,x^{T},

where
m(T)=|T^{-1}({\mathcal{A}}^{-})|
is the number of negative entries in

T
.

Remark 2.8.3
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu)
has only one component, then there are no
attacking pairs, and
\inv_{{\rm o}}(T)=0
for every tableau
T
. In this
case,
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(X;t)=s_{\nu}(X)
is a skew Schur function,
and (
25
) is the usual super tableau formula
for the signed alphabet specialization
s_{\nu}[X^{{\mathcal{A}}}]
.

2.9.
\GL_{n}
characters

We recall some standard notation associated to
\GL_{n}
weights and characters.
The weight lattice is
X=X(\GL_{n})={\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
. Thus, its group
algebra
{\mathfrak{K}}\,X
(over any commutative ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
) is the
Laurent polynomial ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
, and the formal exponential of a
weight
\lambda\in X
is the monomial
x^{\lambda}=x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
.

The dominant weights
X_{+}=X_{+}(\GL_{n})
are the weakly
decreasing vectors

(26)

X_{+}=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\mid\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\}.

The antidominant weights
-X_{+}
are the weakly increasing vectors in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
.
The Weyl group is
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, permuting coordinates in
X={\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, and variables in
{\mathfrak{K}}\,X={\mathfrak{K}}[x^{\pm 1}]
.
Note that the stabilizer of a dominant or antidominant weight is always a Young subgroup

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
for some strict composition
{\mathbf{r}}
of
n
.

A weight
\lambda
is
regular
if its stabilizer in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}

is trivial; this means that
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
has distinct entries. Hence, the set of regular
dominant weights is

(27)

X_{++}=\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\mid\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{n}\}.

Any weight
\rho
such that
\rho_{i+1}-\rho_{i}=1
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, for example
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,0)
, is minimally dominant and regular, in the sense
that

(28)

X_{++}=\rho+X_{+}.

Such a weight
\rho
is unique up to adding a constant vector.

The irreducible characters of
\GL_{n}
are given by the Weyl
character formula

(29)

\chi_{\lambda}(x)={\boldsymbol{\pi}}(x^{\lambda})\quad\text{for
$\lambda\in X_{+}$},

where
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is the
Weyl
symmetrization operator

(30)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f(x)=\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}}w\left(\frac{f(x)}{\prod_{i<j}(1-x_{j}/x_{i})}\right).

Here we have written only
i<j
for
1\leq i<j\leq n
, and will do so hereafter whenever the range is clear from context.

The
polynomial
irreducible characters of
\GL_{n}
are the

\chi_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\cap X_{+}
, i.e., for

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0
, so
\lambda
is a
partition of length
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
with possible trailing
zeroes. The polynomial characters are Schur functions, i.e.,

\chi_{\lambda}(x)=s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

2.10.
Levi subgroups

Given a strict composition
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
of
n
, we define

(31)

\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}\cong\GL_{r_{1}}\times\cdots\times\GL_{r_{k}}

to be the subgroup of block diagonal matrices
g\in\GL_{n}
with
blocks of size
r_{i}\times r_{i}
.

The Weyl group of
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
is the Young subgroup
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}

of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. A weight
\lambda
is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}

regular—i.e., its stabilizer in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
is trivial—if
and only if its restriction
(\lambda_{i},\ldots,\lambda_{j})
to each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block
\{i,\ldots,j\}
has distinct entries. Similarly,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}

belongs to the set
X_{+}(\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}})
of
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
dominant
weights if and only if
\lambda_{i}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{j}
on each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block; the set of antidominant weights
-X_{+}(\GL_{\mathbf{r}})
of
\GL_{\mathbf{r}}
consists of
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}

which are weakly increasing on each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block.
As before, the set of regular dominant weights can be
expressed as

(32)

X_{++}(\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}})=\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}+X_{+}(\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}})

if
(\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}})_{i}-(\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}})_{i+1}=1
whenever
i
and
i+1
are in the same
{\mathbf{r}}
-block.
Such a
weight
\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}
is unique up to adding an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
-invariant weight, i.e., a vector constant on each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block.
For combinatorial reasons, in §
6
, we will need the
specific choice

(33)

\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}=(0,\,-1,\,\ldots,\,1-r_{1},\,0,\,-1,\,\ldots,\,1-r_{2},\,\ldots,\,0,\,-1,\,\ldots,\,1-r_{k})\,.

2.11.
Demazure characters and atoms

We review some basic facts and definitions for Demazure characters and
atoms for
\GL_{n}
, recall Lascoux’s Cauchy formula

[
30
]
, and prove some results for our discussion of
atom positivity in §
6.6
. References for
Demazure characters and atoms for
\GL_{n}
include
[
46
,
13
,
31
]
.

The
Demazure operator
or
isobaric divided difference operator
\pi_{i}
(
1\leq i<n
) acting on a
Laurent polynomial
f(x)=f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is given by

(34)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\,f(x)

\displaystyle=(1+s_{i})\frac{f(x)}{1-x_{i+1}/x_{i}},

which is the same as the Weyl symmetrization operator
in variables
x_{i},x_{i+1}
.
The Demazure operators satisfy the braid relations
\pi_{i}\pi_{i+1}\pi_{i}=\pi_{i+1}\pi_{i}\pi_{i+1}
and the 0-Hecke relations

\pi_{i}^{2}=\pi_{i}
. Also define the operators
\widehat{\pi}_{i}=\pi_{i}-1
. These satisfy the braid relations and
\widehat{\pi}_{i}^{2}=-\widehat{\pi}_{i}
.
For
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
with reduced expression
w=s_{j_{1}}s_{j_{2}}\cdots s_{j_{d}}
, define the operators

(35)

\displaystyle\pi_{w}

\displaystyle=\pi_{s_{j_{1}}}\cdots\pi_{s_{j_{d}}},

(36)

\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}_{w}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\pi}_{s_{j_{1}}}\cdots\widehat{\pi}_{s_{j_{d}}},

which do not depend on the choice of reduced expression.

The Demazure operator indexed by the longest permutation is equal to the Weyl symmetrization operator, i.e.,

(37)

\pi_{w_{0}}={\boldsymbol{\pi}}

(see, e.g.,
[
31
, eq. (1.5.2)]
).
It then follows from the 0-Hecke relations that for any
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
,

(38)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\,\pi_{w}={\boldsymbol{\pi}}.

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
, the
Demazure character

{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}={\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)={\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

and
Demazure atom

{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}={\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)={\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for
\GL_{n}
are given by

(39)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\pi_{w}(x^{\lambda_{+}}),

(40)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\pi}_{v}(x^{\lambda_{+}}),

where
\lambda_{+}
is the rearrangement of
\lambda
to a dominant weight,

w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is any permutation such that
\lambda=w(\lambda_{+})
,
and
v\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
is the minimal permutation such that
v(\lambda_{+})=\lambda
.

If
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}

is dominant, then
{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)={\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)=x^{\lambda}
,
while if
\lambda
is antidominant, then
{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)
is the irreducible character
\chi_{\lambda_{+}}(x)
.
The sets
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\}
and

\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)\mid\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\}
are bases of
{\mathfrak{K}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]={\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
for any
coefficient ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
, and the subsets with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}

are bases of
{\mathfrak{K}}[{\mathbf{x}}]
(see, e.g.,
[
46
, Corollary 7]
).

Demazure characters and atoms are related by

(41)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\,\in\,{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}\lambda,\\[2.27621pt]
w_{\mu}\leq w_{\lambda}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x),

where
w_{\eta}
denotes the minimal permutation such that
w_{\eta}(\eta_{+})=\eta
and
\leq
is Bruhat order on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

From
\pi_{i}\widehat{\pi}_{i}=0
,
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=\pi_{w_{0}}
, and the fact that
(
36
) does not depend on the choice of reduced expression, we have

(42)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)=\begin{cases}\chi_{\mu}(x)&\text{if $\mu=\mu_{+}$,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(43)

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)+{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{i}(\mu)}(x)&\text{if $\mu_{i}>\mu_{i+1}$},\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)&\text{if $\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}$},\\
0&\text{if $\mu_{i}<\mu_{i+1}$}.\end{cases}

We record some consequences of these facts for our discussion of atom
positivity in §
6.6
and §
7.6
.

Remark 2.11.1
.

(i) By (
41
), a symmetric polynomial is Schur positive if and only if it is atom positive.

(ii) By (
42
), in the expansion of any Laurent polynomial
f(x)
into Demazure atoms,
the coefficient of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)
for dominant
\lambda
is equal to the
coefficient of
\chi_{\lambda}(x)
in the expansion of
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f(x)
into irreducible
\GL_{n}
characters.

(iii) It follows from (
43
) that
\pi_{w}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)
is a multiplicity-free sum
of atoms
{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta}(x)
with
\eta
a permutation of
\mu
, for
any
w
.

(iv) It also follows from (
43
) that a Laurent
polynomial
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
is symmetric in

x_{i},\ldots,x_{j}
if and only if the coefficients
\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)\rangle\,f(x)
in its expansion in terms of atoms are
independent of permuting
\mu_{i},\ldots,\mu_{j}
.

Let
\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
be the symmetric inner product
on the Laurent polynomial ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]
defined by

(44)

\displaystyle\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{0}\rangle\,f(x)\,g(x)\,\Omega[-\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}].

Proposition 2.11.2

(
[
30
,
13
]
)
.

(i) Demazure characters
{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)
and atoms
{\mathcal{A}}_{-\lambda}(x)
are dual bases for this inner product, i.e.,

(45)

\langle{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x),A_{-\mu}(x)\rangle_{0}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}.

(ii) We have the following ‘Cauchy identities’ for Demazure characters and
atoms:

(46)

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/y_{j}]=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)\mathcal{A}_{-\lambda}(y)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)\mathcal{{\mathcal{D}}}_{-\lambda}(y).

Proof.

Our inner product differs from that of
[
13
]
by the involution
\theta\colon x_{i}\mapsto x_{w_{0}(i)}^{-1}
in the second factor.
Hence, (i) follows from
[
13
, eq. (35)]
and (the second of) the following two identities

(47)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x_{n}^{-1},\ldots,x_{1}^{-1})={\mathcal{D}}_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}(x)\ \ \,\text{ and }\ \ \,{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x_{n}^{-1},\ldots,x_{1}^{-1})={\mathcal{A}}_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}(x),

which in turn follow from
\theta\pi_{i}\theta=\pi_{n-i}
.
Likewise, both versions of the Cauchy identity in (
46
) can be obtained from the one in

[
30
, Theorem 6]
using (
47
).
∎

By virtue of (
45
),
(either) Cauchy identity in (
46
)
is equivalent to each of the two
‘reproducing kernel’ identities

(48)

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\langle f(z),\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0}\quad\text{for $f(x)\in{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]$;}

(49)

\displaystyle g(y)

\displaystyle=\langle g(z),\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}z_{i}/y_{j}]\rangle_{0}\quad\text{for $g(y)\in{\mathfrak{K}}[y_{1}^{-1},\ldots,y_{l}^{-1}]$,}

where the inner products are in the
z
variables.

Lemma 2.11.3
.

For a dominant regular weight
\lambda\in X_{++}(\GL_{n})
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{w_{0}(\lambda)}(x)=x^{w_{0}(\rho)}\chi_{\lambda-\rho}(x)
for any

\rho
as in §
2.9
.

Proof.

By
[
31
, eq. (1.5.3)]
, the operator
\widehat{\pi}_{w_{0}}
can be
expressed in terms of the Weyl symmetrization operator by

(50)

\widehat{\pi}_{w_{0}}\,=x^{w_{0}(\rho)}\,{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\,x^{-\rho}.

Applying both sides to
x^{\lambda}
gives the result.
∎

Lemma 2.11.4
.

Assume that
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
, so
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)
is a polynomial.
For any
k
, setting
x_{k}=0
and re-indexing, we have

(51)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1},0,x_{k},\ldots,x_{n-1})=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})&\text{if $\mu_{k}=0$,}\\
0&\text{if $\mu_{k}>0$,}\end{cases}

where
\eta=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k-1},\mu_{k+1},\ldots,\mu_{n})
.

Proof.

If
\mu=\mu_{+}
is dominant, then
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}=x^{\mu_{+}}
,
and the result is clear. Otherwise, we prove it (for all
k
) by
induction on the length of the minimal
w
such that
\mu=w(\mu_{+})
.

If
\mu_{i}<\mu_{i+1}
for some
i\not\in\{k,k-1\}
, then

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}=\widehat{\pi}_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{i}\mu}
, and the result
holds for
s_{i}\mu
by induction. The result for
\mu
follows
because
\widehat{\pi}_{i}
commutes with setting
x_{k}=0
. Thus, we
can assume that we either have
\mu_{k-1}<\mu_{k}
(with
k>1
),
or
\mu_{k}<\mu_{k+1}
(with
k<n
).

If
\mu_{k-1}<\mu_{k}
, then we are in the case
\mu_{k}>0
, and
must show that
x_{k}
divides
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}=\widehat{\pi}_{k-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k-1}\mu}
. We can assume by induction that
x_{k-1}
divides
{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k-1}\mu}
. The result now follows because
x_{k}
divides

\widehat{\pi}_{k-1}x_{k-1}^{a}x_{k}^{b}
if
a>0
.

If
\mu_{k}<\mu_{k+1}
, then
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}=\widehat{\pi}_{k}{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k}\mu}
. We can assume by induction that if
(s_{k}\mu)_{k+1}=\mu_{k}>0
, then
{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k}\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},0,x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n-1})=0
, and if
(s_{k}\mu_{k})_{k+1}=\mu_{k}=0
,
then
{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k}\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},0,x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n-1})={\mathcal{A}}_{\eta}
, where
\eta=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k-1},\mu_{k+1},\ldots,\mu_{n})
as above. Since
(s_{k}\mu)_{k}>0
, we can also
assume by induction that
x_{k}
divides
{\mathcal{A}}_{s_{k}\mu}
. The
result now follows using that, for
a>0
,
(\widehat{\pi}_{k}x_{k}^{a}x_{k+1}^{b})|_{x_{k}=0}=x_{k+1}^{a}0^{b}
and hence,

\big((\widehat{\pi}_{k}x_{k}^{a}x_{k+1}^{b})|_{x_{k}=0}\big)_{x_{k+1}\mapsto x_{k}}=(x_{k}^{a}x_{k+1}^{b})|_{x_{k+1}=0}
.
∎

Corollary 2.11.5
.

For
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
, setting any subset of the variables to zero
and re-indexing, we have that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(0,\ldots,0,x_{1},0,\ldots,0,x_{2},0,\ldots,0,\ldots,0,\ldots,0,x_{m},0,\ldots,0)

is equal to zero if we set
x_{i}=0
for any
i
such that
\mu_{i}>0
; otherwise it is equal to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
,
where
\eta
is obtained from
\mu
by deleting the entries
\mu_{i}=0
in the positions
i
corresponding to the variables
x_{i}

that we set equal to zero.

3.
Flagged symmetric functions

Broadly speaking, the concept of flagged symmetric functions
originated in the study of flagged Schur polynomials and, more
generally, Schubert polynomials, with a history going back at least to
Macdonald’s notes
[
40
]
, in which he in turn credits
earlier work of Lascoux for many of the main ideas. However, in

[
40
]
and subsequent literature, one does not find a
precise formalization of the general setting in which flagged
symmetric function techniques apply.
As we need such a formalization for our work here, we now introduce a
general definition of ‘flagged symmetric function.’ This concept will
provide a natural framework for the definition of nonsymmetric
plethysm in §
4
. Furthermore, flagged LLT
polynomials—whose definition and study are at the heart of this
work—are examples of flagged symmetric functions. The flagged
symmetric function formalism will thus turn out be useful for deriving
results about nonsymmetric plethystic transformations on flagged LLT
polynomials, such as Proposition
6.7.3
.

3.1.
The space of flagged symmetric functions

Let
\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})=\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
be the algebra of multi-symmetric functions
over a ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
which will usually be implicit from the context.
Although
{\mathfrak{K}}
is not assumed to be a field, we use the term
‘(sub)space’ as a shorthand for ‘free
{\mathfrak{K}}
-(sub)module.’

We say that a multi-symmetric function
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is a

flagged symmetric function
if it belongs to the distinguished
subspace
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\subset\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
defined as follows.

Definition 3.1.1
.

The space
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
of
flagged symmetric
functions
is the subspace of
\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
spanned
by the functions

(52)

{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}h_{a_{1}}[X_{1}]h_{a_{2}}[X_{1}+X_{2}]\cdots h_{a_{l}}[X_{1}+\cdots+X_{l}]

for
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
.

We also write
\mathcal{F\!L}_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
if we need to
make
{\mathfrak{K}}
explicit. Note that
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is
only a subspace, not a subalgebra, of
\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
.

The expansion of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
in the basis

\{h_{\lambda^{(1)}}(X_{1})\cdots h_{\lambda^{(l)}}(X_{l})\}
of

\Lambda(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
has leading term

h_{a_{1}}(X_{1})\cdots h_{a_{l}}(X_{l})
, with respect to any
ordering that extends lexicographic order on the reversed degree
sequence
(|\lambda^{(l)}|,\ldots,|\lambda^{(1)}|)
. Since they
have distinct leading terms, the elements
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
are linearly independent, hence they form a
free
{\mathfrak{K}}
-module basis of
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
.

Traditional notions such as flagged Schur functions fit into this
framework when we specialize the formal alphabets
X_{i}
to blocks of
variables in a finite alphabet
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
in the following
way.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Given integers
0=b_{0}\leq b_{1}\leq\cdots\leq b_{l}\leq n
, the

specialization with flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
of
a flagged symmetric function
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is its
evaluation
f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
in the finite alphabets

(53)

X_{i}^{b}=x_{b_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+x_{b_{i}}\quad\text{or,
equivalently,}\quad X_{1}^{b}+\cdots+X_{i}^{b}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{b_{i}}.

Thus, the specialization
f[X_{1}^{b},\ldots,X_{l}^{b}]
is a polynomial
in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{l}}
, symmetric in each of
l
blocks of
consecutive variables of sizes
b_{1},b_{2}-b_{1}
, …,

b_{l}-b_{l-1}
. We allow the number
n
of ambient variables to
exceed
b_{l}
for flexibility, although
f[X_{1}^{b},\ldots,X_{l}^{b}]

is independent of any variables beyond
x_{b_{l}}
.

The following examples may help to motivate the definitions.

Example 3.1.3
.

(i) The
flagged complete symmetric functions

{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
in (
52
) are flagged
symmetric functions by definition. The specialization of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
with flag bounds
b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
is the
product

(54)

h_{a_{1}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{1}})h_{a_{2}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{2}})\cdots h_{a_{l}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{l}}).

In particular, the specialization of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
with flag bounds
b_{1}=\ldots=b_{l}=n

is the usual complete symmetric function
h_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, where
\lambda
is the partition whose parts
are the nonzero parts of
{\mathbf{a}}
. The polynomials
{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
appear in the study of Schubert polynomials and
related polynomials—see, e.g.,
[
12
, §11]
,
[
33
]
, and
[
40
, Chapter
III]
.

(ii)
The
flagged skew Schur function
indexed by a skew diagram

\lambda/\mu
with
l
rows is defined by the
l\times l

determinant

(55)

{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda/\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})=\det\biggl(h_{\lambda_{i}-\mu_{j}+j-i}[X_{1}+\cdots+X_{i}]\\
\biggr),

generalizing the Jacobi-Trudi formula for an ordinary skew Schur
function. From the Leibniz formula for the determinant, we see that

{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda/\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is a flagged
symmetric function in the sense of Definition
3.1.1
.
Up to a change of notation for the formal alphabets
X_{i}
, flagged skew
Schur functions are the same as the multi-Schur functions
studied in
[
33
]
and
[
40
, eq. (3.1)]
.

Specializing with flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
recovers the
flagged skew Schur functions of
[
34
]
, which by

[
15
,
49
]
are also given by the
combinatorial formula

(56)

{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda/\mu}[X_{1}^{b},\ldots,X_{l}^{b}]=\sum\nolimits_{T}x^{T}\,,

where the sum is over tableaux
T\in\SSYT(\lambda/\mu)
such that
all entries in the
i
-th row of
T
are bounded above by
b_{i}
.

The following property is immediate from the definitions.

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
be the specialization of a flagged
symmetric function with flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
.
Setting
x_{k}=0
in
f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
and re-indexing the
variables
x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{n}
to
x_{k},\ldots,x_{n-1}
yields the
specialization
f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
of
f
with flag
bounds

b^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}b_{i}-1,&\text{if $b_{i}\geq k$}\\
b_{i},&\text{if $b_{i}<k$}.\end{cases}

The specialization with flag bounds
1,\ldots,l
, that is, with
the formal alphabets specialized to individual variables
X_{i}=x_{i}
, plays a special role.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

The evaluation map

(57)

\xi\colon\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\rightarrow{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}],\quad f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\mapsto f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}],

defined by specializing each
X_{i}
to a single variable
x_{i}
, is
a
{\mathfrak{K}}
-linear isomorphism from
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
to
the polynomial ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
in
l
variables.

Proof.

The specialized elements
{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]=h_{a_{1}}(x_{1})h_{a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots h_{a_{l}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
are unitriangularly related to

h_{a_{1}}(x_{1})h_{a_{2}}(x_{2})\cdots h_{a_{l}}(x_{l})=x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{l}^{a_{l}}
.
∎

Remark 3.1.6
.

The isomorphism
\xi
is independent of
l
in the following sense.
For
l<m
, the space
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
embeds in
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
as the subspace consisting of flagged symmetric
functions
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
which are independent of
X_{i}
for

i>l
. If
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})
is in this subspace, then

f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
is a polynomial in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}
, and
the isomorphism for
l
is the restriction of the one for
m
.

Remark 3.1.7
.

An arbitrary multi-symmetric function
f(X_{1},\dots,X_{l})
is
determined by its specialization
f[X^{b}_{1},\dots,X^{b}_{l}]
with
X^{b}_{i}

as in (
53
) when the
b_{i}-b_{i-1}
are
sufficiently large. More precisely, expanding
f
in the basis of
elements
m_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}=m_{\lambda^{(1)}}(X_{1})m_{\lambda^{(2)}}(X_{2})\cdots m_{\lambda^{(l)}}(X_{l})
of the algebra
of multi-symmetric functions, the coefficient of
m_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
in

f(X_{1},\dots,X_{l})
is equal to the coefficient of
x^{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}
in

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
when
b_{i}-b_{i-1}\geq\ell(\lambda^{(i)})
.

Lemma
3.1.5
implies, by contrast, that if
f
is a flagged
symmetric function, then the specialization with flag bounds
b=1,2,\dots,l
already determines
f
.

In a sense, Lemma
3.1.5
means that flagged symmetric functions
are no different from ordinary (nonsymmetric) polynomials. The point,
however, is that by passing between the two, we can make use of
plethystic operations on flagged symmetric functions to
give natural constructions of new operations on
ordinary polynomials that would otherwise appear quite
artificial, or be difficult to define at all.

3.2.
Action of Demazure operators and Weyl symmetrization

Let
f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
be a flagged symmetric function
specialized with flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
. If
k
is
equal to a flag bound, then
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
are not in the same
block of variables, and
f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
is generally not
symmetric in
x_{k},x_{k+1}
. The next proposition says that if the
flag bound
b_{j}=k
is not repeated, then the Demazure operator

\pi_{k}
symmetrizes in
x_{k},x_{k+1}
by raising
b_{j}
. Note,
this observation was made in
[
40
, eq. (3.10)]
for
flagged Schur functions using a similar argument.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Given flag bounds
b_{1}\leq\cdots\leq b_{l}
, suppose that
b_{j}=k
, and that
b_{i}\not=k
for
i\not=j
. If
j=l
, assume further
that
n>k
, where
n
is the number of variables. Define new flag
bounds
b^{\prime}_{i}
by setting
b^{\prime}_{j}=k+1
and
b^{\prime}_{i}=b_{i}
for

i\not=j
. Then the specializations with flag bounds

b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
and
b^{\prime}=b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{l}
of any flagged
symmetric function are related by

(58)

\pi_{k}f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}].

Proof.

By linearity, we can assume that
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
. Our assumptions imply that each factor

h_{a_{i}}[X^{b}_{1}+\cdots+X^{b}_{i}]=h_{a_{i}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{i}})

for
i\not=j
is symmetric in
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
, hence commutes
with
\pi_{k}
. The result now follows from the identity

(59)

\pi_{k}\,h_{a}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=h_{a}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k+1}).\qed

Starting with flag bounds
1,\ldots,l
, then applying
Proposition
3.2.1
repeatedly to raise
l
to

b_{l}
, then
l-1
to
b_{l-1}
, and so on, we obtain the following
corollary.

Corollary 3.2.2
.

Assuming that
b_{i}\geq i
for all
i
, the specialization

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
with flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
of any
flagged symmetric function
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is given in terms
of its evaluation
f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
in single variable alphabets
by

(60)

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=\pi_{w}f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}],

where
w=(s_{b_{1}-1}s_{b_{1}-2}\cdots s_{1})\,(s_{b_{2}-1}s_{b_{2}-2}\cdots s_{2})\cdots(s_{b_{l}-1}s_{b_{l}-2}\cdots s_{l}).

Remark 3.2.3
.

By combining Corollary
3.2.2
with
Lemma
3.1.4
, one can express the specialization

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
with any flag bounds
b
(dropping the
hypothesis
b_{i}\geq i
) as the result of applying a Demazure
operator to
f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
, then setting some variables to
zero and re-indexing.

Corollary 3.2.4
.

Given flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
and
j\leq k
, assume that

b_{i}\geq b_{j-1}+i-j+1
for
i=j,\ldots,k
(where
b_{0}=0
, by
convention, if
j=1
). Let
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
be the Weyl symmetrization
operator in the variables
x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{b_{k}}
that occur
in
X^{b}_{j},\ldots,X^{b}_{k}
. Then

(61)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{j-1},X^{b}_{j}+\cdots+X^{b}_{k},0,\ldots,0,X^{b}_{k+1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}],

i.e., applying
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to the flag bound specialization

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
has the effect of raising all the flag
bounds
b_{j},\ldots,b_{k}
to
b_{k}
. If
b_{i}\geq i
for all
i
,
then Weyl symmetrization in all variables
x_{i}
yields

(62)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=f[X,0,\ldots,0],

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}
.

Proof.

Define new flag bounds
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
by
b^{\prime}_{j}=\cdots=b^{\prime}_{k}=b_{k}
and
b^{\prime\prime}_{i}=b_{j-1}+i-j+1
for
i=j,\ldots,k
, and
b^{\prime}_{i}=b^{\prime\prime}_{i}=b_{i}
for
i\not\in[j,k]
. Note that
b^{\prime\prime}_{i}\leq b_{i}\leq b^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
, and that, by definition,

f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
is equal to the right hand side of
(
61
). In particular,

f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
is symmetric in

x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{b_{k}}
.

By the same procedure that gave
Corollary
3.2.2
, raising the bounds
b^{\prime\prime}_{i}

for
i=j,\ldots,k
one at a time to
b_{i}
, we can find a Demazure
operator
\pi_{v}
that involves only the variables

x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{b_{k}}
, such that

f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=\pi_{v}f[X^{b^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime\prime}}_{l}]
. In the same way, we can find

\pi_{w}
such that
f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]=\pi_{w}f[X^{b^{\prime\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime\prime}}_{l}]
. Since
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\,\pi_{u}={\boldsymbol{\pi}}
for any Demazure operator
\pi_{u}
in the variables

x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{b_{k}}
, it follows that
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]={\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
,
and we have
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]=f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
, since

f[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
is symmetric in

x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{b_{k}}
.

If
k=l
, the same argument with
n\geq b_{l}
in place of
b_{k}

shows that Weyl symmetrization in
x_{b_{j-1}+1},\ldots,x_{n}
yields

(63)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=f[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{j-1},x_{b_{j-1}+1}+\cdots+x_{n},0,\ldots,0].

For
j=1
, this is (
62
).
∎

Example 3.2.5
.

(i) Given
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
, so the Demazure character
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
is a polynomial, let
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})=\xi^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
,
where
\xi
is the isomorphism in Lemma
3.1.5
. Thus,
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
is the unique flagged symmetric function
such that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]={\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
. Corollary
3.2.2
implies
that for flag bounds
b=b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
such that
b_{i}\geq i

for all
i
, and
n\geq b_{l}
, the specialization
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
is a Demazure character
{\mathcal{D}}_{w(\mu;\,0^{n-l})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, for
w
as in the corollary. For
distinct flag bounds
0<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{l}
,
w(\mu;\,0^{n-l})

is the interleaving of
\mu
in positions
b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
with
zeroes in all other positions, giving

(64)

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]={\mathcal{D}}_{(0^{b_{1}-1},\,\mu_{1},\,0^{b_{2}-b_{1}-1},\,\mu_{2},\,\dots\,,\,0^{b_{l}-b_{l-1}-1},\,\mu_{l},\,0^{n-b_{l}})}(x),

In particular, this shows that for any given
\mu
, the Demazure
characters on the right hand side of (
64
) all
fit together as specializations with varying flag bounds of the same
flagged symmetric function
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
.
These flagged symmetric functions are special cases of the
‘multi-symmetric Schur functions’ in
[
2
]
(in the
general case, however, the latter are not in
the subspace of flagged symmetric functions).

(ii) Let
\lambda
be a partition, possibly padded with
zeroes to length
l
. By (
56
), the flagged
Schur function
{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
with
\mu=\emptyset
, evaluated with flag bounds
1,2,\dots,l
, is
{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]=x^{\lambda}
, since there is a
unique tableau
T\in\SSYT(\lambda)
with entries in row
i
bounded
by
i
. But we also have
x^{\lambda}={\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
. Hence, using Lemma
3.1.5
and example (i),
above, we recover the theorem of Reiner-Shimozono
[
46
, Theorem
23]
that any flagged Schur function evaluated with flag bounds

b_{i}\geq i
is given by

(65)

{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]={\mathcal{D}}_{w\,(\lambda;\,0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\,,

with
w
as in Corollary
3.2.2
. Note that
when
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{l}
, (
64
) with
\lambda

in place of
\mu
gives a simpler formula for this. Further, since

{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
only depends on the first

\ell(\lambda)
plethystic alphabets
X^{b}_{i}
, we may assume that
l=\ell(\lambda)
. Then, if the flag bounds do not satisfy
b_{i}\geq i

for all
i
, the set of tableaux
T\in\SSYT(\lambda)
with entries
in row
i
bounded by
b_{i}
is empty, implying that
{\mathfrak{s}}_{\lambda}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=0
.

The next proposition is akin to Example
3.2.5
(i), but
for atoms instead of Demazure characters. Here,
\xi
is again the
isomorphism in Lemma
3.1.5
.

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Given
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})=\xi^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}
be the unique flagged symmetric function such that

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
is the Demazure atom
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
. Then, for any flag bounds
b=b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{l}
and
n\geq b_{l}
, the specialization
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
is a multiplicity-free sum of atoms

(66)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]=\sum_{\eta\in S}{\mathcal{A}}_{\eta}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),

where
S
is a (possibly empty) set of elements
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}

such that the nonzero parts of
\eta
are a permutation of the
nonzero parts of
\mu
.

Proof.

If
b_{i}\geq i
for all
i
, this follows from
Corollary
3.2.2
and Remark
2.11.1
(iii), which says that for any
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
,
\pi_{w}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}
is a sum of the form in (
66
), with
every
\eta
a permutation of
(\mu;0^{n-l})
. In the general
case, Lemma
3.1.4
implies that we can obtain
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l}]
from another specialization
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}[X^{b^{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,X^{b^{\prime}}_{l}]
with flag bounds satisfying

b^{\prime}_{i}\geq i
, by setting some of the variables to zero and
re-indexing. By Corollary
2.11.5
, this procedure
turns any sum of the form in (
66
) into another sum
of the same form.
∎

3.3.
Flagged Cauchy identity

The generating function
\Omega[X\,z^{-1}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}z^{-k}\,h_{k}(X)
for complete symmetric functions immediately
implies the following ‘Cauchy identity’ for flagged symmetric
functions:

(67)

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}\!\!X_{i}\,z_{j}^{-1}]=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l}}z^{-{\mathbf{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{l}).

In particular, the coefficients of
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}\,z_{j}^{-1}]
as a formal power series in

z_{1}^{-1},\ldots,z_{l}^{-1}
belong to
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
.
Hence,

(68)

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}\,z_{j}^{-1}]=\xi^{-1}\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}],

where
\xi
is the isomorphism in Lemma
3.1.5
, and we apply

\xi^{-1}
coefficient-wise to
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]

considered as a power series in the
z_{i}^{-1}
with coefficients in

{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
.

3.4.
Coproduct

As discussed in §
2.5
, (vi),
the plethystic operation
X\mapsto X+Y
induces a coproduct
\Delta\colon\Lambda(X)\rightarrow\Lambda(X)\otimes\Lambda(Y)
. We can
extend this to a coproduct
\Delta
on multi-symmetric functions by
taking the symmetric function coproduct
X_{i}\mapsto X_{i}+Y_{i}
in
each formal alphabet. As shown in the next proposition, this
coproduct preserves flagged symmetric functions.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

For
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\in\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
, the multi-symmetric function
f[X_{1}+Y_{1},\ldots,X_{l}+Y_{l}]
lies in
\mathcal{F\!L}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\otimes\mathcal{F\!L}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l})
. Explicitly,

(69)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[X_{1}+Y_{1},\ldots,X_{l}+Y_{l}]=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}}{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{p}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{l})\,,

where all indices
{\mathbf{a}}
,
{\mathbf{p}}
, and
{\mathbf{q}}
are in
{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
.

Proof.

By definition,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[X_{1}+Y_{1},\ldots,X_{l}+Y_{l}]=h_{a_{1}}[X_{1}+Y_{1}]h_{a_{2}}[X_{1}+X_{2}+Y_{1}+Y_{2}]\cdots
. Now, apply the
coproduct formula
h_{a_{k}}[X_{1}+\dots+X_{k}+Y_{1}+\dots+Y_{k}]=\sum_{p_{k}+q_{k}=a_{k}}h_{p_{k}}[X_{1}+\cdots+X_{k}]h_{q_{k}}[Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{k}]

on each factor.
∎

4.
Nonsymmetric plethysm

In this section we introduce the operation
\Pi_{A,x}
of
nonsymmetric plethysm
on polynomials in variables

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. We view
\Pi_{A,x}
and its inverse
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
as natural nonsymmetric analogs of the plethystic
transformations on symmetric functions
f(X)\mapsto{f[X/(1-A)]}
and

f(X)\mapsto f[(1-A)X]
, respectively.

4.1.
Initial definition

From here through §
4.6
, we define
\Pi_{A,x}
,
characterize it in various ways, and show that the transformations

f(X)\mapsto{f[X/(1-A)]}
and
f(X)\mapsto f[(1-A)X]
on symmetric
functions are limiting cases of
\Pi_{A,x}
and
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
.
To develop the theory, we work in the context where
A
is a formal
alphabet. Afterwards we explain how to specialize to the
one-parameter case
A=t
, where
t
is an indeterminate in the
coefficient ring, which is what we use for the applications to flagged
LLT polynomials and nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials in §§
6
–
7
. For now, we don’t restrict
ourselves to the one-parameter case, because the general case is no
harder, clarifies the reasoning, and may have other potential
applications.

To define
\Pi_{A,x}
in the generic context, let
\Lambda(A)=\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)
be the algebra of symmetric functions in a
formal alphabet
A
over a coefficient ring
{\mathfrak{K}}
, where
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
, as in §
2.3
. Recall
from Definition
3.1.1
that the space
\mathcal{F\!L}_{\Lambda(A)}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
of flagged symmetric functions
over
\Lambda(A)
has a basis consisting of elements
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
for
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
. Evaluating them with

X_{1}=x_{1}
and
X_{i}=x_{i}-A\,x_{i-1}
for
i>1
yields
polynomials

(70)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]\\
=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]\,h_{a_{2}}[x_{2}+(1-A)\,x_{1}]\cdots h_{a_{n}}[x_{n}+(1-A)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1})].

By the same triangularity argument as in the proof of
Lemma
3.1.5
, these polynomials form a basis of
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
as a free
\Lambda(A)
-module.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The
nonsymmetric plethysm

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}
, or

\Pi_{A,x}
for short, is the invertible
\Lambda(A)
-linear
operation on
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
given on the basis in
(
70
) by

(71)

\Pi_{A,x}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}].

For convenience, we define

(72)

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[X_{1},X_{2}-AX_{1},\ldots,X_{n}-AX_{n-1}]

(a multi-symmetric function, but not, in general, a flagged symmetric
function), and write

(73)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\,,\qquad{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2}-Ax_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-Ax_{n-1}]\,,

in terms of which (
71
) takes the form
\Pi_{A,x}\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}.

It can be helpful to picture the definition in the following way.
Define a deformation
\xi_{A}\colon\mathcal{F\!L}_{\Lambda(A)}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\rightarrow\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
of
the isomorphism
\xi
in Lemma
3.1.5
by

(74)

\xi_{A}\,f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=f[x_{1},\,x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}].

Then
\Pi_{A,x}
and
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
are determined by the
following commutative diagram.

(75)

\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
\mathcal{F\!L}_{\Lambda(A)}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{A,x}}

Remark 4.1.2
.

(i) In the definition of
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}
, the ordering
of the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
matters. However, the number of
variables does not really matter: if
f
is a function of the first

m
variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
, then
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}f(x)=\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}f(x)
.
This follows directly from the definition, using the fact that

{{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})={{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-m})}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
for
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
.

(ii) The operations
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
on
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
preserve
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. They can then be defined on
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]={\mathfrak{K}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
for any
{\mathfrak{K}}
by extending scalars
from
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. It follows that all of
the results from here through
\lx@sectionsign\ref{ss:symmetric-limit}
hold
without the assumption
{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
.

Example 4.1.3
.

We calculate the plethysm

\Pi_{A,x}
on monomials
x^{{\mathbf{a}}}\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
in two cases.

(i) For monomials
x_{n}
of degree
1
and any
n
, we first note
that Definition
4.1.1
gives

(76)

\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{(0^{n-1};1)}={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{(0^{n-1};1)}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}-h_{1}(A)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}).

Writing
x_{n}=h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]-h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{(0^{n-1};1)}-{\mathfrak{h}}_{(0^{n-2};1)}
,
