we obtain

(77)

\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,x_{n}=x_{n}-h_{1}(A)\,x_{n-1},\quad\text{or, equivalently,}\quad\Pi_{A,x}(x_{n})=x_{n}+h_{1}(A)\,\Pi_{A,x}(x_{n-1})\,.

Iterating this gives

(78)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}\,x_{n}=x_{n}+h_{1}(A)\,x_{n-1}+\cdots+h_{1}(A)^{n-1}\,x_{1}.

(ii) For any monomial in
n=2
variables, we note that the bases

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{\mathbf{a}}
of
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},x_{2}]
are given by

{\mathfrak{h}}_{a_{1},a_{2}}=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]h_{a_{2}}[x_{1}+x_{2}]
and
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{a_{1},a_{2}}=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]h_{a_{2}}[x_{2}+(1-A)x_{1}]
. Setting
Y=x_{2}+(1-A)x_{1}
,
we have

(79)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{a_{1},a_{2}}=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]\,h_{a_{2}}[Y+A\,x_{1}]=h_{a_{1}}[x_{1}]\sum_{i+j=a_{2}}h_{j}[A\,x_{1}]\,h_{i}[Y]\\
=\sum_{i+j=a_{2}}h_{j}(A)\,h_{a_{1}+j}[x_{1}]\,h_{i}[Y]=\sum_{i+j=a_{2}}h_{j}(A)\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{a_{1}+j,i}.

We can now compute
\Pi_{A,x}(x_{2}^{2})
, for instance,
by first expressing
x_{2}^{2}
in terms of the
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{a_{1},a_{2}}
,
as follows:

\displaystyle x_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2})-x_{1}(x_{1}+x_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}_{0,2}-{\mathfrak{h}}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\big(h_{2}(A)\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{2,0}+h_{1}(A)\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{1,1}+{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{0,2}\big)-\big(h_{1}(A)\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{2,0}+{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{1,1}\big)\qquad\text{by \eqref{e:h-by-ht-n=2}}

\displaystyle=(h_{2}(A)-h_{1}(A))\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{2,0}+(h_{1}(A)-1)\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{1,1}+{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{0,2}\,.

Then

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}(x_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=(h_{2}(A)-h_{1}(A))\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{2,0}+(h_{1}(A)-1)\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{1,1}+{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,2}

\displaystyle=x_{2}^{2}+h_{1}(A)\,x_{1}x_{2}+h_{2}(A)\,x_{1}^{2}\,.

A similar computation shows that, more generally,

(80)

\Pi_{A,x}(x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b})=x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}+h_{1}(A)x_{1}^{a+1}x_{2}^{b-1}+h_{2}(A)x_{1}^{a+2}x_{2}^{b-2}+\cdots+h_{b}(A)x_{1}^{a+b}.

In particular, when
A=t
, this reduces to

(81)

\Pi_{A,x}(x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b})=x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}+t\,x_{1}^{a+1}x_{2}^{b-1}+t^{2}x_{1}^{a+2}x_{2}^{b-2}+\cdots+t^{b}x_{1}^{a+b}.

We will present a number of alternative characterizations of
\Pi_{A,x}
and its inverse. The first is essentially just a
reformulation of the definition.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

We have the identity

(82)

\Pi_{A,x}\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}],

where
\Pi_{A,x}
is applied to each coefficient in the expansion of

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
as a
power series in
z_{1}^{-1},\ldots,z_{n}^{-1}
over
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

Proof.

Specializing the flagged Cauchy identity (
67
) in
two ways, we obtain

(83)

\displaystyle\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}z^{-{\mathbf{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}];

(84)

\displaystyle\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}z^{-{\mathbf{a}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}].

This given, (
82
) is immediate from the definition of

\Pi_{A,x}
.
∎

Another straightforward consequence of the definition is a formula for
inverse nonsymmetric plethysm on a flagged symmetric function
specialized with strictly increasing flag bounds.

Proposition 4.1.5
.

For any flagged symmetric function
f\in\mathcal{F\!L}_{\Lambda(A)}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
, the inverse nonsymmetric plethysm of its
specialization with flag bounds
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{l}
is given by

(85)

\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,f[X_{1}^{b},\,X_{2}^{b},\,\ldots,\,X_{l}^{b}]=\,f\bigl[X_{1}^{b}-A\,{X^{\prime}_{1}}^{b},\,X_{2}^{b}-A\,{X^{\prime}_{2}}^{b},\ldots,X_{l}^{b}-A\,{X^{\prime}_{l\,}}^{b}\bigr]\,,

where
X^{b}_{i}=x_{b_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+x_{b_{i}}
, as in
Definition
3.1.2
, and we set
X^{\prime}=(0,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
, so
{X^{\prime}_{i\,}}^{b}=x_{b_{i-1}}+\cdots+x_{b_{i}-1}
.

Proof.

If
b_{1}=0
, the result reduces to the case with
l-1
formal alphabets

X_{i}
and flag bounds
b_{2},\ldots,b_{l}
. Assume now that

b_{1}>0
. Reducing by linearity to the case
f={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
, the specialization of
f
with flag bounds

b_{i}
becomes
\prod_{i}h_{a_{i}}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{b_{i}}]
.
Since the
b_{i}
are distinct and nonzero, this is equal to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{l}}]
, where
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}_{b_{i}}=a_{i}
and

{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}_{j}=0
for all other
j
. The left hand side of
(
85
) is therefore
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{b_{l}}]
, which is equal to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}-A\,x_{l-1}]
by definition.
This is the same as the right hand side of
(
85
) for
f={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}
.
∎

4.2.
Inner product formulas for nonsymmetric plethysm

Define an inner product

(86)

\displaystyle\langle f(x),\,g(x)\rangle_{A}\,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\,\langle x^{0}\rangle f(x)\,g(x)\,\Omega[(A-1)\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]

(87)

\displaystyle=\langle f(x),\,g(x)\,\Omega[A\,\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]\rangle_{0}

on
\Lambda(A)[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
, generalizing the
inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
in
(
44
), as well as the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{t}
in (
166
), which will be discussed when
we get to nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomials. We will express
the nonsymmetric plethysm
\Pi_{A,x}
in terms of the inner product

\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
by means of two different formulas. To
derive these formulas we first need the following ‘reproducing kernel’
property of
\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

For all
f(x)\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},,\ldots,x_{n}]
, we have

(88)

f(x)=\langle f(z),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{A},

where the inner product is in the
z
variables, and applies to each
coefficient in the expansion of
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
as a power series in
x
over the Laurent
polynomial ring
\Lambda(A)[z_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,z_{n}^{\pm 1}]
.

Proof.

The right hand side of (
88
) is equal to

(89)

\displaystyle\langle z^{0}\rangle\,f(z)\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}+A\,(\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}-\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle z^{0}\rangle\,f(z)\,\frac{\prod_{i<j}(1-z_{i}/z_{j})}{\prod_{i\leq j}(1-x_{i}/z_{j})}\,\Omega[A\,(\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}-\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j})].

Since
f(z)
is a polynomial, everything in
(
89
) other than the factor
1-x_{1}/z_{1}
in the denominator is a power series in
z_{1}
.
Expanding
(1-x_{1}/z_{1})^{-1}
as a geometric series in

x_{1}/z_{1}
, we have
\langle z_{1}^{0}\rangle\,g(z_{1})\,(1-x_{1}/z_{1})^{-1}=g(x_{1})
for any power series
g(z_{1})
.
Thus, after taking the constant term
\langle z_{1}^{0}\rangle
in
(
89
), and observing that apart from the
factor
(1-x_{1}/z_{1})^{-1}
, everything with index
i=1
cancels
when
z_{1}=x_{1}
, we are left with

(90)

\langle z_{2}^{0}\cdots z_{n}^{0}\rangle f(x_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})\,\frac{\prod_{1<i<j}(1-z_{i}/z_{j})}{\prod_{1<i\leq j}(1-x_{i}/z_{j})}\,\Omega[A\,(\sum_{1<i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}-\sum_{1<i<j}x_{i}/z_{j})].

This is equal to
f(x)
by induction on the number of variables, the
one-variable case being trivial.
∎

Proposition 4.2.2
.

For all
f(x)\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, we have the
identity

(91)

\Pi_{A,x}f(x)=\langle f(z),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{A},

where the inner product is in the
z
variables.

Proof.

Apply
\Pi_{A,x}
to each side of (
88
),
noting that
\Pi_{A,x}
moves inside the inner product, since it
operates on the
x
variables. Then use
Proposition
4.1.4
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.3
.

For all
f(x)\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
and
g(x)\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}]
, we have

(92)

\langle\Pi_{A,x}f(x),g(x)\rangle_{0}=\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle_{A},

where
\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
is as in
(
44
).

Proof.

Take
\langle-,g(x)\rangle_{0}
on both sides of
(
91
). On the right hand side, the inner product
in the
x
variables moves inside inner product in the
z
variables
in (
91
), replacing
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
with
\langle g(x),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0}
, which is equal to
g(z)
, by
(
49
). This gives
\langle f(z),g(z)\rangle_{A}
, which is the same as
\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle_{A}
.
∎

4.3.
Polynomial part formula for nonsymmetric plethysm

For the next characterization of
\Pi_{A,x}
, we first introduce a
suitable notion of ‘polynomial part’ of a Laurent polynomial.
For the moment, we denote the coefficient ring by
{\mathfrak{R}}

instead of
{\mathfrak{K}}
, to avoid confusion when we take

{\mathfrak{R}}=\Lambda(A)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)
.

Let
N
be the orthogonal complement of
{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{-1}]={\mathfrak{R}}[x_{1}^{-1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}]
in
{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]={\mathfrak{R}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
, with respect to the inner
product
\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
in (
44
).
Since both
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\in-{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\}
and
\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\in-{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\}
span
{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{-1}]
, we have

(93)

N={\mathfrak{R}}\{{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\not\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\}={\mathfrak{R}}\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}\mid\lambda\not\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\}.

In particular, this implies that we have a direct sum decomposition

{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]={\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}]\oplus N
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

The
polynomial part

f(x)_{\pol}
of a Laurent polynomial

f(x)\in{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]
is the image of
f(x)
under the
projection of
{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]
on
{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}]
with kernel

N
. In other words,
f(x)_{\pol}
is the truncation of either
expansion
f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}c_{\lambda}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}(x)
or
f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}}b_{\lambda}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\lambda}(x)
to terms indexed by
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
.

Example 4.3.2
.

Using
x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}={\mathcal{A}}_{(-1,1)}-{\mathcal{A}}_{(0,0)}
, we have

(x_{1}^{-1}x_{2})_{\pol}=-{\mathcal{A}}_{(0,0)}=-1
.
This shows that the polynomial part operator is not the same as the naïve truncation of a Laurent
polynomial to monomials with nonnegative exponents.

Remark 4.3.3
.

The restriction of the polynomial part operator to symmetric Laurent polynomials
recovers the following familiar notion of polynomial truncation:
it just takes the sum

f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in X_{+}}c_{\lambda}\,\chi_{\lambda}

to its truncation
f(x)_{\pol}=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\cap X_{+}}c_{\lambda}\,\chi_{\lambda}
to
polynomial irreducible characters.

The Cauchy identity for Demazure characters and atoms (
46
) immediately implies the following
formula for
f(x)_{\pol}
.

Lemma 4.3.4
.

For all
f\in{\mathfrak{R}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]
, we have

(94)

f(x)_{\pol}=\langle f(z),\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0},

where the inner product is in the
z
variables.

Now we can give a formula for
\Pi_{A,x}
in terms of the polynomial
part defined as above.

Proposition 4.3.5
.

For all
f\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, we have

(95)

\Pi_{A,x}f(x)=\bigl(f(x)\,\Omega[A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]\bigr)_{\pol},

where
(-)_{\pol}
applies term by term in the expansion of
f(x)\,\Omega[A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]
as a symmetric formal series in

A
with coefficients in
{\mathfrak{K}}[{\mathbf{x}}^{\pm 1}]
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.3.4
, formula (
87
),
and Proposition
4.2.2
, we have

(96)

\displaystyle\bigl(f(x)\,\Omega[A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]\bigr)_{\pol}

\displaystyle=\langle f(z)\,\Omega[A\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}],\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\langle f(z),\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{A}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{A,x}f(x).

All steps hold term by term as symmetric series in
A
.
∎

Note that, although the right hand side of (
95
) is

a priori
an infinite series, it is a consequence of the proof
above that the series terminates. Alternatively, we can see directly
that only finitely many terms of the series contribute to the
polynomial part, using the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

If
{{\mathbf{a}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
with
a_{k}+a_{k+1}+\dots+a_{n}<0
for some

k
, then
(x^{{\mathbf{a}}})_{\pol}=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3.4
,

(97)

\displaystyle(x^{{\mathbf{a}}})_{\pol}=\langle z^{0}\rangle\,z^{{\mathbf{a}}}\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}]=\langle z^{0}\rangle\frac{z^{{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{i<j}(1-z_{i}/z_{j})}{\prod_{i\leq j}(1-x_{i}/z_{j})}.

Every monomial
z^{{\mathbf{b}}}
that occurs in the expansion of the fraction
in the last expression satisfies
\sum_{i=k}^{n}b_{i}\leq\sum_{i=k}^{n}a_{i}<0
. Hence the coefficient of
z^{0}
is zero.
∎

Example 4.3.7
.

We revisit our computations in Example
4.1.3
, now using
Proposition
4.3.5
.

(i) When
n=2
and
f(x)=x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}
, as in Example
4.1.3
(ii), formula
(
95
) becomes

(98)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}\,x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}

\displaystyle=\big(x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}\sum_{i\geq 0}h_{i}[A\,x_{1}/x_{2}]\big)_{\pol}

\displaystyle=\big(x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}+h_{1}(A)x_{1}^{a+1}x_{2}^{b-1}+\cdots+h_{b}(A)x_{1}^{a+b}+h_{b+1}(A)x_{1}^{a+b+1}x_{2}^{-1}+\cdots\big)_{\pol}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{a}x_{2}^{b}+h_{1}(A)x_{1}^{a+1}x_{2}^{b-1}+h_{2}(A)x_{1}^{a+2}x_{2}^{b-2}+\cdots+h_{b}(A)x_{1}^{a+b},

using Proposition
4.3.6
and
the fact that
(x^{\mathbf{a}})_{\pol}=x^{\mathbf{a}}
when all exponents are non-negative.

(ii) The case of
\Pi_{A,x}\,x_{3}
, as in Example
4.1.3
(i), demonstrates the dependence of formula (
95
)
on the fact that the polynomial part operator is not the same as
naïve truncation to monomials with nonnegative exponents. In this
case, formula (
95
) gives

(99)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}\,x_{3}

\displaystyle=\big(x_{3}\sum_{i,j,k\geq 0}h_{i}[A\,x_{1}/x_{2}]h_{j}[A\,x_{1}/x_{3}]h_{k}[A\,x_{2}/x_{3}]\big)_{\pol}

\displaystyle=\big(x_{3}+h_{1}(A)(x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}x_{3}+x_{1}+x_{2})+h_{1}(A)^{2}x_{1}\big)_{\pol}\qquad\text{by Proposition \ref{p: pol part tail}}

\displaystyle=x_{3}+h_{1}(A)x_{2}+h_{1}(A)^{2}x_{1},

where the last equality follows from

(100)

\displaystyle(x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}x_{3}+x_{1})_{\pol}=({\mathcal{A}}_{(1,-1,1)})_{\pol}=0.

4.4.
Formulas for the inverse

While the various formulas for
\Pi_{A,x}
in §§
4.1
–
4.3
of course characterize

\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
implicitly, we can also give more explicit formulas
for
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

The inverse nonsymmetric plethysm
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
is given
explicitly by either of the two formulas

(101)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=\langle f(z),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\,\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0}\,,

(102)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl(f(x)\,\Omega[-A\,\sum_{i<j}w_{i}/x_{j}]\bigr)_{\pol}\,|_{w\mapsto x}\ ,

where the inner product in (
101
) is in the
z
variables.

Proof.

Applying
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
to both sides of
(
48
) and using (
82
)
yields (
101
). By Lemma
4.3.4
, the right hand side of
(
102
) is equal to
\langle f(z),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\,\sum_{i<j}w_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0}|_{w\mapsto x}
. Having expressed it in a form not involving
operations in the
x
variables, we are now free to carry out the
substitution
w\mapsto x
, showing that (
102
) is
equivalent to (
101
).
∎

Remark 4.4.2
.

Note that (
102
) is not the same as
(
95
) with
-A
substituted for
A
. The difference
is that in (
95
), all variables
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
in

\Omega[A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}]
affect the polynomial part, while
in (
102
), only the variables
x_{j}
in the
denominators do so, as the substitution
w\mapsto x
occurs after
taking
(-)_{\pol}
in the
x
variables.

Similarly, the definition of
\langle-,-\rangle_{A}

implies that (
91
) is equivalent to

(103)

\Pi_{A,x}f(x)=\langle f(z),\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}+A\sum_{i<j}z_{i}/z_{j}]\rangle_{0},

but (
101
) is not the same as
(
103
) with
-A
substituted for
A
.

4.5.
Recursive formulas

We now derive recursive formulas for
\Pi_{A,x}
and
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
in terms of the same operations in fewer variables.

Proposition 4.5.1
.

For
0<m<n
, we have identities

(104)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\\
=\Bigl(\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}\bigl((\xi^{-1}\Pi_{A,(x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,f)[x_{m+1}+W+(A-1)X,\,x_{m+2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}]\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr|_{W\mapsto X},

(105)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}^{-1}\,f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\\
=\Bigl(\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}^{-1}\,\Pi_{A,(x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n})}^{-1}\,\bigl((\xi^{-1}f)[x_{m+1}+(1-A)\,W-X,\,x_{m+2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}]\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr|_{W\mapsto X},

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
, and
\xi
is the isomorphism from
Lemma
3.1.5
for polynomials in
x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n}
. More
explicitly, for
p(x)\in\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
,
\xi^{-1}\,p
is the unique flagged symmetric function in
n-m
formal alphabets
with coefficients in
\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
such that
(\xi^{-1}\,p)[x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n}]=p(x)
.

Note that the substitution
W\mapsto x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
in formulas
(
104
)–(
105
) takes place after
the recursively applied nonsymmetric plethysm. Thus, the ‘dummy
formal alphabet’
W
acts as a placeholder for a copy of
x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
shielded from the action of
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}^{\pm 1}
.

Proof.

It suffices to verify (
104
) formally for
f(x)=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
and
(
105
) for
f(x)=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
, because the coefficients with respect to
z
of the
formal series in each case form a basis of the polynomial ring

\Lambda(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.

By Proposition
4.1.4
, in (
104
) for

f=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
,
we have
\Pi_{A,x}\,f=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
on the
left hand side, and also

(106)

\Pi_{A,(x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,f\\
=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[(1-A)X(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})]\,\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,,

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
. Applying
\xi^{-1}
in the
variables
x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n}
to the right hand side of
(
106
) only affects the factor
\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
, as the other factors are independent of
these variables. By definition,
\xi^{-1}\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]=\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{j}]
. Hence,
by (
68
), we have

(107)

(\xi^{-1}\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}])[x_{m+1}+W+(A-1)X,\,x_{m+2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}]\\
=\Omega[(W+(A-1)X)(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})]\,\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,,

and therefore, combining (
106
) and (
107
), we get

(108)

(\xi^{-1}\Pi_{A,(x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,f)[x_{m+1}+W+(A-1)X,\,x_{m+2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}]\\
=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[W(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})]\,\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,.

Applying
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}
, then setting
W=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
, this becomes the desired quantity

(109)

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m})(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})]\,\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}].

On the left hand side of (
105
) for
f=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
, Proposition
4.1.4

gives
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,f=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
. On the right hand side, factoring out

\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
from
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
and proceeding similarly to
(
107
)–(
108
), we obtain

(110)

\displaystyle(\xi^{-1}f)[x_{m+1}+(1

\displaystyle-A)\,W-X,\,x_{m+2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}]

\displaystyle=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[((1-A)W-X)(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})]

\displaystyle=\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j\leq m}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[\sum_{m<i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}]\,\Omega[(1-A)W(z_{m+1}^{-1}+\cdots+z_{n}^{-1})].

Applying
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}^{-1}\,\Pi_{A,(x_{m+1},\ldots,x_{n})}^{-1}
and then setting
W=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
yields the desired quantity
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}x_{i}/z_{j}-A\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/z_{j}]
.
∎

In one variable,
\Pi_{A,x_{n}}
is the identity, and
\xi^{-1}(x_{n}^{k})=h_{k}(X_{n})
. Hence,
(
104
)–(
105
) take the
following simpler form for
m=n-1
.

Corollary 4.5.2
.

(111)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,\big(f(x_{1},\ldots,\,x_{n-1})\,x_{n}^{k}\big)\\
=\bigl(\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})}\,f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})\,h_{k}[x_{n}+W+(A-1)X]\bigr)\bigr|_{W\mapsto X},

(112)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}^{-1}\,\big(f(x_{1},\ldots,\,x_{n-1})\,x_{n}^{k}\big)\\
=\bigl(\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})}^{-1}\,f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})\,h_{k}[x_{n}+(1-A)\,W-X]\bigr)\bigr|_{W\mapsto X},

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}
.

Using (
111
)–(
112
)
recursively often seems to be a good way to compute
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
in practice.

4.6.
Symmetric limit

Although nonsymmetric plethysm does not preserve symmetric
polynomials, there is nevertheless a sense in which, if
f(X)
is a
symmetric function, the nonsymmetric plethysms
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]
and
\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}^{-1}\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]
converge for
large
n
to
f[X/(1-A)]
and
f[(1-A)X]
, respectively. More general
‘factored’ versions of these limits hold for polynomials symmetric in
all but the first
r
and last
s
variables. We will state these
results in a form that gives precise bounds on how closely the
nonsymmetric plethysms approximate the symmetric limits. The bounds
turn out to depend on the level where
f(X)
appears in the following
filtration on symmetric functions.

Definition 4.6.1
.

The
length filtration
is the filtration
{\mathfrak{K}}=V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset\cdots\subseteq\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(X)
of the algebra
of symmetric functions, where

(113)

V_{l}={\mathfrak{K}}\cdot\{s_{\lambda}\mid\ell(\lambda)\leq l\}={\mathfrak{K}}\cdot\{h_{\lambda}\mid\ell(\lambda)\leq l\}

is the subspace spanned by the Schur functions
s_{\lambda}
, or
equivalently by the complete symmetric functions
h_{\lambda}
,
indexed by partitions
\lambda
with at most
l
parts.

In the case of the forward plethysm
\Pi_{A,x}
, the convergence
bounds also depend on the order of approximation in
A
. To express
this, we need an additional definition.

Definition 4.6.2
.

Let
f
and
g
be (Laurent) polynomials or (multi
-
)symmetric functions with coefficients in
\Lambda(A)
. We say that
f

and
g
agree to order
e
in
A
, and write

(114)

f\equiv g\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}},

if all coefficients of
f-g
belong to the ideal
\Lambda(A)_{>e}\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{d>e}\Lambda(A)_{d}
in
\Lambda(A)
spanned by
symmetric functions homogeneous of degree greater than
e
in
A
.
Note that every
f
has a canonical representative
g\equiv f
(mod

\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) such that
g
has coefficients of degree at most

e
in
A
. We say that
f
has a given property (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
)—for instance, that
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is symmetric
(mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
)—if its canonical representative has the
property in question.

Equipped with these definitions, we can now state the theorems. The
proofs will be given at the end of this section.

Theorem 4.6.3
.

Let
f(X)
be a symmetric function, and let
g(x)=g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
and
h(y)=h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
be
polynomials, all with coefficients in
\Lambda(A)
. If
f(X)\in V_{l}
,
\deg(h)\leq d
, and
n\geq r+l+d+e
, then

(115)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\bigr)

is symmetric
(mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
)
in the variables

x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}
, and upon setting
x_{i}=0
for

n-(l+d+e)+1<i\leq n
, it reduces to

(116)

(\Pi_{A,x}\,g(x))\,f\!\left[\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}}{1-A}\right]\,(\Pi_{A,y}\,h(y))\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}}.

Theorem 4.6.4
.

Let
f(X)
,
g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
,
h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
be as in
Theorem
4.6.3
. If
f(X)\in V_{l}
,
\deg(h)\leq d
, and

n\geq r+l+d
, then

(117)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}^{-1}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\bigr)

is symmetric in the variables
x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n-(l+d)}
, and upon
setting
x_{i}=0
for
n-(l+d)<i\leq n
, it reduces to

(118)

(\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,g(x))\,f\left[(1-A)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d)})\right]\,(\Pi_{A,y}^{-1}\,h(y)).

Theorems
4.6.3
and
4.6.4
imply the
following weaker but simpler form of the limiting behavior that was
alluded to at the beginning of this subsection.

Corollary 4.6.5
.

Let
f(X)
be a symmetric function, and let
g(x)=g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
and
h(y)=h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
be
polynomials, all with coefficients in
\Lambda(A)
. Then

(119)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\bigr)\\
=(\Pi_{A,x}\,g(x))\,f[X/(1-A)]\,(\Pi_{A,y}\,h(y)),

(120)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}^{-1}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\bigr)\\
=(\Pi_{A,x}\,g(x))\,f[(1-A)X]\,(\Pi_{A,y}\,h(y)),

where
X=x_{1},x_{2},\dots
is a formal alphabet, and the
limits converge formally (see §
2.6
, (iii)) in
X
,

y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
, and
A
.

Remark 4.6.6
.

Every polynomial
p(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+s})
symmetric in all but the
first
r
and last
s
variables is a linear combination of products

g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{n+s})
. Thus, Theorems
4.6.3
and

4.6.4
and Corollary
4.6.5
in effect
describe the limiting behavior of
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
on all such
polynomials
p(x)
. The use of
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
in place of

x_{n+1},\ldots,x_{n+s}
is merely a convenient way to assign indices
independent of
n
to the last
s
variables.

Example 4.6.7
.

(i) By definition, we have

(121)

\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]=x_{n}+h_{1}[(1-A)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1})].

As asserted by Theorem
4.6.4
for
g=h=1
and

f=h_{1}
, with
l=1
and
d=0
, the right hand side is symmetric in
all but
x_{n}
, and setting
x_{n}=0
gives
f[(1-A)(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1})]
.

(ii) After changing
n
to
n+1
and using variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1}
in place of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1}
, we can rewrite the first
identity in (
77
) as

(122)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1})}^{-1}\,y_{1}=y_{1}-h_{1}(A)\,x_{n}.

The right hand side is again symmetric in all but
x_{n}
, and setting

x_{n}=0
gives
y_{1}
. Since
y_{1}=\Pi^{-1}_{A,y_{1}}(y_{1})
, this
confirms the assertion of Theorem
4.6.4
for
f=g=1
and
h(y_{1})=y_{1}
, with
l=0
and
d=1
.

(iii) Summing the identity (
78
) gives

(123)

\displaystyle\Pi_{A,x}\,h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{1}(A)^{n-i}\,h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}].

Modulo
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
, only the terms in (
123
) for

i\geq n-e
survive. Hence, as asserted by Theorem
4.6.3

for
g=h=1
and
f=h_{1}
, with
l=1
and
d=0
, the right hand side
is symmetric (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-e}
, and
setting
x_{n-e+1}=\ldots=x_{n}=0
gives

(124)

(1+h_{1}(A)+\cdots+h_{1}(A)^{e})h_{1}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-e}]\equiv h_{1}\!\left[\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-e}}{1-A}\right]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}}.

(iv) After changing
n
to
n+1
, we can rewrite (
78
) as

(125)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1})}\,y_{1}=y_{1}+h_{1}(A)\,x_{n}+\cdots+h_{1}(A)^{n}\,x_{1}.

The right hand side is
symmetric (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-e}
, and on
setting
x_{n-e+1}=\ldots=x_{n}=0
, it reduces (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) to
y_{1}
. As
y_{1}=\Pi_{A,y_{1}}y_{1}
, this confirms the assertion of
Theorem
4.6.3
for
f=g=1
and

h(y_{1})=y_{1}
, with
l=0
and
d=1
.

For the proofs of Theorems
4.6.3
and
4.6.4
, we first need
to obtain formulas for the product of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
or
\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
with a symmetric
polynomial.

Lemma 4.6.8
.

For
Y
a formal alphabet and
x
a single variable, we have the
identity

(126)

h_{r}[x+Y]h_{a}[x+Y]=\sum_{j=0}^{a+r}c_{j}\,h_{j}[Y]\,h_{a+r-j}[x+Y],

where
c_{j}=1
for
0\leq j\leq\min(r,a)
,
c_{j}=-1
for
\max(r,a)<j\leq a+r
, and
c_{j}=0
otherwise.

Proof.

Both sides of (
126
) are symmetric in
r
and
a
, so we may
assume that
r\leq a
. The coproduct formula
h_{k}[x+Y]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}x^{i}\,h_{k-i}[Y]
implies the identity

(127)

h_{k}[x+Y]=h_{k}[Y]+x\,h_{k-1}[x+Y].

For
k=j
in
h_{j,b}[x+Y]=h_{j}[x+Y]h_{b}[x+Y]
and for
k=b+1
in
h_{j-1,b+1}[x+Y]
, this gives

(128)

h_{j,b}[x+Y]-h_{j-1,b+1}[x+Y]=h_{j}[Y]\,h_{b}[x+Y]-h_{j-1}[x+Y]\,h_{b+1}[Y],

as the terms
x\,h_{j-1,b}[x+Y]
cancel. Setting
b=a+r-j
in (
128
)
and summing from
j=0
to
j=r
yields (
126
).
∎

Lemma 4.6.9
.

Given
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
,
l>0
, and
f(X)\in V_{l}
, we have

(129)

f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{k}g_{k}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i-1}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}+k\,\varepsilon_{i}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}],

where
g_{k}(X)\in V_{l}
, or
g_{k}(X)\in V_{l-1}
if
a_{i}=0
,
and
g_{k}
depends only on
a_{i}
,
k
, and
f
, independent of
n
,

i
, and
a_{j}
for
j\not=i
.

With different functions
g_{k}(X)
, whose coefficients are now in

\Lambda(A)
, we also have

(130)

f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]\\
=\sum_{k}g_{k}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i-1}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}+k\,\varepsilon_{i}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}],

where
g_{k}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}\,V_{l+d}
—in other
words,
g_{k}(X)\in V_{l+e}

(mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
)
for all

e
—or
g_{k}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}\,V_{l-1+d}
if

a_{i}=0
, and
g_{k}
again depends only on
a_{i}
,
k
, and
f
,
independent of
n
,
i
, and
a_{j}
for
j\not=i
.

Proof.

The second formula implies the first, by specializing
A=0
and noting
that this specialization collapses
\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}\,V_{l+d}
to
V_{l}
.

To prove the second formula, we first consider the case when
l=1
. In
this case, we can assume that
f=h_{m}
. Setting
X_{i-1}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i-1}
and
Y=(1-A)X_{i-1}
, we have

(131)

h_{m}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}]=h_{m}[x_{i}+Y+A\,X_{i-1}]=\sum_{r=0}^{m}h_{r}[x_{i}+Y]\,h_{m-r}[A\,X_{i-1}].

Using Lemma
4.6.8
with
a=a_{i}
,
x=x_{i}
and this
Y
,
(
131
) implies

(132)

h_{m}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]=\\
\sum_{r=0}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{a_{i}+r}c_{r,j}\,h_{j}[(1-A)X_{i-1}]\,h_{m-r}[A\,X_{i-1}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}+(r-j)\,\varepsilon_{i}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}],

where
c_{r,j}
is
c_{j}
in Lemma
4.6.8
for the given
r

and
a=a_{i}
.
This has the form of (
130
), with

(133)

g_{k}(X)=\sum_{r-j=k}c_{r,j}\,h_{j}[(1-A)X]\,h_{m-r}[A\,X]=\sum_{r-j=k}c_{r,j}\sum_{p+q=j}h_{p}(X)\,h_{q}[-A\,X]\,h_{m-r}[A\,X].

Since every symmetric function
h
satisfies
h[A\,X]\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}V_{d}(X)
, we see that
g_{k}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}V_{1+d}
.
For
a=0
in Lemma
4.6.8
, the
only nonzero term in (
126
) is the term for
j=0
. Hence if

a_{i}=0
, we only have the term with
j=0
in (
133
), in which
case
g_{k}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}V_{d}
. It is clear
that the expression in (
133
) only depends on
a_{i}
,
k
,
and
f
, so the result holds for
l=1
.

For
l>1
, we can assume that
f(X)=h_{m}(X)f_{1}(X)
with

f_{1}(X)\in V_{l-1}
, and that the result holds for
f_{1}(X)
by
induction on
l
, with coefficients
(g_{1})_{k}(X)
, say. Then
(
132
) gives the expansion in (
130
)
with

(134)

g_{k}(X)=\sum_{k^{\prime}+r-j=k}c_{r,j}\,h_{j}[(1-A)X]\,h_{m-r}[A\,X]\,(g_{1})_{k^{\prime}}(X).

Given that
(g_{1})_{k^{\prime}}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}V_{l-1+d}

for all
k^{\prime}
, it follows that
g_{k}(X)\in\sum_{d}\Lambda(A)_{\geq d}V_{l+d}
, as desired. If
a_{i}=0
, we have the same implication
with
l-1
in place of
l
. Since the coefficients
(g_{1})_{k^{\prime}}
are
independent of
n
,
i
, and the
a_{j}
for
j\not=i
, so is

g_{k}
.
∎

Note that Lemma
4.6.9
does not hold for
l=0
and

a_{i}=0
, since
f(X)
may be a nonzero constant, but
V_{-1}=0
.
However, the lemma is unnecessary for
l=0
, since the left hand sides
of (
129
) and (
130
) have trivial
expansions if
f(X)
is constant.

Proposition 4.6.10
.

(a) Given
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
,
1\leq i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq n
, and

f(X)\in V_{l}
, suppose that
a_{j}=0
for at least
l
indices

j\in[i_{0},i_{1}]
. Then

(135)

f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i_{1}}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{i_{1}-i_{0}+1}}c_{{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime};{\mathbf{b}};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}],

where
{\mathbf{a}}=({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{0}-1})
,
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}=(a_{i_{0}},\ldots,a_{i_{1}})
, and
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(a_{i_{1}+1}\ldots,a_{n})
, and
c_{{\mathbf{b}}}
depends only on
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}
,

{\mathbf{b}}
, and
f
.

(b) Given
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
,
1\leq i_{0}\leq i_{1}\leq n
, and

f(X)\in V_{l}
, suppose that
a_{j}=0
for at least
l+e
indices

j\in[i_{0},i_{1}]
. Then

(136)

f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{i_{1}}]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]\\
\equiv\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{i_{1}-i_{0}+1}}c_{{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime};{\mathbf{b}};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime})}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}}

where
{\mathbf{a}}=({\mathbf{a}}^{\prime};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime};{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
with
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{0}-1})
,
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}=(a_{i_{0}},\ldots,a_{i_{1}})
, and
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(a_{i_{1}+1}\ldots,a_{n})
, and
c_{{\mathbf{b}}}
depends
(mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
)
only on
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}
,
{\mathbf{b}}
, and
f
.

Proof.

If
l=0
, then
f(X)
is constant, and the sole term on the right hand
side in either formula is for
{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime\prime}
, with
c_{{\mathbf{b}}}
equal to
the constant value of
f
.

For
l>0
, if
i_{0}=i_{1}
, then we must have
l=1
and
a_{i_{1}}=0
, and in part (b) we must have
e=0
. Then
Lemma
4.6.9
gives an expansion with coefficients

g_{k}(X)
that are constant (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>0}
) in part (b), or
just constant, in part (a); the coefficients
c_{{\mathbf{b}}}=c_{(k)}
are
then given by
c_{(k)}=g_{k}
.

If
l>0
and
i_{0}<i_{1}
, then the result follows by induction on

i_{1}
, using Lemma
4.6.9
and the induction
hypothesis for the same
l
if
a_{i}>0
, or for
l-1
if
a_{i}=0
.
∎

Proposition
4.6.10
supplies the essential facts needed
for the proofs of Theorems
4.6.3
and

4.6.4
, to which we now turn.

Proof of Theorem
4.6.3
.

We set
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[X_{1},X_{2}-A\,X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}-A\,X_{k-1}]
, as in (
72
). By linearity, we
can assume that
g(x)={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]
and
h(y)={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
, where
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
. Using the coproduct formula (
69
), we can express
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
in terms of flagged symmetric functions in the full list of
variables
x,y
, as

(137)

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots,y_{s}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{p}}}[(A-1)X,0,\ldots,0]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[(1-A)X+y_{1},y_{2}-A\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}-A\,y_{s-1}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{(0^{n};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}],

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}
, and
h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}
is the complete
symmetric function indexed by the partition
{\mathbf{p}}_{+}
whose parts are
the nonzero entries of
{\mathbf{p}}
. Then

(138)

g(x)\,f[X]\,h(y)=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}].

Since
V_{d}
contains all symmetric
functions of degree
\leq d
, we have
f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\in V_{l+d}
. By Proposition
4.6.10
(b) with

[i_{0},i_{1}]=[n-(l+d+e)+1,n]
, it follows that

(139)

f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]\\
\equiv\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d+e)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}},

where the coefficients
c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\in\Lambda(A)
are independent
of
n
,
{\mathbf{a}}
, and
{\mathbf{q}}
. In particular, by taking
{\mathbf{a}}=0^{r}

and
{\mathbf{q}}=0^{s}
, we conclude that

(140)

f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\equiv\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{(0^{n-(l+d+e)};\,{\mathbf{b}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}},

for all
n\geq l+d+e
. Letting
m=n-(l+d+e)
and setting
x_{m+1}=\ldots=x_{n}=0
, this becomes

(141)

f[Z]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)Z]\equiv\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,h_{{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}[(1-A)Z]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}},

where
Z=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}
. Since
m
can be arbitrarily large,
identity (
141
) holds for
Z
a general formal alphabet.

Now, (
138
) and (
139
) imply

(142)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}\bigl(g(x)\,f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\,h(y)\bigr)\\
\equiv\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d+e)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]\pmod{\Lambda(A)_{>e}}.

The block of zeroes in positions
r+1
through
n-(l+d+e)
of
({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d+e)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})
implies that each term is
symmetric in
x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}
, as claimed.
Furthermore, on setting
x_{j}=0
for
n-(l+d+e)+1<j\leq n
, the
right hand side of (
142
) becomes

(143)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,h_{{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}[X]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[X+y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]

where we now set
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}
. Taking
Z=X/(1-A)
in (
141
), we see that (
143
) is
congruent (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) to

(144)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]f[X/(1-A)]\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[-X]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[X+y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}].

The summation factor here reduces to

(145)

\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{p}}}[-X,0,\ldots,0]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[X+y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}],

so (
144
) is equal to

(146)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\,f\!\left[\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}}{1-A}\right]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}],

which is (
116
) for
g(x)={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]
and
h(y)={\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6.4
.

In essence, this is the same as the preceding proof with the roles of

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}
interchanged. In more detail, we
first assume by linearity that
g(x)={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]
and
h(y)={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
. Specializing

A=0
in (
137
), we have

(147)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[-X]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{(0^{n};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}],

where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}
. The counterparts of
(
138
)–(
139
) are now

(148)

\displaystyle g(x)\,f[X]\,h(y)=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[-X]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}],

(149)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f[X]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[-X]\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots&\,,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]\\
&=\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]\end{aligned}

with integer coefficients
c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}
independent of
n
,
{\mathbf{a}}
,
and
{\mathbf{q}}
. For (
149
), instead of
Proposition
4.6.10
(b), we have used
Proposition
4.6.10
(a), with
[i_{0},i_{1}]=[n-(l+d)+1,n]
. The same argument that gave (
141
) now
yields

(150)

f[Z]\,h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[-Z]=\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,h_{{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}[Z]

for a general formal alphabet
Z
.

From (
148
) and (
149
), we obtain the analog of
(
142
):

(151)

\Pi_{A,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}^{-1}\bigl(g(x)\,f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\,h(y)\bigr)\\
=\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}].

The block of zeroes in
({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})

implies that each term is symmetric in
x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n-(l+d)}
,
as claimed. Note that this is slightly different from the proof of
Theorem
4.6.3
, because
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
is only symmetric in the
variables
x_{i}
corresponding to the positions of the zeroes, while

{\mathfrak{h}}_{({\mathbf{a}};\,0^{n-r-(l+d+e)};\,{\mathbf{b}};\,{\mathbf{q}})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
is also symmetric in the
extra variable corresponding to the first position after the zeroes.

On setting
x_{n-(l+d)+1}=\ldots=x_{n}=0
, the right hand side
of (
151
) becomes

(152)

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l+d+e}}c_{{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{b}}}\,h_{{\mathbf{b}}_{+}}[(1-A)X]\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[X+y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]

where we now set
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d)}
. Taking
Z=(1-A)X

in (
150
) shows that this is equal to

(153)

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]f[(1-A)X]\sum_{{\mathbf{p}}+{\mathbf{q}}={\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}h_{{\mathbf{p}}_{+}}[(A-1)X]\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{q}}}[X+y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}].

As in (
144
), the summation factor reduces to

{\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
, giving
(
118
) for
g(x)={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]

and
h(x)={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}^{\prime}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}]
.
∎

4.7.
One-parameter specialization

For applications to flagged LLT polynomials and Macdonald polynomials,
we need nonsymmetric plethysm operations
\Pi_{t,x}^{\pm 1}
on

{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, where
t\in{\mathfrak{K}}
is an
indeterminate, defined by specializing
A=t
in the operations
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
for generic
A
. To make this precise, for any

{\mathfrak{K}}
algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
, we define an
{\mathfrak{R}}
algebra
homomorphism

(154)

\ev_{t}\colon\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{R}}}(A)={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes_{{\mathfrak{K}}}\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{R}},\qquad\ev_{t}f(A)=f[t]=f(t).

The polynomials

(155)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-t\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-t\,x_{n-1}]=\ev_{t}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]

given by specializing the basis of
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
in (
70
) form a basis
of
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. We define nonsymmetric plethysm
with
A=t
by specializing the definition of
\Pi_{A,x}
.

Definition 4.7.1
.

The nonsymmetric plethysm
\Pi_{t,x}=\Pi_{t,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}

is the invertible
{\mathfrak{K}}
-linear operation on
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
given on the basis in
(
155
) by

(156)

\Pi_{t,x}\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2}-t\,x_{1},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}-t\,x_{n-1}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},\,x_{2},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}].

To draw conclusions about
\Pi_{t,x}
from results already
established for the generic nonsymmetric plethysm
\Pi_{A,x}
, we
need the fact that the evaluation homomorphism
\ev_{t}
intertwines
the two. The next lemma makes this precise.

Lemma 4.7.2
.

We have identities of maps
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\rightarrow{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
:

(157)

\displaystyle\Pi_{t,x}\circ\ev_{t}=\ev_{t}\circ\,\Pi_{A,x},

(158)

\displaystyle\Pi_{t,x}^{-1}\circ\ev_{t}=\ev_{t}\circ\,\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}.

Proof.

We prove (
157
); taking
\Pi_{t,x}^{-1}\circ(-)\circ\Pi_{A,x}^{-1}
on both sides then implies (
158
).
Regarding
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
as a
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)

module via the homomorphism
\ev_{t}\colon\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{K}}
, all maps in question are
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)
-linear. Hence it suffices to check (
157
) on the
elements of a basis of
\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{K}}}(A)[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
.
But (
157
) on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\mathbf{a}}}[x_{1},x_{2}-A\,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-A\,x_{n-1}]
is just the defining formula
(
156
).
∎

Lemma
4.7.2
implies that applying
\ev_{t}
to
essentially any quantity expressed in terms of
A
and the
nonsymmetric plethysm operations
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
has the effect
of changing
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
to
\Pi_{t,x}^{\pm 1}
, as well as
replacing all other occurrences of
A
with
t
.

For most of the results on
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
in §§
4.1
–
4.6
, including
Theorem
4.6.4
, it follows that the same results hold
for
\Pi_{t,x}^{\pm 1}
after replacing
\Lambda(A)
with
{\mathfrak{K}}

and setting
A=t
in all formulas. In particular, the results in §
4.2
involving
\Pi_{A,x}^{\pm 1}
and the inner
product
\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
in (
86
)
specialize to results for
\Pi_{t,x}^{\pm 1}
and the inner product

\langle-,-\rangle_{t}
in (
166
), below.

If the elements of
{\mathfrak{K}}
are polynomials or power series in
t
,
then Theorem
4.6.3
also follows, with the additional
modification that we replace congruence (mod
\Lambda(A)_{>e}
) with
congruence (mod
(t^{e+1})
). For more generality, we can let the
elements of
{\mathfrak{K}}
be formal Laurent series in
t
. This also
allows for
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t,\ldots)\subseteq{\mathfrak{K}}
, by expanding
rational functions as formal Laurent series in
t
. If we assume that
the coefficients of
f(X)
,
g(x)
, and
h(y)
have no poles at
t=0
,
then the Laurent series in question reduce to ordinary power series,
giving the following version of Theorem
4.6.3
.

Theorem 4.7.3
.

Assume that the elements of
{\mathfrak{K}}
are formal Laurent series in

t
. Let
f(X)
be a symmetric function, and let
g(x)=g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
and
h(y)=h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
be
polynomials, all with coefficients in
{\mathfrak{K}}
. Assume that their
coefficients have no poles at
t=0
. If
f(X)\in V_{l}
,
\deg(h)\leq d
, and
n\geq r+l+d+e
, then

(159)

\Pi_{t,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}]\,h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\bigr)

is symmetric
(mod
(t^{e+1})
)
in the variables

x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}
, and upon setting
x_{i}=0
for

n-(l+d+e)+1<i\leq n
, it reduces to

(160)

(\Pi_{t,x}\,g(x))\,f\!\left[\frac{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-(l+d+e)+1}}{1-t}\right]\,(\Pi_{t,y}\,h(y))\pmod{(t^{e+1})},

where
p\equiv q\pmod{(t^{e+1})}
means that the coefficients of

p-q
vanish to order
\geq e+1
at
t=0
.

Corollary
4.6.5
specializes a little more
straightforwardly.

Corollary 4.7.4
.

Assume that
{\mathfrak{K}}
is a ring of formal Laurent series in
t
. Let

f(X)
be a symmetric function, and let
g(x)=g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})

and
h(y)=h(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})
be polynomials, all with
coefficients in
{\mathfrak{K}}
. Setting
A=t
in
(
119
) and (
120
), the
limits converge formally in
X
,
y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}
, and
t
.

Proof.

Since
f(X)
,
g(x)
, and
h(y)
have finitely many coefficients, we
can multiply by a power of
t
to remove any poles at
t=0
. The
corollary now follows from Theorem
4.7.3
and the fact
that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}=p
converges formally in
t

if and only if the same holds with
p_{n}
and
p
multiplied by a
power of
t
.
∎

5.
Preliminaries, continued…

5.1.
Hecke algebras

In §§
5.1
–
5.2
, we review the action of
the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathfrak{S}}_{l})
on Laurent polynomials, as in
Lusztig
[
36
]
or Macdonald
[
39
]
, and define
nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomials. Our treatment here closely
follows that in
[
7
, §§4.2–4.3]
.

The
Demazure-Lusztig
operators

(161)

T_{i}=t\,s_{i}+\frac{1-t}{1-x_{i}/x_{i+1}}\,(s_{i}-1)

for
i=1,\dots,l-1
generate an action of the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathfrak{S}}_{l})
on the ring of Laurent polynomials
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{l}^{\pm 1}]
. Here,
{\mathfrak{K}}
is any coefficient ring and

t\in{\mathfrak{K}}
any invertible element (typically,
{\mathfrak{K}}
will be

{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
or
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
). The generators of
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathfrak{S}}_{l})

satisfy the braid relations
T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}

and
T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}
for
|j-i|>1
, and the quadratic
relations

(162)

(T_{i}-t)(T_{i}+1)=0.

The elements
T_{w}
, defined by
T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}T_{i_{2}}\cdots T_{i_{k}}

for any reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
, form a

{\mathfrak{K}}
-basis of
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathfrak{S}}_{l})
, as
w
ranges over

{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
.

Using an overbar
\overline{\,\vphantom{t}\cdot\,}
to signify
inverting the variables
t
,
x_{i}
, one has
\overline{T_{i}}=T_{i}^{-1}
and hence
\overline{T_{w}}=T_{w^{-1}}^{-1}
.

Remark 5.1.1
.

The version of the Demazure-Lusztig operators
T_{i}
used here agrees
with
[
17
,
21
,
29
]
. In terms of the
notation here, the Demazure-Lusztig operators in
[
6
,
7
]
are
\overline{T_{i}}|_{t^{-1}\mapsto q}
, and those in

[
4
]
are
t\,\overline{T_{i}}
.

From the definition of
T_{i}
, we see that a Laurent polynomial
f(x)=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})
is
s_{i}
invariant—i.e., symmetric in

x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
—if and only if
T_{i}\,f(x)=t\,f(x)
.
For any Young subgroup
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
, it
follows that every Laurent polynomial in the image of the
symmetrization operator

(163)

{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}=\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}T_{w}

is
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
invariant, and that if
f(x)
is
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}

invariant to begin with, then
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\,f(x)=W_{{\mathbf{r}}}(t)\,f(x)
, where
W_{{\mathbf{r}}}(t)=\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}t^{\ell(w)}
.

We say that
f(x)
is
Hecke antisymmetric
in
x_{i}
and

x_{i+1}
if
T_{i}f(x)=-f(x)
. Analogous to the case of ordinary
antisymmetric polynomials, if
f
is Hecke antisymmetric in
x_{i}

and
x_{i+1}
, then
t\,x_{i}-x_{i+1}
divides
f(x)
, and

f(x)/(t\,x_{i}-x_{i+1})
is symmetric. The converse also holds.

5.2.
Nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomials

For each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
, define the
nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomials

E_{\lambda}(x;t)
and
F_{\lambda}(x;t)
by

(164)

\displaystyle E_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})=t^{\ell(w)}\overline{T_{w}}\,x^{\lambda_{+}},

(165)

\displaystyle F_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})=\overline{T_{v}}\,x^{\lambda_{-}},

where
\lambda=w(\lambda_{+})=v(\lambda_{-})
with

\lambda_{+}\in X_{+}
,
\lambda_{-}\in-X_{+}
, and
v
chosen to be
minimal if
\lambda_{-}
has non-trivial stabilizer. The polynomials

E_{\lambda}(x;t)
and
F_{\lambda}(x;t)
here are the same as in
[
7
, §4.3]
with
q
changed to
t
, because formulas
(
164
)–(
165
) are correct for the differing
conventions on the definition of
T_{i}
.

The
F_{\lambda}(x;t)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
form a basis for the
Laurent polynomial ring
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{l}^{\pm 1}]
,
and the subset with
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
(resp.
\lambda\in-{\mathbb{N}}^{l}
)
form a basis for
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}]
(resp.
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{-1},\ldots,x_{l}^{-1}]
).

Define an inner product on
{\mathfrak{K}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{l}^{\pm 1}]
by

(166)

\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle_{t}=\langle x^{0}\rangle\,f(x)\,g(x)\,\Omega[(t-1)\sum_{i<j}x_{i}/x_{j}],

so that
\langle-,-\rangle_{t}
is the
A=t
specialization of

\langle-,-\rangle_{A}
from (
86
).

Lemma 5.2.1
.

(a) The elements
E_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})
and
\overline{F_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})}
are dual bases with respect to
\langle-,-\rangle_{t}
, and (b) The elements
w_{0}\,\overline{E_{\lambda}(x;t)}
and

w_{0}\,F_{\lambda}(x;t)
are also dual bases. In symbols:

(167)

\langle E_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1}),\,\overline{F_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})}\rangle_{t}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}=\langle w_{0}\,\overline{E_{\lambda}(x;t)},\,w_{0}\,F_{\lambda}(x;t)\rangle_{t}.

Proof.

Part (a) is
[
7
, Prop. 4.3.2]
with
q
there changed to

t^{-1}
. Part (b) follows from the identity
\langle w_{0}\,f(\overline{x}),\,w_{0}\,g(\overline{x})\rangle_{t}=\langle f(x),\,g(x)\rangle_{t}
, which is immediate from (
166
).
∎

Lemma 5.2.2
.

The operators
T_{i}
are self-adjoint for the inner product in
(
166
).

Proof.

Equivalent to
[
7
, Lemma 4.3.3]
, using Remark
5.1.1
.
∎

Lemma 5.2.3
.

(a) For all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
and
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(168)

F_{\lambda+(d^{l})}(x;t)=(x_{1}\cdots x_{l})^{d}\,F_{\lambda}(x;t).

(b) If
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{l})
satisfies
\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{l}
for all
i
, then

(169)

F_{\lambda}(x;t)=x_{l}^{\lambda_{l}}\,F_{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{l-1})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l-1};t).

(c) With the same hypothesis as in (b),

(170)

\overline{T_{m}}\cdots\overline{T_{l-1}}\,F_{\lambda}(x;t^{-1})=t^{-e}\,F_{s_{m}\cdots s_{l-1}(\lambda)}(x;t^{-1}),

where
e
is the number of indices
m\leq i<l
such that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{l}
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from the fact that
T_{i}
commutes with multiplication
by any Laurent polynomial symmetric in
x_{i},x_{i+1}
. Part (b)
follows directly from the definition of
F_{\lambda}
. Part (c) follows
directly from the definition of
F_{\lambda}
and the following fact: if

\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}
, then
\overline{T}_{i}F_{\lambda}(x;t)=t^{-1}F_{\lambda}(x;t)
; this follows from
\overline{T}_{i}\overline{T_{v}}\,x^{\lambda_{-}}=\overline{T_{v}}\,\overline{T}_{j}\,x^{\lambda_{-}}=t^{-1}\overline{T_{v}}\,x^{\lambda_{-}}
, where
v
and
\lambda_{-}
are as in
(
165
), and
j=v^{-1}(i)
satisfies
(\lambda_{-})_{j}=(\lambda_{-})_{j+1}
.
∎

5.3.
Leading terms in Bruhat order

We need the concept of leading term of a Laurent polynomial with
respect to Bruhat order on the weight lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
of
\GL_{m}
. We also need to adapt this concept to functions
f(x,Y,z)

that are polynomials in
x=x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
and

z=z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}
and symmetric functions in
Y
.

There is a Bruhat order on the weight lattice of any reductive group

G
. For
G=\GL_{m}
, the Bruhat order on the weight lattice
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
has the following combinatorial description, due to Björner
and Brenti
[
5
, Theorem 6.2]
. For
\mu\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
,
let
S(\mu)=\{(k,i)\in{\mathbb{Z}}\times[m]\mid k\leq\mu_{i}\}
,
where
[m]={1,\ldots,m}
. For any subset
S\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}\times[m]
such that
k
is bounded above for
(k,i)\in S
, define
\varphi_{S}(k,i)
to be the number of elements
(k^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in S
such that

(k^{\prime},i^{\prime})\geq(k,i)
in lexicographic order. Then
\lambda\leq\mu

in Bruhat order on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
if and only if
|\lambda|=|\mu|
and

\varphi_{S(\lambda)}(k,i)\leq\varphi_{S(\lambda)}(k,i)
for all

(k,i)\in{\mathbb{Z}}\times[m]
.

Similar to the case of minimal coset representatives in §
2.2
, every orbit of a Young subgroup
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{m}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
has a unique minimal element in Bruhat
order on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
. Comparing minimal orbit representatives induces
a Bruhat order on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
orbits (comparing maximal elements
induces the same ordering on orbits).

We say that a Laurent polynomial
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
has
leading term

x^{\mu}
in Bruhat order if
x^{\mu}
is greatest in
Bruhat order among all monomials in the support of
f
.

Given a polynomial
f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
symmetric in

x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{r+n}
, let
m=r+n+s
and set
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
, so that
f(x,y,z)
is symmetric in
y
. Then
the coefficients
\langle x^{\eta}\,y^{\nu}\,z^{\kappa}\rangle\,f(x,y,z)=\langle x^{(\eta;\nu;\kappa)}\rangle\,f(x)
are
constant on
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+n]}
orbits of
\GL_{m}
weights
(\eta;\nu;\kappa)
, and the coefficient
\langle x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(y)\,z^{\kappa}\rangle\,f
, where
\ell(\lambda)\leq n
, is
equal to the coefficient
\langle x^{\eta}\,y^{\nu}\,z^{\kappa}\rangle\,f
for any permutation
\nu
of
(\lambda;0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})
. We say that
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(y)\,z^{\kappa}
is the
leading term
of
f(x,y,z)
if the corresponding

{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+n]}
orbit of
\GL_{m}
weights is greatest in
Bruhat order among all orbits in the support of
f
. Given the
symmetry in
y
, this is equivalent to
f
having leading monomial
term
x^{\eta}\,y^{\nu}\,z^{\kappa}
, where
\nu=(0^{n-\ell(\lambda)};\lambda_{-})
, with
\lambda_{-}
the rearrangement of
the parts of
\lambda
in increasing order.

It follows from the Björner–Brenti description that the Bruhat
order on weights
(\eta;\,0^{n-\ell(\lambda)};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)
, for
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and

\lambda
a partition, is independent of
n
as long as
n\geq\ell(\lambda)
. This induces a well-defined ‘stable’ Bruhat order on

{\mathbb{N}}^{r}\times\operatorname{Par}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, where

\operatorname{Par}
is the set of all partitions. We say that

x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)\,z^{\kappa}
is the leading term of

f(x,Y,z)\in{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)\otimes{\mathbbm{k}}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]
if it is the leading term in the stable
Bruhat order, which is equivalent to
f[x,y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},z]

having leading term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(y)\,z^{\kappa}
for
all (or for any)
n\geq\deg_{Y}(f)
.

5.4.
Nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

The
nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

E_{\mu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m};q,t)
for
\GL_{m}
form a basis for the ring of Laurent polynomials

{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{m}^{\pm 1}]
as
\mu
ranges over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
,
where
{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
. Our conventions for
E_{\mu}(x;q,t)
match
those in
[
21
]
, and we mainly follow this reference for
the following brief review of essentials.

The
E_{\mu}(x;q,t)
are characterized uniquely by a triangularity and
orthogonality condition (see, e.g.,
[
21
, §2.1]
). The
former states that

(171)

\displaystyle E_{\mu}(x;q,t)=x^{\mu}+\sum_{\lambda<\mu}c_{\lambda}(q,t)x^{\lambda},

where
<
is the Bruhat order on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
, i.e.,
E_{\mu}(x;q,t)

is monic with leading term
x^{\mu}
in the sense of §
5.3
.

For
\GL_{m}
, the nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials are also
determined by the Knop-Sahi recurrence
[
26
,
47
]
, consisting of the following two identities and the
initial condition
E_{(0^{m})}(x;q,t)=1
. The first is the

intertwiner relation
: for
\mu\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
such that
\mu_{i}>\mu_{i+1}
,

(172)

E_{s_{i}\,\mu}(x;q,t)=\left(T_{i}+\frac{1-t}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}t^{w_{\mu}(i)-w_{\mu}(i+1)}}\right)E_{\mu}(x;q,t),

where
w_{\mu}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{m}
is the minimal permutation such that

w_{\mu}^{-1}(\mu)
is antidominant, i.e., the permutation such that
for
i<j
, we have
w_{\mu}(i)>w_{\mu}(j)
if and only if
\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}
.

The second is the
rotation identity

(173)

E_{\mu_{m}+1,\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m-1}}(x;q,t)=q^{\mu_{m}}\,x_{1}\,E_{\mu}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{m},q^{-1}x_{1};q,t).

We also note that for
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}
, we have
s_{i}E_{\mu}(x;q,t)=E_{\mu}(x;q,t)
.

For any orbit
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\,\mu
of a Young subgroup
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{m}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{m}
, there exists an
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
invariant
{\mathbbm{k}}
-linear combination of nonsymmetric
Macdonald polynomials

(174)

\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\mu}c_{\gamma}\,E_{\gamma}(x;q,t),

unique up to a scalar factor. More precisely, the intertwiner
relation (
172
) implies that such a linear combination
is given by
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\,E_{\mu}(x;q,t)
, where
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}

is the symmetrization operator in (
163
). Still
more precisely, if we take
\mu
to be
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
dominant, let

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}=\Stab_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mu)
, and let
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
denote the set of coset representatives
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
such that
w
is minimal in
w\,{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
, then

(175)

\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}}T_{w}\,E_{\mu}(x;q,t)

is the unique monically normalized version of
(
174
), in which the coefficient of the leading
term
x^{w(\mu)}
is equal to
1
, for all
w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
.

6.
Flagged LLT polynomials

In this section we define and study
flagged LLT polynomials

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
—flagged
symmetric functions (in the sense of §
3
) which generalize both symmetric
LLT polynomials and flagged skew Schur functions. We present
algebraic and combinatorial formulas for flagged LLT polynomials,
taking the algebraic formula as the definition, and deriving the
combinatorial formula from it. We then formulate an atom positivity
conjecture for a natural sub-class of flagged LLT polynomials, which
generalizes the Schur positivity of symmetric LLT polynomials. In §
7
, we will see that this leads to an atom positivity
conjecture for plethystically transformed nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomials, which generalizes the positivity theorem
[
24
]

for symmetric Macdonald polynomials.

6.1.
Indexing data

Flagged LLT polynomials
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
are indexed by pairs
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
in
which
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is given either by a pair of dominant weights for a
Levi subgroup
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}\subseteq\GL_{l}
, or by a tuple of
ragged-right skew diagrams

(Definition
6.1.3
), and
\sigma
is a
permutation satisfying a certain compatibility condition with
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
. We start with the definition in terms of weights.

Definition 6.1.1
.

Flagged LLT indexing data

{\mathbf{r}}
,
\gamma
,
\eta
,
\sigma

of length
l
consist of a strict composition
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
of
l
, a pair of
\GL_{l}
weights
\gamma,\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{l}
, and a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
,
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

\gamma_{i}\leq\eta_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,l
;

(ii)

\gamma\in X_{++}(\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}})
and
\eta\in X_{+}(\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}})
are
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
dominant, and
\gamma
is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}

regular;

(iii)

\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
, where
\eta_{-}\in-X_{+}(\GL_{l})
is the
\GL_{l}
antidominant weight in

{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}\,\eta
;

(iv)

\sigma
is maximal in its coset
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\,\sigma
.

We say that a permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
is
compatible

with the other data
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
when (iii) and (iv) hold
(this does not depend on
\gamma
).

Spelling things out more, condition (ii) says that for each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block
\{i,\ldots,j\}
, we have
\eta_{i}\geq\cdots\geq\eta_{j}
and
\gamma_{i}>\cdots>\gamma_{j}
, condition (iii) is that

\eta_{-}=\sigma^{-1}(\eta)
is weakly increasing, and condition
(iv) is that
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\cdots>\sigma^{-1}(j)
on each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block.

Since
\eta
is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
dominant, its stabilizer in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
is a Young subgroup
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}=\Stab_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\eta)\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
. The condition
\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
only depends on the coset
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\,\sigma
.
Since this condition means that
\sigma^{-1}
sorts
\eta
into
weakly increasing order, we automatically have
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(i+1)
if
\eta_{i}>\eta_{i+1}
. Condition (iv) then says that
for
i
,
i+1
in the same
{\mathbf{r}}
-block, we also have
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(i+1)
if
\eta_{i}=\eta_{i+1}
. Thus, given
condition (iii), condition (iv) is equivalent to
\sigma
being
maximal in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\,\sigma
. In other words, the compatible
permutations
\sigma
are the maximal elements of the cosets
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\,\sigma
whose elements satisfy
\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
.

Since
\eta_{-}
is antidominant, there is a unique minimal
\sigma
such that
\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
. From the preceding, we see
that this minimal
\sigma
is compatible with
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)

if and only if
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
is trivial, i.e., if and only if
\eta
(like
\gamma
) is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
regular.

Definition 6.1.2
.

Given
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
satisfying conditions (i) and (ii) in
Definition
6.1.1
, and assuming further that both

\gamma
and
\eta
are
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
regular, the
standard
compatible permutation

\sigma
is the minimal
\sigma
such that

\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
.

For combinatorial purposes, we need to represent
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
by means of a tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of diagrams somewhat more general
than ordinary skew diagrams. For every positive integer
r
, fix the
distinguished minimal dominant regular
\GL_{r}
weight

(176)

\rho_{r}=-(0,1,\ldots,r-1).

Definition 6.1.3
.

For
\alpha,\,\beta+\rho_{r}\in X_{+}(\GL_{r})
satisfying

\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}
for all
i
, the diagram
\beta/\alpha

(as defined in §
2.7
, i.e., row
j
consists of boxes
with northeast corner
(i,j)
for
\alpha_{j}<i\leq\beta_{j}
)
is a
ragged-right skew diagram
.

If
\beta\in X_{+}(\GL_{r})
, the definition reduces to that of an
ordinary skew diagram. The weaker condition
\beta+\rho_{r}\in X_{+}(\GL_{r})
means that
\beta_{j+1}\leq\beta_{j}+1
for
1\leq j<r
. Thus, in a ragged-right skew diagram, the right end of a row
is allowed to overhang the row below by at most one box, as in the
example shown here.

(177)

\beta/\alpha=(7,8,5,5,6,7)/(3,3,1,0,0,0)\qquad\hbox to68.69pt{\vbox to51.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
