Our convention is that empty rows matter, i.e., length zero rows

\beta_{j}/\alpha_{j}
with
\alpha_{j}=\beta_{j}
count as part
of the diagram
\beta/\alpha
. The reason for this is that the
boxes with northeast corner
(\alpha_{j},j)
and
(\beta_{j}+1,\,j)
to the left and right of each row
\beta_{j}/\alpha_{j}
, empty
rows included, play a role in the combinatorial formula for flagged
LLT polynomials. We define the
end content
of row
j
in a
ragged-right skew diagram to be the content
\beta_{j}+1-j
of
the box to its right. By our choice of
\rho_{r}
, the sequence of
end contents is
\beta+\rho_{r}
. The example in
(
177
) has end contents

(178)

\beta+\rho_{6}=(7,8,5,5,6,7)-(0,1,2,3,4,5)=(7,7,3,2,2,2).

Note that runs of equal end contents correspond to runs of overhanging
rows in the diagram.

Given
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
as in Definition
6.1.1
,
set

(179)

\displaystyle\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}=(\rho_{r_{1}};\ldots;\rho_{r_{k}})=-(0,1,\ldots,r_{1}-1,\,0,1,\ldots,r_{2}-1,\ldots,0,1,\ldots,r_{k}-1)

(180)

\displaystyle\alpha=\gamma-\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}},\quad\beta=\eta-\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}.

Decomposing
\alpha,\beta
as concatenations

(181)

\alpha=(\alpha^{(1)};\ldots;\alpha^{(k)}),\quad\beta=(\beta^{(1)};\ldots;\beta^{(k)})

of subsequences indexed by
{\mathbf{r}}
-blocks, conditions (i) and (ii) in
Definition
6.1.1
say that
\nu^{(i)}=\beta^{(i)}/\alpha^{(i)}
is a ragged-right skew diagram with
r_{i}
rows
for each
i
. Thus, to specify
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
of length
l
, it is
equivalent to specify the tuple
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\ldots,\nu^{(k)})
of ragged-right skew diagrams. The only constraints on

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
are that each
\nu^{(i)}
has at least one row (including
empty rows), and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
has
l
rows altogether.

Definition 6.1.4
.

The tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
constructed above
is the
diagram
of the corresponding weight data
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
satisfying (i), (ii) in Definition
6.1.1

In connection with this definition, we use the following conventions
and terminology.

•

We regard the weight data
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
and the
diagram
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
as interchangeable. In particular, we refer to
both
({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
and
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
as
flagged LLT indexing data
, and say that
\sigma
is compatible
either with
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
or with
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
.

•

As in §
2.8
, we identify
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with the
disjoint union of its components, i.e., a box in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
means a
box in a specific component
\nu^{(j)}
. The total number of boxes
in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is denoted

|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}|\,\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\,|\nu^{(1)}|+\cdots+|\nu^{(k)}|=|\beta|-|\alpha|=|\eta|-|\gamma|.

•

By
rows of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
we mean all rows in all components

\nu^{(i)}
, numbered consecutively from
1
to
l
. Thus, row
j

of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
means row
m
of
\nu^{(i)}
, where
j
is the
m
-th
element of the
i
-th
{\mathbf{r}}
-block. In terms of
\alpha
and
\beta
as defined in (
180
), row
j
of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is
\beta_{j}/\alpha_{j}
.

We also need the following ordering on boxes in any tuple of diagrams.

Definition 6.1.5
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\ldots,\nu^{(k)})
be a tuple of arbitrary
diagrams
\nu^{(i)}=\beta^{(i)}/\alpha^{(i)}
with a total of
l

rows. The
content/row reading order
on boxes in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, or
in any extension of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with additional boxes in each row, is
the lexicographic ordering by (content, row number), where all rows of

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
are numbered consecutively from
1
to
l
.

Figure
1
shows an example of the correspondence
between
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
and its diagram
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
. We
arrange the components
\nu^{(i)}
with
i
increasing from bottom to
top, in keeping with our ‘French’ convention (§
2.7
) on
row indices within each diagram.

\nu^{(1)}=\beta^{(1)}/\alpha^{(1)}=(4,4,2)/(2,0,0)
\nu^{(2)}=\beta^{(2)}/\alpha^{(2)}=(5,6,5,6)/(2,1,1,0)
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{7}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{6}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{5}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{4}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{3}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{2}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle=(3,4)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\alpha+\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}=(2,0,0,2,1,1,0)-(0,1,2,0,1,2,3)=(2,-1,-2,2,0,-1,-3)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\beta+\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}=(4,4,2,5,6,5,6)-(0,1,2,0,1,2,3)=(4,3,0,5,5,3,3)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=(3,7,2,6,1,5,4)

w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}=(3,5,7,1,2,4,6)\ ,\quad\sigma^{-1}(\eta)=(0,3,3,3,4,5,5)

Figure 1.
Flagged LLT indexing data
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
of length
l=7
and the corresponding

({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
, where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)},\nu^{(2)})
is
the diagram of
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
, and
\sigma
is encoded by
writing the flag number
w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}(i)=l+1-\sigma^{-1}(i)

in the flag box in row
i
. Since
\sigma^{-1}(\eta)=\eta_{-}

and each
{\mathbf{r}}
-block of
w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}
is increasing,
\sigma

is compatible.

We incorporate the permutation
\sigma
into the diagram as follows.

Definition 6.1.6
.

Given a tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, the boxes
immediately to the right of each row, with content equal to the end
content of that row, are
flag boxes
. To represent a permutation

\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
on a tuple of diagrams with a total of
l

rows, we label the flag box in row
j
with the
flag number

w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}(j)=l+1-\sigma^{-1}(j)
. In other words,
since row
\sigma(i)
gets flag number
l+1-i
, the sequence
\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(l)
lists the row indices in decreasing order of
flag number.

Figure
1
illustrates this. Compatibility of

\sigma
and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is expressed in terms of flag numbers by the
following lemma.

Lemma 6.1.7
.

(a) A permutation
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
is compatible with
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
if and only if the corresponding flag numbers decrease in flag boxes
of increasing content, and increase from bottom to top in flag boxes
of equal content in the same component
\nu^{(i)}
.

(b) The weight
\eta
in the indexing data is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
regular
if and only if the corresponding tuple consists of ordinary
(non-ragged-right) skew diagrams. In this case, the standard
compatible permutation corresponds to flag numbers decreasing in the
content/row reading order.

Proof.

Exercise, using the fact that
\eta=\beta+\rho_{{\mathbf{r}}}
is the
sequence of end contents of the rows in the corresponding tuple of
diagrams.
∎

Remark 6.1.8
.

(i) Part (a) implies that the flag numbers increase as a function of
the row index within each component
\nu^{(i)}
.

(ii) The lemma gives a pictorial way to understand the observation
preceding Definition
6.1.2
that the minimal

\sigma
such that
\eta=\sigma(\eta_{-})
is only compatible
with
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
when
\eta
is
\GL_{{\mathbf{r}}}
regular. Namely, if

\eta
is not regular, then some
\nu^{(i)}
is not an ordinary skew
diagram, hence it has at least two rows with flag boxes of equal
content. Flag numbers corresponding to a compatible permutation must
increase in these rows, hence they are not decreasing in the
content/row reading order.

6.2.
Triples

Before defining
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
, we need the notion of

triples
for tuples of diagrams. Although this will mainly come
into play when we prove the combinatorial formula for
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
in §
6.5
, it turns out that the algebraic
definition of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
in §
6.3

must depend on the number of triples of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
in order for the
algebraic and combinatorial formulas to match.

For the purposes of the next definition, we allow
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
to be any
tuple of diagrams as defined in §
2.7
. Adjoining the
boxes to the left and right of a row
\beta^{(j)}_{r}/\alpha^{(j)}_{r}
gives an
extended row

(\beta^{(j)}_{r}+1)/(\alpha^{(j)}_{r}-1)
. This includes the case of the two boxes to the left
and right of an empty row with
\alpha^{(j)}_{r}=\beta^{(j)}_{r}
.

Definition 6.2.1
.

A
triple of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
consists of three boxes
x,y,z
such that

(i)

x
and
z
are adjacent boxes in an extended row
(\beta^{(j)}_{r}+1)/(\alpha^{(j)}_{r}-1)
of some component
\nu^{(j)}
,
with
x
left of
z
;

(ii)

y
is a box in
\nu^{(i)}
for some
i<j
;

(iii)

the contents of these boxes satisfy
c(y)=c(z)

(
=c(x)+1)
.

The number of triples of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is denoted
h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
.

This definition is similar to
[
6
, Definition 5.5.1]
, but
here we do not need triples twisted by a permutation.

We also need the number of certain special triples for flagged LLT
indexing data
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
.

Definition 6.2.2
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
be a tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams,
\sigma
a
compatible permutation, encoded by flag numbers in the flag boxes, as
in Definition
6.1.6
, and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
the diagram
consisting of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
together with the flag boxes. A
rising
end triple
is a triple
(x,y,z)
of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
in which
y
and
z

are flag boxes, and the flag numbers
p
in
y
and
q
in
z
satisfy

p<q
.
The number of rising end triples is denoted
e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
.

Alternatively, taking
i,j
to index the rows containing
y,z
in a
rising end triple, we see that
e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
is the number of
pairs
i<j
such that rows
i
and
j
are in different components of

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, their end contents are equal, and
\sigma^{-1}(i)>\sigma^{-1}(j)
.

6.3.
Algebraic definition

From here through the end of §
6
, all
(multi
-
)symmetric functions and (Laurent) polynomials have
coefficients in the field
{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(t)
.

Definition 6.3.1
.

The
flagged LLT polynomial
corresponding to flagged LLT indexing
data
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
of length
l
is the multi-symmetric
function

(182)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)=t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})+e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)}\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}\langle F_{w(\gamma)}(z;\,t^{-1})\rangle\,\overline{T_{\sigma}}\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{w_{0}(j)}]\,z^{\eta_{-}},

where
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
are the weight data with diagram
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
,
w_{0}
is the maximal permutation
w_{0}(j)=l+1-j
in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
,

\overline{T_{\sigma}}
and
F_{\kappa}
are as in §§
5.1
–
5.2
, and
h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})
and

e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
are as just defined in §
6.2
.

The operator
\overline{T_{\sigma}}
in (
182
) acts on
the variables
{\mathbf{z}}=z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}
, coefficient-wise on the
formal series
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{w_{0}(j)}]\,z^{\eta_{-}}
, which is multi-symmetric in
X_{1},\ldots,X_{l}
with
coefficients in
{\mathbbm{k}}[{\mathbf{z}}^{\pm 1}]
. Since
\overline{T_{\sigma}}

is homogeneous of degree zero, only the homogeneous component of

\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{w_{0}(j)}]
of degree
-|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}|
in
z
and degree
|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}|
in
X
contributes to the
coefficient of
F_{w(\gamma)}(z;\,t^{-1})
. By
(
67
), the coefficients of
\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{w_{0}(j)}]
with respect to
z
belong to the space of
flagged symmetric functions
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l})
,
giving the following result.

Proposition 6.3.2
.

The flagged LLT polynomial
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
is a flagged symmetric function,
homogeneous of total degree
|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}|
in the formal alphabets
X_{i}
.

The definition implies that
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
has coefficients in the subring
{\mathbb{Z}}[t^{\pm 1}]\subseteq{\mathbbm{k}}
. The combinatorial formula in the next
subsection further implies that its coefficients with respect to the
basis of monomial multi-symmetric functions are in
{\mathbb{N}}[t]
—see
Remark
6.4.11
.

Orthogonality of nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomials yields an
equivalent alternative version of (
182
).

Proposition 6.3.3
.

In terms of the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle_{t}
in
(
166
), we have

(183)

t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})+e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t^{-1})=\\
\bigl\langle\,z^{\eta_{+}}\,T_{w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,w_{0}}\,w_{0}\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}\,\overline{E_{w(\gamma)}(z;\,t)},\,\Omega[\sum_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{j}]\,\bigr\rangle_{t}.

Proof.

After making a change of variables
t\mapsto t^{-1}
,

z_{i}\rightarrow z_{w_{0}(i)}
in (
182
), this follows
from
\eta_{+}=w_{0}(\eta_{-})
and the properties that
w_{0}\,F_{\lambda}(z;t)
and
w_{0}\,\overline{E_{\lambda}(z;t)}
are
dual bases (Lemma
5.2.1
(b)), that
T_{i}
is
self-adjoint (Lemma
5.2.2
), and that
w_{0}\,\overline{T_{i}}\,w_{0}\,|_{t\rightarrow t^{-1}}=T_{l-i}
,
which is immediate from the definition (
161
) of
T_{i}
, and
implies that the adjoint of
w_{0}\,\overline{T_{\sigma}}\,w_{0}\,|_{t\rightarrow t^{-1}}
is
T_{w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,w_{0}}
.
∎

Since
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
is a flagged
symmetric function, it is determined by its specialization
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l};\,t]
to single-variable
alphabets
X_{i}=x_{i}
, using Lemma
3.1.5
.
Propositions
4.2.2
and

6.3.3
yield the following formula for this
specialization in terms of nonsymmetric plethysm
\Pi_{t,x}
.

Corollary 6.3.4
.

The specialization of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
to single-variable alphabets is given by

(184)

t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})+e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l};\,t^{-1}]=\Pi_{t,x}\,x^{\eta_{+}}\,T_{w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,w_{0}}\,w_{0}\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}\,\overline{E_{w(\gamma)}(x;\,t)},

where the Hecke algebra operator
T_{w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,w_{0}}

acts on
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{l}^{\pm 1}]
. Equivalently, by
Proposition
4.3.5
,

(185)

t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})+e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l};\,t^{-1}]=\Bigl(\frac{x^{\eta_{+}}\,T_{w_{0}\,\sigma^{-1}\,w_{0}}\,w_{0}\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}\,\overline{E_{w(\gamma)}(x;\,t)}}{\prod_{i<j}(1-t\,x_{i}/x_{j})}\Bigr)_{\pol}.

Remark 6.3.5
.

The functions
w_{0}\,\overline{E_{\lambda}(x;t)}
that appear in
(
183
)–(
185
) are also given
by
w_{0}\,\overline{E_{\lambda}(x;t)}=E_{-w_{0}(\lambda)}(x;t^{-1})

As an important special case, when
{\mathbf{r}}=(1^{l})
and all the end
contents of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
are equal, we have
\rho_{\mathbf{r}}=(0^{l})
, every
permutation
\sigma
is compatible, the standard compatible
permutation is the identity, and the flagged LLT polynomial
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
becomes essentially the nonsymmetric plethysm

\Pi_{t,x}
applied to a nonsymmetric Hall-Littlewood polynomial.

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=((d/\alpha_{1}),\ldots,(d/\alpha_{l}))
be a tuple of single-row diagrams
with equal end contents
d
(for any integer
d
). Set

\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{l})
where
\lambda_{i}=d-\alpha_{i}
. Then, for any
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}
,

(186)

t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})+\inv(\sigma)}\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l};t^{-1}]=\Pi_{t,x}\,T_{w_{0}\sigma^{-1}w_{0}}\,E_{w_{0}(\lambda)}(x;t^{-1})\,.

In particular, if
\sigma
is the identity, then

(187)

t^{h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})}\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{l};t^{-1}]=\Pi_{t,x}\,E_{w_{0}(\lambda)}(x;t^{-1})\,.

Proof.

Apply Corollary
6.3.4
with
\beta=\eta=(d^{l})

and
\alpha=\gamma=(d-\lambda_{1},\ldots,d-\lambda_{l})
and use
Remark
6.3.5
. By definition,
e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)=\inv(\sigma)
when each shape has a single flag box and all the flag
boxes have the same content. Equation (
186
) then
follows.
∎

We also note that the flagged LLT polynomial indexed by any tuple of
ragged-right skew diagrams can be expressed as a signed sum of flagged
LLT polynomials indexed by tuples of single-row diagrams.

Corollary 6.3.7
.

Given flagged LLT indexing data

({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
of length
l
with diagram
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, let

G_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};t)=t^{-h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})-e({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};t)
. Then we have

(188)

G_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};t)=\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathbf{r}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}G_{\eta/w(\gamma),\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};t)\,,

where
\eta/w(\gamma)
designates the tuple of
single-row diagrams
(\eta_{i}/w(\gamma)_{i})
if
w(\gamma)_{i}\leq\eta_{i}
for all
i
, and otherwise we take the term
indexed by
w
to be zero.

Proof.

The compatibility of
\sigma
with
({\mathbf{r}},\gamma,\eta)
implies
that
\sigma
is also compatible with
((1^{l}),w(\gamma),\eta)
,
giving valid LLT indexing data
((1^{l}),w(\gamma),\eta,\sigma)

whenever
w(\gamma)_{i}\leq\eta_{i}
for all
i
. For the data

((1^{l}),w(\gamma),\eta,\sigma)
, the sole term in
(
182
) yields

(189)

G_{\eta/w(\gamma),\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};t)=\langle F_{w(\gamma)}(z;t^{-1})\rangle\,\overline{T_{\sigma}}\,\Omega\left[\sum\nolimits_{i\leq j}X_{i}/z_{w_{0}(j)}\right]z^{\eta_{-}}\,.

Moreover, if we do not have
w(\gamma)_{i}\leq\eta_{i}
for all

i
, then the right hand side of (
189
) is equal to
zero. The result now follows by substituting (
189
)
into Definition
6.3.1
for
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}
.
∎

6.4.
Combinatorial formula

Theorem
6.4.9
, below, gives a
combinatorial formula for
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
with the formal alphabets
X_{i}

specialized to (signed) sums of variables indexed by subsets of a
(signed) alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
. Like (
24
),
(
25
) for symmetric LLT polynomials, our
formula is a weighted sum over tableaux—in this case, over
flagged tableaux
. Before stating the theorem, we define these
tableaux.

Definition 6.4.1
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
be flagged LLT indexing data, interpreted as a
tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with
\sigma
encoded
by flag numbers in the flag boxes (Definition
6.1.6
).
A box
x
in a component
\nu^{(j)}
of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is
i
-admissible
if

(i)

i
is less than or equal to the flag number in the row
containing
x
, and

(ii)

i
is greater than the flag number in any flag box below

x
in the column of
\nu^{(j)}
containing
x

(condition (ii) holds vacuously if
\nu^{(j)}
is an ordinary skew
diagram).

Figure
2
illustrates
i
-admissible regions.
The following lemma may help clarify the picture.

Lemma 6.4.2
.

Given a component
\nu^{(j)}
of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, let
\theta\subseteq\nu^{(j)}
be the subdiagram consisting of rows with flag number
greater than or equal to
i
. The set of
i
-admissible boxes in
\nu^{(j)}
is the largest ordinary skew diagram contained in
\theta
,
i.e., the skew diagram obtained by deleting all boxes of
\theta

that overhang the end of a lower row in
\theta
.

Proof.

If the flag number in every row of
\nu^{(j)}
is greater than
i
,
then
\theta
is empty, and there are no
i
-admissible boxes in
\nu^{(j)}
. Otherwise, let row
r
be the first row of
\nu^{(j)}

which contains a flag number greater than or equal to
i
. Then, by
Remark
6.1.8
(i),
\theta
consists of rows
r
and
above. In particular,
\theta
is a ragged-right skew diagram. It
is straightforward to see that every ragged-right skew diagram
contains a largest ordinary skew diagram, obtained by deleting all
overhanging boxes.

By construction, a box
x
in
\nu^{(j)}
satisfies condition (i) if
and only if
x\in\theta
, and
x
satisfies condition (ii) if and
only if no flag box in a row of
\theta
is below
x
in the same
column. Thus, the
i
-admissible boxes in
\nu^{(j)}
are the boxes
in
\theta
that do not overhang any flag box in
\theta
.
∎

\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{7}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{6}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{5}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{4}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{3}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{2}
\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{1}
[5,6]
\leq 6
\leq 4
\leq 2
2
1
\leq 7
\leq 5
\leq 2

Figure 2.
Admissible regions for the indexing
data
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
in Fig.
1
. The

i
-admissible region is where
i
belongs to the indicated range of
values.

Definition 6.4.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
be flagged LLT indexing data of length
l
. Let

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}<\cdots<{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
be subsets of a signed alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
, where
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}<{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
means
a<b
for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
,
b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
. A
flagged tableau

T
on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}

is a filling
T\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow\bigcup_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
such
that

(i)

every row of
T
is weakly increasing with no repeated
negative letter;

(ii)

within each component
\nu^{(j)}
, every column of
T

is weakly increasing with no repeated positive letter;

(iii)

if
T(x)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, the box
x
is
i
-admissible.

For brevity, we denote the set of flagged tableaux on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
by

\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})
, although it also depends on the
subsets
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
. We also use the term
flagged semistandard
tableau
for a flagged tableau with entries in an alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}
with only positive letters.

Remark 6.4.4
.

If
x\in\nu^{(j)}
, and the flag box
v
in some row of

\nu^{(j)}
is above
x
in the same column, then the flag numbers

p
in the row of
x
and
q
in
v
satisfy
p<q
. Hence, by
condition (iii), if
T(x)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, then
i\leq p<q
. If
y\in\nu^{(j)}
is above
v
in the same column, then
T(y)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}>q
, hence
T(x)<T(y)
. Thus, given that
T
satisfies
conditions (i) and (iii), condition (ii) reduces to the condition that

T
is weakly increasing with no repeated positive letters on each
partial column of
\nu^{(j)}
consisting of contiguous boxes, not
separated by a flag box.

The following alternative point of view on the definition of flagged
tableaux is often useful. Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
contains letters

b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}
, or adjoin them if needed, ordered so that

(190)

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\preccurlyeq b_{1}\prec{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\preccurlyeq b_{2}\prec\cdots\preccurlyeq b_{l-1}\prec{\mathcal{A}}_{l}\preccurlyeq b_{l}

in the notation of (
23
). More precisely, we require that

(191)

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{j}\preccurlyeq b_{j}\prec{\mathcal{A}}_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{l}.

for all
j
(this formulation is unambiguous if some of the
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
are empty). In particular, these conditions hold if
b_{j}
is
positive and is the largest letter in
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
, of if
b_{j}
is negative and is the smallest letter in
{\mathcal{A}}_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
be the extended tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams
consisting of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
together with the flag box in each row, and
for any filling
T\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow\bigcup_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
,
define
T^{\prime}\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
by

(192)

T^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}T(x)&\text{if $x\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}$}\\
b_{j}&\text{if $x$ is the flag box containing flag number $j$}.\end{cases}

By construction, for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, we have
a\preccurlyeq b_{j}
if
and only if
i\leq j
, and
a\succ b_{j}
if and only if
i>j
.
Using this and Remark
6.4.4
, we get the following
characterization of flagged tableaux in terms of the extended tableau

T^{\prime}
.

Lemma 6.4.5
.

A filling
T\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow\bigcup_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
is a
flagged tableaux if and only if
T^{\prime}
is a ragged-right super
tableau on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
, meaning that for adjacent boxes
x,y
in the
same row of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
, with
x
left of
y
, we have
T^{\prime}(x)\preccurlyeq T^{\prime}(y)
, and for adjacent boxes
x,y
in the same column of any
component
\nu^{\prime}\,{}^{(j)}
, with
x
below
y
and
y
not a flag box,
we have
T^{\prime}(x)\prec T^{\prime}(y)
.

Remark 6.4.6
.

(i) In certain contexts (e.g.,
Corollary
6.4.10
,
Definition
6.7.8
, and
Proposition
6.7.9
) the letters
b_{j}
in
(
190
)–(
192
) will be the flag bounds

b_{j}
in the flag bound specialization
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l};t]
of a flagged LLT polynomial. We call the
letters
b_{j}
in the general setting
flag bounds
as well.

(ii) If
{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1}=\emptyset
, the conditions in
(
191
) do not force
b_{i}\prec b_{i+1}
. For
compatibility with flag bound specializations, we in fact want to
allow positive letters
b_{i}=b_{i+1}
in this case.

(iii) If
b_{i}\prec b_{i+1}
for all
i
, then
Remark
6.4.4
implies that the tableau
T^{\prime}
in
Lemma
6.4.5
has the stronger property that within each
component of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
, its full, possibly non-contiguous, columns
are weakly increasing with no repeated positive letters. However, if

{\mathcal{A}}_{i+1}=\emptyset
and we do not have
b_{i}\prec b_{i+1}
,
it is still possible to have
v
above
u
with
T^{\prime}(u)=b_{i}
,

T^{\prime}(v)=b_{i+1}
, for instance, if
v
is the flag box with flag
number
i+1
, and
u
is either the flag box with flag number
i
, or
an
i
-admissible box with
T(u)=b_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.

Example 6.4.7
.

Consider flagged LLT data with
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\nu^{(1)})
consisting of a single ordinary Young diagram
\nu^{(1)}=(2,1)
, and
\sigma=(2,1)
the unique compatible
permutation. The
i
-admissible regions are illustrated below, as in
Figure
2
, with flag numbers in the flag boxes to
the right of each row.

(193)

\vphantom{1}{}^{\#}\!\mathsf{1}

\leq 2

We list the tableaux in
\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})
for positive alphabets
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\{1,\ldots,b_{1}\}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}=\{b_{1}+1,\ldots,b_{2}\}
, for
the two choices of flag bounds
(b_{1},b_{2})=(1,3)
and

(b_{1},b_{2})=(2,3)
. We illustrate them by displaying the extended
tableaux
T^{\prime}
with flag bound
b_{i}
written in the flag box with flag

i
.

We now adapt the notions of attacking pairs and attacking inversions
(§
2.8
) to the flagged setting. A new feature is
that the second box in an attacking pair can be a flag box.

Definition 6.4.8
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
be a tuple of ragged-right skew diagrams, and let

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime}
be the extended tuple consisting of
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
together
with the flag boxes. An
attacking pair
for
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
consists
of boxes
x\in\nu^{(i)}
and
y\in\nu^{\prime}\,{}^{(j)}
such that either

i<j
and
c(y)=c(x)
, or
i>j
and
c(y)=c(x)+1
.

An
attacking inversion
in a flagged tableau
T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})
is an attacking pair
(x,y)
such that
T^{\prime}(x)\succ T^{\prime}(y)
in the notation of (
23
), where
T^{\prime}
is
the extended tableau in (
192
). More explicitly,

(x,y)
is an attacking inversion if
x,y\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
T(x)\succ T(y)
, or if
y
is the flag box with flag number
p
, and

T(x)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{s}
for
s>p
. The number of attacking inversions in

T
is denoted
\inv(T)
.

Theorem 6.4.9
.

Given a signed alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
and subsets
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}<\cdots<{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
, define
X_{i}^{\mathcal{A}}=\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{+}}\,x_{a}-\sum_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{i}^{-}}\,x_{b}
. Then, on specializing the
formal alphabets
X_{i}
to
X^{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, we have

(194)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[X^{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\ldots,X^{\mathcal{A}}_{l};\,t]=\sum_{T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})}(-1)^{m(T)}t^{\inv(T)}x^{T},

where
x^{T}=\prod_{u\in{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}x_{T(u)}
,
\inv(T)
is as in
Definition
6.4.8
, and
m(T)=|T^{-1}({\mathcal{A}}^{-})|
is
the number of negative entries in
T
.

We prove Theorem
6.4.9
in §
6.5
. For now, we note some of its consequences. To start,
we can deduce a combinatorial formula for every flag bound
specialization of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
. Given flag bounds
b_{1}\leq\cdots\leq b_{l}\leq n
, we write

{\mathcal{A}}_{+}^{b}
for the positive alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}=\{1<2<\cdots<n\}
, with distinguished subsets

(195)

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\{1,2,\dots,b_{1}\},\,{\mathcal{A}}_{2}=\{b_{1}+1,\dots,b_{2}\},\,\ldots,\,{\mathcal{A}}_{l}=\{b_{l-1}+1,\dots,b_{l}\}\,,

so that the
X^{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
in Theorem
6.4.9

for
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{+}^{b}
become the
X_{i}^{b}
from Definition

3.1.2
. The set of flagged tableaux on these alphabets
is then denoted
\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}}_{+}^{b})
. Explicitly, a
tableau
T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}}_{+}^{b})
is a filling

T\colon{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that the extended
filling
T^{\prime}
with fixed entry
b_{i}
in the flag box with flag number

i
is a ragged-right semistandard tableau, as in
Example
6.4.7
.

Corollary 6.4.10
.

The specialization of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
with flag bounds
b_{1}\leq\cdots\leq b_{l}
, as in
Definition
3.1.2
, is given by

(196)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[X_{1}^{b},\ldots,X_{l}^{b};\,t]=\sum_{T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,\mathcal{A}_{+}^{b})}t^{\inv(T)}x^{T}\,.

Remark 6.4.11
.

Corollary
6.4.10
shows that the polynomial

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[X^{b}_{1},\ldots,X^{b}_{l};\,t]
has
coefficients in
{\mathbb{N}}[t]
. By Remark
3.1.7
, it
follows that, in fact, the expansion of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{l};\,t)
in terms of the basis of multi-symmetric
functions
m_{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}=m_{\lambda^{(1)}}(X_{1})m_{\lambda^{(2)}}(X_{2})\cdots m_{\lambda^{(l)}}(X_{l})
has coefficients
in
{\mathbb{N}}[t]
.

Example 6.4.12
.

We illustrate Corollary
6.4.10
and
Theorem
6.4.9
by computing flagged LLT
polynomials combinatorially and algebraically for two specializations:
one with a positive alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}
, and one with a signed alphabet.
Fix
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=((2)/(1),(2))
.

(i) For each
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
, we compute

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},x_{2};t]
using
Corollary
6.4.10
for the alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}^{b}_{+}
with flag bounds
(b_{1},b_{2})=(1,2)
. For each

T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}}^{b}_{+})
, we display the extended
tableaux
T^{\prime}
with
b_{i}
in the flag box with flag number
i
. We
indicate inversions with arrows, and write each term
t^{\inv(T)}\,x^{T}
in (
196
) below the corresponding
tableau
T^{\prime}
.

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},(2,1)}[x_{1},x_{2};t]&=&x_{1}^{3}&+&t\,x_{1}^{2}x_{2}&+&t^{2}\,x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\end{array}

For comparison, we now compute
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},x_{2};t^{-1}]
algebraically using Corollary
6.3.6
with

d=2
and
\alpha=(1,0)
. Note that
h({\boldsymbol{\nu}})=1
. For

\sigma=(1,2)
, we get

(199)

t\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},(1,2)}[x_{1},x_{2};t^{-1}]=\Pi_{t,x}(E_{21}(x;\,t^{-1}))=\Pi_{t,x}(x_{1}^{2}x_{2})=t\,x_{1}^{3}+x_{1}^{2}x_{2}\,,

where we used
E_{21}(x;\,t)=x_{1}^{2}x_{2}
and (
81
). For
\sigma=(2,1)
, noting that
\inv(\sigma)=1
, we get

(200)

t^{2}\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},(2,1)}[x_{1},x_{2};t^{-1}]=\Pi_{t,x}(T_{1}\,E_{21}(x;t^{-1}))=\Pi_{t,x}(x_{1}x_{2}^{2})=t^{2}\,x_{1}^{3}+t\,x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\,.

(ii) For
\sigma=(2,1)
, we compute
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},(2,1)}[x_{1},x_{2}-x_{\overline{1}}\,;t]
using
Theorem
6.4.9
with the signed alphabet

{\mathcal{A}}=\{1<\overline{1}<2\}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\{1\}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}=\{\overline{1},2\}
.

(201)
