).
∎

Lemma 7.3.3
.

The specialized integral form Macdonald polynomial
{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x,y,0^{k},z;\,q,t)
in
(
282
) is independent of permuting the parts of

\lambda
.

Proof.

Let
\mu=(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)
and let
i\in[r+n+1,r+n+k-1]
, so
s_{i}
acts on
\mu
by switching two parts of

\lambda
. By Proposition
7.1.6
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{s_{i}\mu}(x;q,t)-{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu}(x;q,t)
is Hecke antisymmetric,
hence divisible by
t\,x_{i}-x_{i+1}
(§
5.1
).
Therefore
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu}(x;q,t)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{s_{i}\mu}(x;q,t)

become equal upon setting
x_{i}=x_{i+1}=0
.
∎

Corollary 7.3.4
.

The function
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x;Y;z;\,q,t)
is
independent of permuting the parts of
\lambda
, and also of deleting
any zeroes in
\lambda
.

Proof.

Lemma
7.3.3
and
Proposition
7.3.2
give independence of permuting
the parts of
\lambda
. If there are
p
zeroes in
\lambda
, we
can assume that
\lambda=(0^{p};\lambda_{+})
. Then
{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x,y,0^{k},z;\,q,t)={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n+p};\,\lambda_{+};\,\kappa)}(x,y,0^{k-p},z;\,q,t)|_{y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{n+p}=0}
, so
Proposition
7.3.2
implies
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;\,q,t)=J_{\eta|\lambda_{+}|\kappa}(x,Y,z;\,q,t)
.
∎

In view of this corollary, we usually assume from now on that the
index
\lambda
for
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x;Y;z;\,q,t)
is
a partition, without trailing zeroes.

We now compare the above construction using specialization with the
one using symmetrization. In part, this is just for completeness,
since symmetrization appears often in other literature. But we also
need the comparison to see that
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
has leading term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)\,z^{\kappa}
,
and to determine the leading coefficient. In particular, this will
show that the functions
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)

do not vanish identically, and form a basis of
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)\otimes{\mathbbm{k}}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]
(recall that
{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
throughout §
7
).

Lemma 7.3.5
.

Given
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and
b>0
, the two
polynomials

(295)

\begin{gathered}P={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n},b;\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)|_{x_{r+n+1}=0}\,,\\
Q=\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,b,\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+1}=0}\,,\end{gathered}

symmetric in
x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{r+n}
, with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
, satisfy
P\equiv(1-t)\,Q\pmod{(t^{n+1})}
.
If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, we have the more precise identity

(296)

(1-q^{b}t^{l(v)+n+1})P=(1-t)Q,

where
l(v)
is the leg of the box
v
of content
-1
in row
s+1

(of length
b
) in
\dg(\eta;b;\kappa)
.

Proof.

To calculate
P
, we use (
265
) with
\mu=(\eta;\,0^{n},\,b,\,\kappa)
,
m=r+n+s+1
. Let
j=s+1
, so that row

j
of
\dg(\mu)
is the row of length
b
with flag bound
r+n+1
,
corresponding to
\mu_{r+n+1}=b
, and rows
j+1
through
j+n
have
length zero, corresponding to
(\mu_{r+1},\ldots,\mu_{r+n})=(0^{n})
. Setting
x_{r+n+1}=0
, we see that
P
is given by the
sum of the terms in (
265
) for non-attacking fillings

U\colon\dg(\mu)\rightarrow[m]
with no entry equal to
r+n+1
.

We can calculate
Q
similarly, using (
279
)
for
\widehat{\mu}=(\eta;\,b,\,0^{n};\,\kappa)
. Note that
\dg(\widehat{\mu})
and
\dg(\mu)
differ by exchanging rows
j
and
j+n
;
this induces a bijection
u\mapsto\widehat{u}
between boxes
u\in\dg(\mu)
,
\widehat{u}\in\dg(\widehat{\mu})
, and a bijection
U\mapsto\widehat{U}

between fillings
U\colon\dg(\mu)\rightarrow[m]
,
\widehat{U}\colon\dg(\widehat{\mu})\rightarrow[m]
. Let
v\in\dg(\mu)
be the box of
content
-1
in row
j
. The map
u\mapsto\widehat{u}
preserves arms,
legs, and attacking pairs, with only the following exceptions:

l(\widehat{v})=l(v)+n
, because
l(\widehat{v})
counts
n
extra hands in
the zero-length rows
j
through
j+n-1
, and
\widehat{v}
attacks the
flag boxes in these rows, which
v
does not attack.

Let
Q_{i}=T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,b,\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)|_{x_{r+n+1}=0}
. Then
Q_{i}
is given
by the sum in (
279
) over non-attacking
fillings
\widehat{U}:\dg(\widehat{\mu})\rightarrow[m]
with no entry equal to

r+n+1
, where
\widehat{U}^{\prime}
and
\coinv(\widehat{U})
are defined using flag
bound
r+i+1
in row
j+n
, and flag bounds
r+n+1,\ldots,r+1
,
omitting
r+i+1
, in rows
j
through
j+n-1
. The flag bounds in
other rows are the standard ones. Since
\widehat{v}
attacks a flag box
with entry
s
for every
s\in[r+1,m]\setminus\{r+i+1\}
, we either
have
\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})\leq r
or
\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})=r+i+1
. Let

Q_{i,1}
(resp.
Q_{i,2}
) be the partial sum over
\widehat{U}
such
that
\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})=r+i+1
(resp.
\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})\leq r
). In
particular,
Q_{n,1}=0
.

Similarly, since the box
v\in\dg(\mu)
attacks flag boxes with
entries
r+n+2
through
m
, every non-attacking filling
U\colon\dg(\mu)\rightarrow[m]
satisfies
U(v)\leq r+n+1
, hence those in the
sum for
P
satisfy
U(v)\leq r+n
. Let
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
) be
the partial sum over
U
such that
U(v)\in[r+1,r+n]
(resp.
U(v)\leq r
). Thus, we have
P=P_{1}+P_{2}
and
Q=Q_{1}+Q_{2}
,
where
Q_{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}Q_{i,1}
and
Q_{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}Q_{i,2}
. We will show that

(297)

(1-t)Q_{1}=(1-q^{b}t^{l(\widehat{v})+1})P_{1},\qquad(1-t)Q_{2}=(1-t^{n+1})P_{2},

and therefore

(298)

(1-t)\,Q=(1-q^{b}t^{l(\widehat{v})+1})P_{1}+(1-t^{n+1})P_{2}\,.

By Remark
7.2.4
,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
have coefficients in

{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
. Hence, since
l(\widehat{v})=l(v)+n
,
(
298
) implies
P\equiv(1-t)\,Q\pmod{(t^{n+1})}
.
If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, then, by the same argument as in the
proof of Proposition
7.3.2
, if
U\colon\dg(\mu)\rightarrow[m]
is non-attacking, then the box
v_{i}
of content

-1
in row
m+1-i
of
\dg(\mu)
has
U(v_{i})=i
for all

i=1,\ldots,r
. Since every
v_{i}
attacks
v
, this implies

U(v)>r
, hence
P_{2}=0
and
P_{1}=P
. In this case,
(
298
) implies (
296
).

For the first identity in (
297
), let
U\colon\dg(\mu)\rightarrow[m]
be non-attacking with
U(v)\in[r+1,r+n]
, so
U

indexes a term in the sum for
P_{1}
. Then
\widehat{U}
indexes a term
in the sum for
Q_{i,1}
for the unique
i\in[0,n-1]
such that

\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})=U(v)=r+i+1
. Since
Q_{n,1}=0
, this gives each
term in
Q_{1}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}Q_{i,1}
exactly once. The term

(299)

q^{\maj(U)}\,t^{\coinv(U)}\hskip-12.91663pt\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\dg(\mu)\\
U^{\prime}(u)=U^{\prime}(e(u))\end{subarray}}\hskip-12.91663pt(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})\hskip-12.91663pt\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\dg(\mu)\\
U^{\prime}(u)\not=U^{\prime}(e(u))\end{subarray}}\hskip-12.91663pt(1-t)\;x^{U}

for
U
in (
265
) differs from the similar term for

\widehat{U}
in (
279
) in three ways: (1) the term
for
\widehat{U}
contains an extra factor
t^{\ell(w)}=t^{i}
; (2) we
have
t^{\coinv(\widehat{U})}=t^{-i}\,t^{\coinv(U)}
, because
\inv(\widehat{U})
counts
i
attacking inversions between the entry
\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})=r+i+1
and flag boxes with entries in
[r+1,r+i]
which do
not correspond to attacking inversions of
U
; and (3) since

U^{\prime}(v)\not=U^{\prime}(e(v))=r+n-1
, but
\widehat{U}^{\prime}(\widehat{v})=\widehat{U}^{\prime}(e(\widehat{v}))=r+i-1
, the factor
(1-t)
for
u=v
in (
299
) is
replaced by a factor
(1-q^{b}t^{l(\widehat{v})+1})
for
\widehat{u}=\widehat{v}
in
the term for
\widehat{U}
. The extra factors
t^{i}
and
t^{-i}
cancel,
giving
(1-t)Q_{1}=(1-q^{b}t^{l(\widehat{v})+1})P_{1}
.

For the second identity in (
297
), if
U(v)\leq r
, so

U
indexes a term in the sum for
P_{2}
, then
\widehat{U}
indexes a
term in the sum for
Q_{i,2}
for all
i=0,\ldots,n
. In this case

\widehat{U}(\widehat{v})\leq r
does not form an attacking inversion with any
flag box in rows
j
through
j+n-1
, and the factors for
u=v
in
(
299
) and for
\widehat{u}=\widehat{v}
in the similar term for

\widehat{U}
are both
(1-t)
. The only difference is the extra factor

t^{i}
in the term for
\widehat{U}
. This shows that
Q_{2}=(1+t+\cdots+t^{n})P_{2}
, hence
(1-t)Q_{2}=(1-t^{n+1})P_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.6
.

Given
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and
\lambda\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{k}
, define polynomials

(300)

\begin{gathered}P={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{k},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s};q,t)\,,\\
Q=\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}.y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{k},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})},\end{gathered}

symmetric in
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
, with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
,
where
w
in the sum for
Q
ranges over the set of minimal coset
representatives
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+k+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+k+1,r+k+n]}
,
i.e.,
w
permutes indices
r+1
through
r+k+n
without reordering
the indices corresponding to
0^{n}
in
(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)
. Then

(301)

P\equiv(1-t)^{k}\,Q\pmod{(t^{n+1})}\,.

If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, we have the more precise identity

(302)

\prod_{i}(1-q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{l_{i}+n+1})P=(1-t)^{k}Q,

where
l_{i}
is the leg of the box of content
-1
in row
r+s+1-i

(of length
\lambda_{i}
) in
\dg(\eta;\lambda;\kappa)
.

Proof.

Renaming the variables, we can instead take
P
and
Q
to be the
result of setting
x_{r+n+1},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}=0
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)
and
\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)
,
respectively. Let
\lambda=(b,\lambda^{\prime})
with
b=\lambda_{1}
.
Lemma
7.3.5
gives
P^{\prime}\equiv(1-t)Q^{\prime}\pmod{(t^{n+1})}
, where

(303)

\begin{gathered}P^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)|_{x_{r+n+1}\mapsto 0}\,,\\
Q^{\prime}=\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,b,\,0^{n};\,\lambda^{\prime};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+1}\mapsto 0}.\end{gathered}

Since the operators
T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}
commute with setting

x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}=0
, we have

(304)

Q^{\prime}|_{x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}=\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}\,P^{\prime\prime}\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+1}\mapsto 0}\,,

where
P^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,b,\,0^{n};\,\lambda^{\prime};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)|_{x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}
. We can assume by
induction on
k
that
P^{\prime\prime}\equiv(1-t)^{k-1}Q^{\prime\prime}\pmod{(t^{n+1})}
,
where

(305)

Q^{\prime\prime}=\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}\,,

in which the sum is over
w
permuting indices
r+2
through
r+k+n

without reordering
r+k+1
through
r+k+n
.
The result now follows from

(306)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)|_{x_{r+n+1},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}

\displaystyle=P^{\prime}|_{x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}

\displaystyle\equiv(1-t)Q^{\prime}|_{x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}

\displaystyle\equiv(1-t)^{k}\bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{n}T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}\,Q^{\prime\prime}\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+1}\mapsto 0}

\displaystyle=(1-t)^{k}\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x_{r+n+1},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}\mapsto 0}=(1-t)^{k}Q\,,

where congruences are modulo
(t^{n+1})
, the last sum is over the
same
w
as in (
300
), and we have again used the fact that

T_{r+i}\cdots T_{r+1}
commutes with setting
x_{r+n+2},\ldots,x_{r+n+k}=0
. If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}>0)^{r}
, we get
(
302
) by using the more precise identity
(
296
) from Lemma
7.3.5
, with
equalities instead of congruences in (
306
).
∎

Given
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and a partition

\lambda
, Proposition
7.3.2
shows that
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
is the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
of the polynomials
P
in (
300
). By
Proposition
7.3.6
with
k=\ell(\lambda)
, this is
equal to
(1-t)^{\ell(\lambda)}
times the limit of the polynomials

Q
. By definition,
Q
is a scalar multiple of the symmetrization
in (
284
) with
y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)}
set to zero, that is, of

(307)

\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}}T_{w}\,E_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}.y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\lambda)},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})}\,

where
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
is the stabilizer of
(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)
in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}
. Before
setting
y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)}=0
, this had leading
term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(y)\,z^{\kappa}
with coefficient
1
.
For
n\geq\ell(\lambda)
, the same holds after setting

y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)}=0
.

We now calculate the scalar factor. To begin with, the sum in the
formula for
Q
is over a larger set of coset representatives
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+\ell(\lambda)+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}
, instead of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
. This has the effect of acting on the sum in
(
307
) with
\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}}T_{w}
, where

{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\cong\Stab_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{\ell(\lambda)}}(\lambda)
is
the stabilizer of
(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)
in

{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)]}
. Since this sum is already
symmetric, the redundant symmetrization introduces a factor

(308)

W_{\Stab(\lambda)}(t)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\sum\nolimits_{w\in\Stab_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{\ell(\lambda)}}(\lambda)}t^{l(w)}\,.

The other difference between (
307
) and the formula for
Q

is that the latter has
{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)
in place of
E_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)
. This introduces a factor
\Pi_{u}(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})

for
u\in\dg((\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa))
.

With the obvious identifications between boxes of
\dg((\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa))
for different values of
n
, the
factor
(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})
is independent of
u
, except for
u

of content
c(u)=-1
in the top
r+\ell(\lambda)
rows,
corresponding to the parts of
(\eta;\lambda)
. For these boxes,

a(u)
is independent of
n
, and
l(u)=l_{0}(u)+n
, where

l_{0}(u)
is the leg of
u
when
n=0
. The factor in this case is
therefore
(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l_{0}(u)+n+1})\equiv 1\pmod{t^{(n+1)}}
.

Propositions
7.3.2
and
7.3.6

thus imply the following formula.

Corollary 7.3.7
.

Given
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and a partition

\lambda
, define

(309)

\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)=(1-t)^{\ell(\lambda)}\,W_{\Stab(\lambda)}(t)\,\prod_{u\in S}(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})\,

where
S\subseteq\dg((\eta;\lambda;\kappa))
excludes boxes of
content
c(u)=-1
in the top
r+\ell(\lambda)
rows. Let

(310)

E_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl(\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\hskip-18.08331pt}T_{w}\,E_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\,\bigr)|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}.y_{1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})}

be the (
t
-adically convergent)
limit of the monically normalized symmetrizations in
(
284
). Then
E_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
has leading term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)\,z^{\kappa}

with coefficient
1
, and

(311)

J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)=\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)\,E_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)\,.

If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, then the limit converges strongly, so
that for all
n
we have

(312)

J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}[x,y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},z;q,t]\\
=\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)\,\bigl(\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\hskip-18.08331pt}T_{w}\,E_{(\eta;\lambda;0^{n};\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)\bigr|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\lambda)},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})}\,.

Proof.

As calculated above, the polynomials
P
in (
300
), which
converge
t
-adically to
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
,
are multiples of (
307
) by scalar factors that converge

t
-adically to
\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)
. Setting

y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{\ell(\lambda)}=0
in (
307
) makes no
difference in the limit. Hence (
310
) converges, and
(
311
) holds.

Here we only calculated the
t
-adic limit of the scalar factors,
eliminating
(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})
for
u\not\in S
because it
converges to
1
. If
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, however, we can
calculate the scalar factors exactly, using
(
288
) and (
302
).
In this case, we get
\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)
on the nose,
because the denominator factors in (
288
)
and the factors multiplying
P
in (
302
) are
the same as the factors
(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})
for
u\not\in S
.
∎

The normalizing factor
\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)
in
(
309
) can be recast in a form more closely
resembling the factor in Definition
7.1.2
. If

r_{i}
are the part multiplicities of
\lambda=(1^{r_{1}},2^{r_{2}},\ldots)
, then
\Stab(\lambda)={\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
, and the
first two factors in (
309
) are given by

(313)

(1-t)^{\ell(\lambda)}\,W_{\Stab(\lambda)}(t)\,=\,\prod_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{r_{i}}(1-t^{j})\;=\!\prod_{a_{\lambda}(u)=0}(1-t^{l^{\prime}(u)+1})\,,

where the last product is over boxes
u\in\dg(\lambda)
with arm

a_{\lambda}(u)=0
, and
l^{\prime}(u)
is the number of indices
i<j
such
that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
, where
u
is in the row
corresponding to
\lambda_{j}
.

Let
S_{\eta},S_{\lambda},S_{\kappa}\subseteq\dg((\eta;\lambda;\kappa))
be the sets of boxes in rows corresponding to
\eta
,

\lambda
,
\kappa
, respectively, and let
S^{\prime}_{\eta}\subseteq S_{\eta}
,
S^{\prime}_{\lambda}\subseteq S_{\lambda}
consist of boxes
u

with
c(u)\not=-1
, so we have
S=S^{\prime}_{\eta}\cup S^{\prime}_{\lambda}\cup S_{\kappa}
in (
309
). For
u\in S_{\lambda}
,
define the
reduced leg

l^{\prime}(u)
to be the leg of
u
in
\dg((\eta;\lambda;\kappa))
with hands of content
c(u)-1
in rows of

S_{\eta}
excluded, along with all hands of content
c(u)
.

For
v\in S^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, we have
e(v)\in S_{\lambda}
. Setting

u=e(v)
, we have
a(u)=a(v)+1
. Since
\lambda
is a partition,
there are no hands of content
c(u)-1=c(v)
in rows of
S_{\lambda}

above the row containing
u
and
v
, so the hands excluded in the
definition of
l^{\prime}(u)
are exactly those not included in
l(v)
. Hence

l^{\prime}(u)=l(v)
. For
u\in S_{\lambda}
with arm
a(u)=0
, i.e.,

u
not of the form
e(v)
,
l^{\prime}(u)
is the same as in
(
313
). Hence we have

(314)

\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\kappa}(q,t)=\prod_{u\in S^{\prime}_{\eta}\cup S_{\kappa}}(1-q^{a(u)+1}t^{l(u)+1})\times\prod_{u\in S_{\lambda}}(1-q^{a(u)}t^{l^{\prime}(u)+1})\,,

where
l^{\prime}(u)
denotes the reduced leg, as defined in the preceding
paragraph.

Corollary 7.3.8
.

The functions
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
for
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
,
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, and all partitions
\lambda
are a
basis of
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)\otimes{\mathbbm{k}}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]
.

Proof.

Immediate from
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)

having leading term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)\,z^{\kappa}
with
non-vanishing coefficient.
∎

Caution: although the functions
J_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(x,Y,z;q,t)
have coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
, they do not form a
basis of
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t][x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]}(Y)\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t][z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]
.

Remark 7.3.9
.

(i) In the case
\eta=\emptyset
, the partially symmetric monic and
integral form Macdonald polynomials
E_{\emptyset|\lambda|\kappa}(Y,z;q,t)
and
J_{\emptyset|\lambda|\kappa}(Y,z;q,t)
have no
x

variables, since
s=0
. Since the condition
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{s}
holds vacuously in this case, they are strongly convergent
limits of the monically normalized symmetrization

(315)

\bigl(\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+\ell(\lambda)}/\Stab((\lambda;0^{n}))\hskip-12.05554pt}T_{w}\,E_{(\lambda;\,0^{n};\,\kappa)}(x;q,t)\bigr)\bigr|_{x\rightarrow(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s})}

and the specialization

(316)

{\mathcal{E}}_{(0^{n};\,\lambda;\,\kappa)}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\lambda)},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s};\,q,t),

respectively. In particular, these partially symmetric polynomials
are
left stabilized
, with the symmetric variables
Y=y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
preceding the nonsymmetric variables

z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}
.

In the left stabilized case, Goodberry
[
16
,
17
]

introduced both the monic and integral forms, and proved that they are
strongly convergent and that the normalizing factor for the integral
form is the same as what we denote by
\Pi_{\emptyset|\lambda|\kappa}(q,t)
in (
309
) and (
314
).
Our derivation of the general formulas for
\Pi_{\eta|\lambda|\kappa}(q,t)
closely follows that of Goodberry for
\eta=\emptyset
.

Lapointe
[
29
]
studies the same left stabilized
polynomials as introduced by Goodberry. Knop
[
27
]
had
earlier studied the special case of left stabilized polynomials in
which both
\eta
and
\lambda
are empty, and shown that these
converge strongly (Knop reverses the indices and variables, so in his
work the polynomials look right stabilized).

(ii) In the next subsection, we examine
right stabilized
partially
symmetric Macdonald polynomials with
\kappa=\emptyset
. This case
was previously studied by Bechtloff Weising
[
4
]
,
who constructed the stable limits for a variant of
t
-adic
convergence, and related them to operators arising in the work of
Carlsson and Mellit
[
10
]
. Bechtloff Weising does not
construct integral forms. His stable limits differ from the monically
normalized ones

E_{\eta|\lambda|\emptyset}(x,Y;q,t)
in (
310
)
by the factor
(1-t)^{\ell(\lambda)}W_{\Stab(\lambda)}(t)
in (
309
) and
(
313
). He also gives a formula in the spirit of
Proposition
7.1.3
for his stable limits in the case

\lambda=\emptyset
and poses the problem of giving a similar
formula for general
(\eta|\lambda)
, a problem which is solved by
Proposition
7.4.2
.

7.4.
Right stable
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

Definition 7.4.1
.

The algebra of
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials
with coefficients in

{\mathbbm{k}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
is
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)
. For
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and
\lambda
a partition, we define
the
right stabilized integral form
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomial

(317)

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}J_{\eta|\lambda|\emptyset}(x,Y,z;q,t)\in{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y).

Note that
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)
has coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q,t]
, and that there are no variables
z_{i}
in the formula, since

\kappa=\emptyset
and
s=0
.

By Proposition
7.3.2
the definition is
equivalent to the specialization formula

(318)

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,0^{n};\,\lambda)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\lambda)};q,t)\,,

and by Corollary
7.3.7
, also to the symmetrization formula

(319)

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)\\
=\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\emptyset}(q,t)\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl(\sum_{w\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+\ell(\lambda)+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}\hskip-18.08331pt}T_{w}\,E_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t)\,\bigr)|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)})}\,,

where
\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\emptyset}(q,t)
is the product in
(
309
) and (
314
), and
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{s}}}
is the stabilizer in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+n+\ell(\lambda)]}
of

(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})
, so the symmetrization inside the limit
is monic with leading term
x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(y)
. Both
limits converge
t
-adically, and if
\eta\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{>0})^{r}
, they
converge strongly. To get strong convergence in the same form as in
(
312
), one can modify (
319
)
without changing the limit by setting
y_{n+1},\ldots,y_{n+\ell(\lambda)}=0
in symmetrization of
E_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}
.

In particular,
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)
has leading term

x^{\eta}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)
with coefficient
\Pi_{\eta,\lambda,\emptyset}(q,t)
. For
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and all partitions

\lambda
, these functions form a
{\mathbbm{k}}
-basis of
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)
.

By Definition
7.3.1
,
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)
is also given by a special case of formula
(
286
). Since
\kappa=\emptyset
, we can
improve upon (
286
), as follows.

Proposition 7.4.2
.

For
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and
\lambda
a partition, we have the
formula

(320)

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)=t^{n((\eta;\lambda)_{+})}\hskip-8.61108pt\sum_{({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)\in R^{\prime\prime}(\mu)}\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to14.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.14055pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{
}{
}{
}{
}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 9.84 0 M 0 9.84 L 9.84 9.84 M 0 19.68 L 9.84 19.68 M 0 0 L 0 19.69 M 9.84 0 L 9.84 19.69 M 9.84 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.19257pt}{9.16284pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)}\bigr){\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}^{-}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}),

where
R^{\prime\prime}(\mu)
is the same as
R(\mu)
in (
274
), with

\mu=(\eta;\lambda)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
and
m=r+\ell(\lambda)
, except
that
R^{\prime\prime}(\mu)
only contains pairs
({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)
in which all
of the augmented ribbons
\nu^{(i)}
are non-ragged-right, i.e., no
augmentation box is in a vertical domino.

Proof.

Setting
x_{r+i}=y_{i}
, the evaluation
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}^{-}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}]
is given by a sum over signed flagged tableaux
T
on

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
with flag bounds
r+n+\ell(\lambda),\ldots,r+n+1
in the
augmentation boxes in the first
\ell(\lambda)
components, and
entries in
[r+n]_{\pm}
, corresponding to the variables

x_{1},\ldots,x_{r+n}
which have not been set equal to zero.

Since all entries of
T
are less than the flag bounds in the first

\ell(\lambda)
components,
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}^{-}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}]=0
if the
augmentation box in any of these components is in a vertical domino.
Since this holds for all
n
, it follows that
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}^{-}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1})=0
unless all
of the first
\ell(\lambda)
components are non-ragged-right. This
suffices, since the sum in (
286
) is already
over
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
in which the last
r
components are
non-ragged-right.
∎

Note that in this proof, the restriction to
\kappa=\emptyset
and

s=0
is necessary, as otherwise the string of zeroes after
Y
in the
formula would be interrupted by variables
z_{i}
corresponding to
entries
a
with
|a|>r+n
in
T
.

Remark 7.4.3
.

By Corollary
7.3.4
,
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)
is also equal to
J_{\eta|\zeta|\emptyset}(x,Y,z;q,t)
for
any weak composition
\zeta
such that
\lambda=\zeta_{+}
, i.e.,
\zeta
is a permutation of the parts of
\lambda
and possible added
zeroes. The same argument then shows that (
320
)
holds with
\zeta
in place of
\lambda
on the right hand side.

7.5.
The modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}

We come now to the crucial innovation of this section: an operation

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
on
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials, mixing symmetric and
nonsymmetric plethysm, which we view as the right way to generalize
the symmetric plethysm
f(X)\mapsto f[X/(1-t)]
. We will use
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}

to define the plethystically modified stable

r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials that will become the subject of our
atom positivity conjectures in §
7.6
.

In the formulas for
J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)
as an element of

{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)
, the formal
alphabet
Y
stands for variables that follow the nonsymmetric
variables, so that an
r
-nonsymmetric polynomial is a function of the
full list of variables
X=x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
which is symmetric in all but the first
r
variables.
Every such function can also be expressed uniquely as a polynomial in
the
x_{i}
and symmetric function in
X
.

More precisely, we have two presentations

(321)

{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(X)\cong{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)

of the algebra of
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials, related as follows:

x_{i}
is the same on both sides, and symmetric functions in the
formal alphabet on either side are expressed in terms of the variables

x_{i}
and symmetric functions in the formal alphabet on the other
side by

(322)

\begin{gathered}f(X)=f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y],\quad g(Y)=g[X-(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r})].\end{gathered}

Definition 7.5.1
.

The
r
-nonsymmetric plethysm

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
is the
{\mathbbm{k}}
-linear operator on the algebra of
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials over

{\mathbbm{k}}
given in terms of the presentation
{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(X)
by
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}=\Pi_{t,x}\otimes(f(X)\mapsto f[X/(1-t)])
, that is, by

(323)

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\bigl(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\,h(X)\bigr)=(\Pi_{t,x}g(x))\,h[X/(1-t)].

Example 7.5.2
.

For
r=2
, we calculate
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{2}(x_{2}e_{1}(Y))={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{2}(x_{2}e_{1}(X)-x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}))=(\Pi_{t,x}\,x_{2})e_{1}[X/(1-t)]-\Pi_{t,x}(x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2})=(tx_{1}+x_{2})e_{1}(X)/(1-t)-((t+t^{2})x_{1}^{2}+(1+t)x_{1}x_{2}+x_{2}^{2})
. If desired, this can
be expressed in terms of
x
and
Y
again by substituting
e_{1}(X)=e_{1}(Y)+x_{1}+x_{2}
.

Theorem
4.7.3
implies that
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
is a stable limit
of nonsymmetric plethysms
\Pi_{t,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})}
acting on polynomials
symmetric in the
y
variables. The following lemma makes this
precise.

Lemma 7.5.3
.

Suppose that
\phi_{n}(x,y,z)\in{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]
are symmetric in
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
, have coefficients
with no pole at
t=0
,
and converge
t
-adically to

(324)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{n}(x,y,z)=\phi(x,Y,z)\in{\mathbbm{k}}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s}]\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathbbm{k}}}(Y)\,.

Then the nonsymmetric plethysms
(\Pi_{t,(x,y,z)}\,\phi_{n}(x,y,z))
, with coefficients expanded as formal power series in

t
, converge formally in
x
,
y
,
z
, and
t
to

(325)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pi_{t,(x,y,z)}\,\phi_{n}(x,y,z)={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\Pi_{t,z}\,\phi(x,Y,z)

(note that
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
, acting on
x,Y
, commutes with
\Pi_{t,z}
).
As a consequence, the following limits also converge formally in
x
,

Y
, and
t
:

(326)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl((\Pi_{t,(x,y,z)}\,\phi_{n}(x,y,z))|_{z=0}\bigr)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pi_{t,(x,y)}\,\phi_{n}(x,y,0)={\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,\phi(x,Y,0).

Proof.

The
t
-adic convergence in (
324
) means that for
any
e
, after reducing all coefficients (mod
(t^{e})
), we have
\phi_{n}(x,y,z)=\phi[x,y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},z]
for
n
sufficiently
large. We can assume by linearity that
\phi(x,Y,z)
has the
factored form
\phi(x,Y,z)=g(x)f[X]h(z)
in
Theorem
4.7.3
, where
X=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y
. Then
Theorem
4.7.3
implies that we have

(327)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}(\Pi_{t,(x,y,z)}\,\phi_{n}(x,y,z))=(\Pi_{t,x}g(x))f[X/(1-t)](\Pi_{t,z}h(z))\,,

converging formally in
x
,
Y
,
z
and
t
. This proves
(
325
).

We always have
(\Pi_{t,z}\,h(z))|_{z=0}=h(0)
, i.e., the operator

\Pi_{t,z}
commutes with setting
z=0
, because
\Pi_{t,z}
is
homogeneous of degree zero and
\Pi_{t,z}\,1=1
. Setting
z=0
in
(
325
) therefore gives the first limit in
(
326
). The second limit in
(
326
) follows from case of
(
325
) with no
z
variables, applied to
\phi_{n}(x,y,0)
and
\phi(x,Y,0)
.
∎

We now get a stable version of
Proposition
6.7.3
.

Lemma 7.5.4
.

For any flagged LLT data
{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma
of length at least
r
,
we have

(328)

{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1})={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}]\,.

Proof.

Specializing
Y=y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}
,
Proposition
6.7.3
gives

(329)

\Pi_{t,(x,y,z)}\,{\mathcal{G}}^{-}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s};\,t^{-1}]\\
={\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},z_{1},\ldots,z_{s};\,t^{-1}]\,.

By (
326
) in Lemma
7.5.3
, if we
set
z=0
in the expression on the left hand side, the result
converges formally as
n\rightarrow\infty
to the left hand side of
(
328
). After setting
z=0
, the right hand
side plainly converges strongly to the right hand side of
(
328
).
∎

It is necessary here that the variables set equal to zero in
(
328
) form an uninterrupted sequence
following
Y
. This is because the proof of
(
326
) uses the fact that
\Pi_{t,z}
commutes
with setting
z=0
, which would not hold if we only set a subset of
the
z
variables to zero.

Theorem 7.5.5
.

For
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and
\lambda
a partition, the
modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

(330)

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}\,J_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)

satisfy the following identities.

(a) The polynomial
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)
is the formally
convergent limit

(331)

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pi_{t,(x;y)}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;0^{n};\lambda)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\lambda)};\,q,t)

of nonsymmetric plethysms of specialized integral form Macdonald
polynomials.

(b) We have the following ‘unsigned’ version of
(
320
):

(332)

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;\,q,t)=t^{n((\eta;\lambda)_{+})}\hskip-12.91663pt\sum_{({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)\in R^{\prime\prime}((\eta;\lambda))}\hskip-4.30554pt\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to14.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.14055pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{
}{
}{
}{
}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 9.84 0 M 0 9.84 L 9.84 9.84 M 0 19.68 L 9.84 19.68 M 0 0 L 0 19.69 M 9.84 0 L 9.84 19.69 M 9.84 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)}\bigr)\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}],

where
R^{\prime\prime}((\eta;\lambda))
only contains pairs
({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)

in which all augmented ribbons
\nu^{(i)}
are non-ragged right, as in
Proposition
7.4.2
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from formula (
318
) and
Lemma
7.5.3
. Part (b) follows from
Proposition
7.4.2
and
Lemma
7.5.4
.
∎

Remark 7.5.6
.

It follows from Remark
7.4.3
that
formula (
332
) also holds with any weak composition

\zeta
such that
\zeta_{+}=\lambda
in place of
\lambda
on
the right hand side.

7.6.
Atom positivity conjecture for
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}

We now formulate our atom positivity
conjecture for the modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y;q,t)
, and show that it would follow from our
atom positivity conjecture for flagged LLT polynomials,
Conjecture
6.6.1
.

By Remark
2.11.1
(iv), if
f(x,Y)
is an

r
-nonsymmetric polynomial, then for any
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and

\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}
, the coefficient
\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\lambda;\,0^{n-s})}(x;y)\rangle\,f[x,y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}]
is
independent of
n
for
n\geq s
and also of permuting or adjoining
zeroes to
\lambda
. Taking
\lambda
to be a partition, the Weyl
symmetrizations in the
y
variables converge strongly to a
stable atom

(333)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{y}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\lambda;\,0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}(x;y),

and the stable atoms form a basis of the algebra of
r
-nonsymmetric
polynomials such that for any
f(x,Y)
, the coefficients satisfy

(334)

\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)\rangle\,f(x,Y)=\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\lambda;\,0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}(x;y)\rangle\,f[x,y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}]

for all
n\geq\ell(\lambda)
. For any
k
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)
is also given by the specialization

(335)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)={\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\lambda;\,0^{k})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0^{k+\ell(\lambda)-1}]

of the flagged symmetric function
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{k})}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r+\ell(\lambda)+k})=\xi^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{k})}(x)
from Proposition
3.2.6
. To
see this, observe first that since
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{k})}(x)
is a function of only the first
r+\ell(\lambda)
variables,

\xi^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{k})}(x)
is a function of
only the first
r+\ell(\lambda)
formal alphabets
X_{i}
, so the
right hand side of (
335
) doesn’t depend
on
k
. Now the identity follows because
Corollary
3.2.4
implies that for any
n\geq\ell(\lambda)
, we have

(336)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{y}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})={\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda;0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1}+\cdots y_{n},0^{n-1}]\,.

Corollary 7.6.1
.

For
\gamma,\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and partitions
\lambda
,
\mu
, let

(337)

K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)\mathrel{\overset{\text{{\rm def}}}{=}}\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)\rangle\,\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\mu}(x,Y;\,q,t)

denote the stable atom coefficients of the modified

r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials. Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, the atom coefficients

(338)

\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\lambda;\,0^{n-\ell(\lambda)})}(x,y)\rangle\,\Pi_{t,(x;y)}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;0^{n};\mu)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},0^{\ell(\mu)};\,q,t)

of the nonsymmetric plethysm of a specialized integral form
nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomial converge formally in
t
to

K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)
(more generally, the
coefficient of
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\,\nu;\,0^{n-\ell(\nu)})}(x,y)

converges to
K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)
for any
\nu

whose nonzero parts are a permutation of
\lambda
).

If Conjecture
6.6.1
is true, then we have stable atom positivity:
K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)\in{\mathbb{N}}[q,t]
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.7.3
, for any
e
, the
result of the nonsymmetric plethysm
in (
338
) is
symmetric (mod
(t^{e})
) in all but the last
p
of the variables
y_{i}
,
for
p
not depending on
n
. This given, the first part of the
corollary is Theorem
7.5.5
, part (a), stated in terms of
atom coefficients.

The positivity implication follows from Corollary
6.6.4

and Theorem
7.5.5
, part (b).
∎

For
\eta=(0^{r})
, if we set
y_{i}=x_{r+i}
, then
\mathsf{H}_{0^{r}|\mu}(x,Y;q,t)=\mathsf{H}_{\emptyset|\mu}(Y;q,t)
, independent of
r
, and

[
21
, Theorem 5.2.1]
implies that
\mathsf{H}_{\emptyset|\mu}(Y;q,t)
is the classical symmetric modified Macdonald polynomial

H_{\mu}(Y;q,t)=J_{\mu}[Y/(1-t);q,t]
. Hence, in this case, we
have

(339)

K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(0^{r}|\mu)}(q,t)=K_{(\gamma;\lambda)_{+},\mu}(q,t),

where
K_{\lambda,\mu}(q,t)
are the classical Kostka-Macdonald
coefficients. Thus, Conjecture
6.6.1
, via its
consequence in Corollary
7.6.1
, generalizes
the known positivity
K_{\lambda,\mu}(q,t)\in{\mathbb{N}}[q,t]
from

[
24
]
.

While stable atom positivity of
\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\mu}(x,Y;\,q,t)

depends on Conjecture
6.6.1
, the following
weaker result, which follows immediately from
Theorem
7.5.5
, part (b), and
Remark
6.4.11
, already provides some evidence to
suggest that our way of defining modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomials is the right one.

Corollary 7.6.2
.

The modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials are monomial-positive,
i.e., the coefficients
\langle x^{\gamma}\,m_{\lambda}(Y)\rangle\,\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\mu}(x,Y;\,q,t)
are in
{\mathbb{N}}[q,t]
.

Example 7.6.3
.

One can calculate

(340)

\mathsf{H}_{(1)|(1)}(x,Y;q,t)={\mathcal{A}}_{(1)|(1)}(x,Y)+t\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(2)|\emptyset}(x,Y)\,,

giving
K_{((1)|(1)),\,((1)|(1))}(q,t)=1
and
K_{((2)|(\emptyset)),\,((1)|(1))}(q,t)=t
. Approximating these by the non-stabilized
atom coefficients in (
338
), one finds

(341)

\Pi_{t,(x;y)}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{((1);0^{4};(1))}(x,y,0;\,q,t)\\
=(1-t^{3})(1-q\,t^{5}){\mathcal{A}}_{1,1,0^{3}}(x,y)+t(1-t^{3})(1-q\,t^{5})\,{\mathcal{A}}_{2,0^{4}}(x,y)+\cdots\,,

(342)

\Pi_{t,(x;y)}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{((1);0^{5};(1))}(x,y,0;\,q,t)\\
=(1-t^{4})(1-q\,t^{6}){\mathcal{A}}_{1,1,0^{4}}(x,y)+t(1-t^{4})(1-q\,t^{6})\,{\mathcal{A}}_{2,0^{5}}(x,y)+\cdots\,,

for
n=4
and
n=5
(we have omitted many other terms). The pattern
continues similarly for larger
n
. The behavior seen here is
typical: the coefficients in (
338
) almost always
have non-positive terms involving powers of
t
that grow with
n
.

Remark 7.6.4
.

We can make (
332
) more explicit by using Theorem

6.4.9
to express the flagged LLT
polynomials in terms of tableaux. We set
y_{i}=x_{r+i}
, so that as
a plethystic alphabet,
Y=x_{r+1}+x_{r+2}+\cdots
. Fix the
positive alphabet
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}^{+}=\{1<2<\cdots\}
, with

{\mathcal{A}}_{i}=\{i\}
for
i\leq r
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{r+1}=\{r+1,r+2,\dots\}
,
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}=\emptyset
for
i>r+1
. This corresponds to flag
bounds
b_{i}=i
for
i=1,\ldots,r
and effectively infinite flag
bounds
b_{i}>{\mathcal{A}}
for
i>r
. For this
{\mathcal{A}}
, the plethystic
alphabets in Theorem
6.4.9
are

X_{i}^{{\mathcal{A}}}=x_{i}
for
i\leq r
,
X_{r+1}^{{\mathcal{A}}}=Y
, and

X_{i}^{{\mathcal{A}}}=0
for
i>r+1
, giving

(343)

{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y,0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}]=\sum_{T\in\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})}t^{-\inv(T)}x^{T}\,.

Since all ribbons in
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
are non-ragged-right for
({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)\in R^{\prime\prime}((\eta;\lambda))
,
\FSST({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma,{\mathcal{A}})

consists of ordinary semistandard tableaux
T
on
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
, subject
only to the flag condition that for
i=1,\dots,r
, the box of
content
c(u)=-1
in the ribbon
\nu^{(m+1-i)}
of length

|\nu^{(m+1-i)}|=\eta_{i}
, with flag bound
b_{i}=i
in its
augmentation box, has entry
T(u)\leq i
. The flag condition implies
that
T
has no attacking inversions involving a flag box, hence
\inv(T)=\inv_{{\rm o}}(T)
(Definition
2.8.1
).

Example 7.6.5
.

We compute
\mathsf{H}_{(2)|(1)}(x,Y;q,t)
using (
332
) and
Remark
7.6.4
. The set
R^{\prime\prime}((2,1))
of
non-ragged-right augmented ribbon tuples is displayed below with flag
bound (
=
flag number)
1
written in the augmentation box of

\nu^{(2)}
(in general, the relevant flag boxes are the
r

augmentation boxes in the ribbons
\nu^{(m)},\dots,\nu^{(m+1-r)}
of
lengths
\eta
), and the monomial
t^{n((\eta;\lambda)_{+})}\prod_{\resizebox{327880}{}{\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to14.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.14055pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.19257pt}{9.16284pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)}
shown to the left of each
tuple.

Infinitely many flagged tableaux with entries in
{\mathcal{A}}=\{1<2<\cdots\}
contribute to
\mathsf{H}_{(2)|(1)}
, so for convenience in
displaying them below, we divide them into five groups determined by
the indicated inequalities, two on the first ribbon tuple above, and
three on the second.

t^{-\inv(T)}&&1&&t^{-1}&&t^{-1}&&t^{-1}&&t^{-2}\\[5.69054pt]
\mathsf{H}_{(2)|(1)}(x,Y;q,t)&=&t\,x_{1}^{3}&\hskip-4.30554pt+&x_{1}^{2}s_{1}(Y)&+&qt\,x_{1}^{2}s_{1}(Y)&+&qt\,x_{1}h_{2}(Y)&+&q\,x_{1}e_{2}(Y)\end{array}

It so happens that each term above is a stable atom (although
organizing the tableaux by inequalities would not usually have this
effect), yielding the stable atom expansion

(345)

\mathsf{H}_{(2)|(1)}(x,Y;\,q,t)=t\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(3)|\emptyset}+{\mathcal{A}}_{(2)|(1)}+qt\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(2)|(1)}+qt\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(1)|(2)}+q\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(1)|(1,1)}.

7.7.
Weyl and Hecke Symmetrization

We define a partial Weyl symmetrization operator
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y}
on

r
-nonsymmetric polynomials by

(346)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y}f(x,Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}}f[x,y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}],

where
x=x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
and
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}}
is the
Weyl symmetrization operator in the variables
x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.
The sequence on the right hand side converges strongly to a
well-defined limit, as can be verified by reducing to the case that

r=1
and
f(x_{1},Y)=x_{1}^{k}\,g[x_{1}+Y]
, where
g
is a
symmetric function. Similarly, we define the full Weyl symmetrization
operator
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
on
r
-nonsymmetric polynomials by

(347)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f(x,Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}}f[x,y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}],

where again we have strong convergence to the limit.

Lemma 7.7.1
.

For any flagged symmetric function
f(X_{1},\dots,X_{l})
,

(348)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y}f[x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y,0^{k}]

\displaystyle=f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r-1},x_{r}+Y,0^{k+1}],

(349)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f[x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y,0^{k}]

\displaystyle=f[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y,0^{r+k}],

where
k=l-r-1
.

Proof.

The first identity follows from combining (
346
), Corollary
3.2.4
, and the fact that

\lim_{n\to\infty}f[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}+y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n},0^{k+1}]

converges strongly to
f[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}+Y,0^{k+1}]
. The second
identity is proved similarly.
∎

For stable atoms, this implies the following analog of
(
42
) and Remark
2.11.1
(ii).

Lemma 7.7.2
.

For
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and partition
\lambda
,

(350)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)=\begin{cases}s_{(\gamma;\lambda)}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y]&\text{if $\gamma_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\gamma_{r}\geq\lambda_{1}$,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Hence, for any
r
-nonsymmetric polynomial
f(x,Y)
, the coefficient
of
s_{\mu}[x+Y]
in the Schur function expansion of
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}f(x,Y)

is equal to the coefficient of the stable atom
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{r})|(\mu_{r+1},\dots,\mu_{k})}(x,Y)
in the stable atom expansion
of
f(x,Y)
(if
k<r
, this means
{\mathcal{A}}_{(\mu;0^{r-k})|\emptyset}(x,Y)
).

Proof.

By (
335
) and (
349
),

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma|\lambda}(x,Y)={\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda)}[x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r}+Y,0,\ldots,0]
. For
Y=y_{1}+\cdots+y_{n}
,
this and the right hand side of (
350
) are both
equal to
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x;y}\,{\mathcal{A}}_{(\gamma;\lambda)}(x;y)
. Given
(
350
), the rest is clear.
∎

The next proposition involves both
r
-nonsymmetric and

(r-1)
-nonsymmetric polynomials. To distinguish them, we use the
respective notations
Y_{r}
,
Y_{r-1}
instead of
Y
. In
terms of an extended list of
x
variables,
Y_{r}=x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\ldots
, while
Y_{r-1}=x_{r},x_{r+1},\ldots
, i.e.,

Y_{r-1}
is
Y_{r}
together with
x_{r}
.

Proposition 7.7.3
.

Partial Weyl symmetrization acts in the following simple way on
modified
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald polynomials: for any
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and partition
\lambda
,

(351)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y_{r}}\,\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y_{r};q,t)=\mathsf{H}_{\widehat{\eta}\,|\,(\eta_{r};\lambda)_{+}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1},Y_{r-1};q,t)\,,

where
\widehat{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})
,

Y_{r}=x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\dots
, and
Y_{r-1}=x_{r},x_{r+1},\ldots
.
Iterating this, we also have

(352)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\,\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y_{r};q,t)=\mathsf{H}_{\emptyset|(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t)=H_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t).

where
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
.

Proof.

Applying
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y_{r}}
to both sides of (
332
)
and using (
348
) in each
term yields

(353)

{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{x_{r},Y_{r}}\,\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\lambda}(x,Y_{r};\,q,t)\\
=t^{n((\eta;\lambda)_{+})}\hskip-8.61108pt\sum_{({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma)\in R^{\prime\prime}(\eta;\lambda)}\bigl(\prod\nolimits_{\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to14.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.14055pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{
}{
}{
}{
}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 9.84 0 M 0 9.84 L 9.84 9.84 M 0 19.68 L 9.84 19.68 M 0 0 L 0 19.69 M 9.84 0 L 9.84 19.69 M 9.84 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.19257pt}{9.16284pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}q^{a(u)+1}\,t^{l(u)}\bigr)\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\sigma}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r-1},Y_{r-1},0,\ldots,0;\,t^{-1}].

The right hand side is the generalized version of formula
(
332
) in Remark
7.5.6
, for

\mathsf{H}_{\widehat{\eta}\,|\,(\eta_{r};\lambda)_{+}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1},Y_{r-1};q,t)
, with
\zeta=(\eta_{r};\lambda)
.
∎

Remark 7.7.4
.

We can now see that the conjectured stable atom positivity of

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\mu}(x,Y;q,t)
generalizes Macdonald positivity in a sense
stronger than in (
339
): namely, equation
(
352
) and Lemma
7.7.2
imply that

K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)=K_{(\gamma;\lambda),(\eta;\mu)_{+}}(q,t)
whenever
(\gamma;\lambda)
is a partition. Thus, not
only are the classical Kostka-Macdonald coefficients special cases of

K_{(\gamma|\lambda),(\eta|\mu)}(q,t)
when
\eta=(0^{r})
, but for
every pair
(\eta|\mu)
, the Kostka-Macdonald
coefficients
K_{\lambda,(\eta|\mu)_{+}}(q,t)
for all
\lambda

are special cases of
stable atom coefficients of

\mathsf{H}_{\eta|\mu}(x,Y;q,t)
.

Example 7.7.5
.

Applying the Weyl symmetrizer
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to
(
345
) we obtain

(354)

t\,s_{3}+s_{21}+qt\,s_{21}+0+q\,s_{111}=\mathsf{H}_{\emptyset|(2,1)}(X;q,t)=H_{21}(X;q,t),

illustrating Proposition
7.7.3
.

We now show that Proposition
7.7.3
has a counterpart
for the unmodified
r
-nonsymmetric integral forms
J_{\eta|\lambda}
, in which Hecke algebra symmetrization replaces Weyl
symmetrization.

Using the notation of (
163
), write
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\mathbf{r}}=(1/W_{\mathbf{r}}(t)){\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{r}}
for the Hecke symmetrization operator
rescaled so that
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}\,f(x)=f(x)
for any
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
-invariant
f(x)
. Also write
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n}}=\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{(1^{r};n-r)}
for this Hecke symmetrizer in the variables

x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Following
[
4
]
, we define a partial Hecke
symmetrization operator
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},Y}
on
r
-nonsymmetric
polynomials by

(355)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},Y}f(x,Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}}f[x,y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}],

It is shown in
[
4
, §4.3]
that the sequence on
the right hand side converges
t
-adically to a well-defined limit.
Similarly, we define the full Hecke symmetrization operator
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}
on

r
-nonsymmetric polynomials by

(356)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}f(x,Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{r},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}}f[x,y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}],

where again we have
t
-adic convergence to the limit.

The following result is similar to
[
4
, Lemma 4.28 and Proposition
5.1]
. In particular,
[
4
, Lemma
4.28]
shows that (
358
) below holds up
to a scalar factor. However, notably, the scalar factor goes away
when working with the integral forms.

Proposition 7.7.6
.

Partial Hecke symmetrization acts in the following simple way on
the right stabilized integral form
r
-nonsymmetric Macdonald
polynomials: for any
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}
and partition
\lambda
,

(357)

\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},Y_{r}}\,J_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},Y_{r};q,t)=J_{\widehat{\eta}\,|\,(\eta_{r},\lambda)_{+}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1},Y_{r-1};q,t)\,,

where
\widehat{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r-1})
,

Y_{r}=x_{r+1},x_{r+2},\dots
, and
Y_{r-1}=x_{r},x_{r+1},\ldots
.
Iterating this, we also have

(358)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}\,J_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y_{r};q,t)=J_{\emptyset|(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t)=J_{(\eta;\lambda)_{+}}(X;q,t),

where
X=x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
.

Proof.

By Propositions
7.3.2
and
7.3.6
with
\kappa=\emptyset
and
k=\ell(\lambda)
,

(359)

J_{\eta|\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r},Y_{r};q,t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(1-t)^{k}\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t)\bigr)|_{x\mapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r+n},0^{k})}\\
=\lim_{n\to\infty}(1-t)^{k}\sum\nolimits_{w}T_{w}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t),

where in both sums
w
ranges over the minimal coset representatives

{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+1,r+k+n]}/{\mathfrak{S}}_{[r+k+1,r+k+n]}
, and the second
equality follows from the definition of
t
-adic convergence using the
fact that the Hecke symmetrized quantity is symmetric. By a similar
argument to that giving (
308
), the quantity inside the
limit is the same as

(360)

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t)^{k}}{W_{(n)}(t)}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t).

Then applying
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}
, using that

\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r+1},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}=W_{(k+n)}(t)\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}
, we obtain

(361)

\displaystyle\frac{(1-t)^{k}W_{(k+n)}(t)}{W_{(n)}(t)}\,\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t)=\Big(\prod_{j=n+2}^{n+k+1}(1-t^{j})\Big)\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t).

By Proposition
7.1.6
,
\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\eta;\,\lambda;\,0^{n})}(x;q,t)=\widehat{{\mathcal{S}}}_{x_{r},\dots,x_{r+k+n}}{\mathcal{E}}_{(\widehat{\eta};\,(\eta_{r};\lambda;\,0^{n})_{+})}(x;q,t)
. The factors

(1-t^{j})
go to
1
in the
t
-adic limit. Hence, using (
359
) again, but now for the pair
\widehat{\eta}|(\eta_{r};\lambda)_{+}
, we see that
the limit of (
361
) is equal to

J_{\widehat{\eta}|(\eta_{r};\lambda)_{+}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r-1},Y_{r-1})
.

Iterating (
357
) gives the first equality in (
358
), and the second follows from the discussion before
(
339
) and the fact that
{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{r}
reduces to
the ordinary plethystic transformation
f[X]\mapsto f[X/(1-t)]
when

r=0
.
∎
